A Geometrical Analysis of Kernel Ridge Regression and its Applications
Georgios Gavrilopoulos, Guillaume Lecué, and Zong Shang email: georgios.gavrilopoulos@stat.math.ethz.ch , email: lecue@essec.edu , email: zong.shang@ensae.fr ETH Zurich, Seminar for Statistics, Rämistrasse 101, 8092 Zurich, Switzerland. ESSEC, business school, 3 avenue Bernard Hirsch, 95021 Cergy-Pontoise, France. CREST, ENSAE, Institut Polytechnique de Paris, 5, avenue Henry Le Chatelier 91120 Palaiseau, France

Abstract
We obtain upper bounds for the estimation error of Kernel Ridge Regression (KRR) for all non-negative regularization parameters, offering a geometric perspective on various phenomena in KRR. As applications: 1. We address the Multiple Descents problem, unifying the proofs of [ LRZ20 ] and [ GMMM21 ] for polynomial kernels in non-asymptotic regime and we establish Multiple Descents for the generalization error of KRR for polynomial kernel under sub-Gaussian design in asymptotic regimes. 2. In the non-asymptotic regime, we have established a one-sided isomorphic version of the Gaussian Equivalent Conjecture for sub-Gaussian design vectors. 3. We offer a novel perspective on the linearization of kernel matrices of non-linear kernel, extending it to the power regime for polynomial kernels. 4. Our theory is applicable to data-dependent kernels, providing a convenient and accurate tool for the feature learning regime in deep learning theory. 5. Our theory extends the results in [ TB23 ] under weak moment assumption. Our proof is based on three mathematical tools developed in this paper that can be of independent interest: 1. Dvoretzky-Milman theorem for ellipsoids under (very) weak moment assumptions. 2. Restricted Isomorphic Property in Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Spaces with embedding index conditions. 3. A concentration inequality for finite-degree polynomial kernel functions.



1
Introduction

We focus on regression problems in the context of kernel learning. Let
\lambda\geq 0
be a tuning parameter and
({\cal H},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\cal H})
be some Reproducing Kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) containing functions from the probability space
(\Omega,\mu)
to
{\mathbb{R}}
. Given
N
independent design vectors
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
distributed as the unknown probability measure
\mu
and associated responses
(Y_{i})_{i=1}^{N}\subset{\mathbb{R}}
, let the Kernel Ridge Regression (KRR) estimator be

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\lambda}\in\underset{f\in{\cal H}}{\argmin}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(f(X_{i})-Y_{i}\right)^{2}+\lambda\left\|f\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\right).

(1)

KRR is a highly effective and adaptable method utilized in various domains, including finance, biology, natural language processing, image analysis and partial differential equations,
[
STC04
,
RW05
,
SC08
,
SS16
]
. Additionally, it serves as a tool for developing mathematical foundations for deep neural networks,
[
JGH18
,
BMM18
]
. This paper presents general bounds for the estimation error of KRR, aiming to investigate various phenomena in KRR and provide insights in the field of statistical deep learning.

From supervised learning theory to deep learning theory

The fundamental goal of supervised learning theory is to approximate an unknown function, denoted as
f^{*}
(also referred to as the target function or signal), based on samples
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
, where
Y_{i}=f^{*}(X_{i})+\xi_{i}
, with
\xi_{i}
representing noise terms independent of
X_{i}
. Our task is to select a functions class
{\cal F}
and construct an estimator
\hat{f}\in{\cal F}
, such that, given the input of
N
samples
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
,
\hat{f}
fits
f^{*}
effectively. One metric for assessing the goodness of fit is the
L_{2}(\mu)
distance
\|\hat{f}-f^{*}\|_{L_{2}}
; it coincides with the excess risk of
\hat{f}
.

The excess risk of
\hat{f}
primarily depends on two factors: approximation error and estimation error,
[
Bac24
, Chapter 4]
. In a broad sense, the approximation error describes whether the best element in
{\cal F}
can fit
f^{*}
effectively, while the estimation error quantifies how far
\hat{f}
deviates from this oracle. To reduce the approximation error, one approach is to increase the complexity of
{\cal F}
, introducing high levels of non-linearity so that
{\cal F}
can describe sufficiently complex non-linear functions. Kernel methods and deep neural networks have both emerged as two approaches born from this line of thought to approach non-linear dependence between
Y
and
X
.

The Core Issues of Deep Learning Theory

Introducing non-linearity increases the estimation error because as the approximation error decreases,
{\cal F}
becomes rich enough to contain many global minimizers of the empirical risk (even interpolant estimators, that is, functions
f
such that
Y_{i}=f(X_{i})
for each
1\leq i\leq N
), leading in general to overfitting issues. This is known as the approximation-estimation trade-off
[
Wai19
, section 13.3]
. Classical statistical learning theory suggests that there must be a wisely-chosen regularization to learn from noisy data. As widely believed, an estimator should not interpolate the input data since it will suffer from interpolation of the noise in the data, which weakens its generalization properties. In
[
HTF09
]
, this phenomenon is called “over-parameterization”. The motivation behind the appearance of (
1
) is to introduce the regularization term
\|f\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
that induces some regularity in
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
and prevents it from overfitting noise, since overfitted estimators tend to be non-smoothed.

However, as shown by the empirical results in
[
ZBH+17
]
, “over-parameterization” may also lead to good statistical properties. In recent years, the statistical properties of interpolant estimators have attracted a lot of attention. In practical applications, deep neural networks, which possess high complexity and strong approximation capability
[
SH20
,
Suz18
,
Yar17
]
, are commonly trained as interpolant estimators (granting the global convergence of empirical risk), but with good generalization properties. Surprisingly, over-parameterized deep neural networks exhibit both a low approximation error and a low estimation error. Hence, the investigation into the phenomenon of overfitting estimators is often regarded as an essential task within the statistical theory of deep neural networks
[
BMR21
,
Bel21
]
.
A key component toward the construction of a statistical theory of deep learning is to analyze the implicit regularization exhibited by learning algorithms, specifically in terms of their selection of empirical risk minimizers. Additionally, researchers aim to investigate the statistical properties of these minimizers, and prove the the existence of benign overfitting,
[
BLLT20
]
.

KRR: A tool for Deep Learning Theory

Similar to deep neural networks, kernel methods exhibit a high degree of non-linearity while possessing characteristics that are relatively easier to study. This makes them a reference point for gaining insights into deep learning theory
[
BMM18
]
, as mentioned at the beginning of this paper. The connection between KRR and deep learning theory can be traced back to the concept of implicit regularization,
[
JGH18
]
.

Characterizing the implicit regularization of algorithms, such as gradient descent, stochastic gradient descent, and ADAM,
[
KB17
]
, for the purpose of training neural networks is a complex and challenging task,
[
JGH18
]
. The parameter trajectory of even a deep
linear
neural network during training exhibits a significantly non-linear behavior over time,
[
SMG14
,
CCFRF22
]
.
It is worth noting, however, that under specific initialization conditions, known as the static Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK) parameterization, the training path can be linearized. This observation is supported by the works of
[
JGH18
,
COB19
,
Yan20
]
. Under (static) NTK parameterization, it has been demonstrated (see for example
[
Boy22
]
), that a deep neural network trained using the first-order gradient method, converges towards a global minimizer of the empirical risk. Furthermore, this minimizer has the lowest possible RKHS norm (thus equals to
\hat{f}_{0}
in (
1
)), where the RKHS is generated by the neural tangent kernel, see for example
[
Boy22
]
. Through this avenue, kernel methods establish a connection with deep learning theory. However, there are numerous criticisms surrounding the concept of static NTK, for instance,
[
WGL+20
,
DWY21
,
BB21
]
.

Towards the feature learning regime

Many studies have already indicated that the estimation error of static NTK is significantly larger than that of deep neural networks. This is primarily due to the fact that static NTK does not exhibit alignment of its eigenfunctions with the unknown function (called lazy regime
[
COB19
]
). This issue is considered a significant drawback of static NTK. In fact, it is widely acknowledged that the strength of Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) lies in their powerful feature construction capability. In the context of kernel machines, this implies that the optimization algorithm for DNNs should automatically select a RKHS in such a way that the eigenfunctions of this RKHS align well with the unknown function, resulting in a smaller estimation error for the associated KRR (
1
). In addressing this issue, the authors in
[
BES+22
,
Lon21
,
RBPB22
,
DLS22
,
DKL+23
,
MLHD23
]
examine data-dependent kernels of neural networks within the context of the
feature learning
regime.

These data-dependent kernels have eigenfunctions that rely on the sample points, which means that to some extent, they can learn from data how to align with the unknown function. This implies that such data-dependent kernels possess the characteristics of feature learning while also being firmly grounded in the theory of RKHS, making them amenable to effective theoretical research. It is for these reasons that data-dependent kernels are highly promising. However, unlike the RKHS typically studied in the past, these data-dependent kernels are neither translation-invariant nor rotationally invariant. That is why obtaining more general results (regarding both kernels and stochastic assumptions) for KRR is expected to be a promising useful tool in statistical deep learning theory.
The general foundation of KRR must be general, not dependent on specific translation invariance or rotational invariance of RKHS, and not contingent on the properties of the RKHS spectrum, such as the power decay of eigenvalues. Furthermore, this bound should be user-friendly, allowing mathematicians studying training dynamics to use it conveniently. At the same time, this bound must be accurate enough to reflect the estimation error of KRR. Finally, the bound must be general enough to be manageable for the study of DNNs.

1.1
Our contributions

1.

The primary contribution of this paper is a general, precise and user-friendly bound for the estimation error of KRR.

To illustrate the precision and convenience of our general bounds, we applied it to various problems, including Multiple Descents, Gaussian equivalent property, the linearization of kernel matrices, and the estimation error of KRR of data-dependent conjugate kernels, resulting in numerous outcomes.

2.

In asymptotic scenario, we demonstrate the occurrence of the Multiple Descents phenomenon for the generalization error of KRR in RKHS with polynomial kernel, with the requirement of sub-Gaussian design, thus generalizes
[
GMMM21
]
. This is considered a well-known challenging problem. In the non-asymptotic case, we improve upon the conclusions of
[
LRZ20
]
.
It is worth noting that we present a
unified
proof for these two fundamentally different setups, which has been considered a challenging problem,
[
DWY21
]
.

3.

In
non-asymptotic
regime, we establish the Gaussian equivalent property for the
upper bound
of the estimation error of KRR in polynomial RKHS, with the stipulation of
sub-Gaussian
design or spherical uniform distribution.

4.

We obtain a geometric perspective on the linearization of kernel random matrices corresponding to polynomial kernels. Additionally, we have extended the proportional regime to the power regime (which will be explained later).

5.

In the specific case of linear ridge regression, we extend the results of
[
TB23
]
to a weaker set of moment assumptions.

6.

For smooth inner product kernels including NTK defined over a Euclidean sphere, we have derived upper bounds on the estimation error of KRR in the non-asymptotic regime. This upper bound is optimal in a minimax sense, which demonstrates that our general upper bounds are sharp compared to
[
MR22
]
.

7.

For data-dependent conjugate kernels, we have verified that the conditions outlined in our general bounds are satisfied. This underscores the potential of our general bounds as a tool to feed a mathematical deep learning theory. We provide the excess risk for the single-neuron model when feature learning occurs.

8.

Our general bounds hold significant geometric implications, and our proof relies on three novel mathematical tools we developed:

(a)

A Dvoretzky-Milman theorem for ellipsoids under (very) weak moment assumptions, see Theorem
2
.

(b)

Restricted Isomorphic Property for RKHS with embedding index condition, see Proposition
3
.

(c)

A concentration inequality for finite-degree polynomial kernel function of a subgaussian vector, see Theorem
10
.

We believe these new mathematical tools will be useful for addressing other statistical problems beyond KRR.

9.

We provide a general upper bound for the excess risk of KRR and sufficient conditions for benign overfitting to occur in the case of model misspecification, as discussed in Proposition
29
and subsequent discussions. This contributes to the examination of feature learning and generalization properties of deep neural networks. Specifically, our generalization bound takes the form of an oracle inequality, meaning that we can choose any oracle in our bound, rather than being required to select the projection of
f^{*}
onto the closure on
{\cal H}
as the oracle (for example,
[
Bac24
, Section 7.5.2]
), thus providing an interface between the theory of approximation error (see
[
DHP21
]
and references therein) and the theory of estimation error (see
[
BMR21
]
and references therein) for deep neural networks.

1.2
Literature review

Thus far, motivated by the theory of DNNs, research into KRR has uncovered some remarkable and intricate phenomena. Within this context, several predominant trends can be identified:

Benign Overfitting

The concept of benign overfitting refers to phenomena in which the estimation error of an interpolant estimator approaches zero as the number of samples and model parameters increase infinitely when the number of parameters exceeds the number of samples. In other words, this estimator demonstrates consistency. It has been demonstrated that in the context of linear regression, many minimum norm interpolant estimators are consistent
[
BLLT20
,
TB23
,
LS22
,
KZSS21
,
WDY22
,
DRSY22
]
, given certain assumptions regarding the relationship between the covariance structure of the design vector and the unknown signal.

Inconsistency

In contrast to the conclusions drawn about Benign Overfitting, there exist findings regarding inconsistency. These findings indicate that for certain RKHSs, including those generated by the (static) NTK defined over a sphere, the Laplace kernel and Sobolev kernel, the minimum RKHS norm interpolant estimator is inconsistent. In other words, its excess risk cannot be expected to approach zero as
N
increases (the dimension of design vectors is fixed). Several results have reported this phenomenon, including
[
RZ19
,
DWY21
,
Buc22
,
HHvLS23
,
LZL23
]
. The significance of such conclusions lies in demonstrating that for these RKHS, benign overfitting is not viable for relatively general unknown functions.

Multiple Descents phenomenon

Starting from
[
MM20
,
HMRT22
]
, the double descents phenomenon of the estimation error of the minimum RKHS norm interpolant estimators
\hat{f}_{0}
has garnered significant interest. In broad terms, the phenomena being discussed pertains to the observation that in the over-parameterized regime, the estimation error of the minimum RKHS norm interpolant estimator diminishes, despite the fact that it over-fits the training data.
Subsequently,
[
GMMM21
,
MMM22
]
demonstrated the occurrence of the Multiple Descents phenomenon in asymptotic regime. This phenomenon pertains to the existence of Multiple Descents of the estimation error of the minimum RKHS norm interpolant estimator (including static NTKs). Notably, this descent is observed when
N\sim d^{\iota}
for some
\iota\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
and
N,d\to\infty
(called the power regime). The significance of the Multiple Descents conclusion is to illustrate that as
N
increases (with
d
also increasing, but
N
being a power of
d
), the estimation error of KRR is not monotonic. For each
\iota
, KRR learns a degree-
\iota
polynomial approximation of the target function. Therefore, if the target function is a degree-
\iota
polynomial or can be well approximated by such a polynomial, over-fitting of the minimum RKHS norm interpolant estimator will be benign.

Linearization

In the process of proving double descents, many studies make use of a tool known as the “linearization of kernel matrices” developed in the studies conducted, for instance, by
[
EK10
,
DV13
,
CS13
,
FM19
]
. In broad terms, this category of tools establishes the following fact: when
d
and
N
both tend to infinity, and their ratio remains at a constant level (referred to as the “proportional regime”), the spectrum of the kernel matrices defined by inner product kernels and translation-invariant kernels can be approximated by the kernel matrix of a linearized kernel. Since the properties of KRR depend heavily on the kernel matrix, in a sense, in the proportional regime, KRR degenerates into a form of linear regression. This means that even with highly nonlinear kernel functions, in this scenario, KRR can only learn a linear approximation of the target function. Consequently, KRR does not offer an advantage over linear regression in this context. This observation has also been made in the context of double descents
[
MM20
,
HMRT22
]
.

Gaussian Equivalence

The Gaussian Equivalent Conjecture is another novel phenomenon that has been frequently observed recently
[
GMKZ20
,
AKLZ20
,
SLTC20
,
DL20
]
. It posits that in the analysis of KRR, we can replace the feature map with a centered Gaussian random vector and replace the integral operator of the RKHS with the covariance matrix of this Gaussian random vector. The excess risk obtained from the analysis of this linear model is nearly identical to the original excess risk of KRR. From this perspective, the Gaussian Equivalent Conjecture is somewhat similar to a variant of the central limit theorem but in a non-asymptotic way. The introduction of this phenomenon is meant to facilitate the study of KRR’s excess risk. This, from another angle, underscores the main theme of this paper, namely that the universality theory of KRR’s excess risk is eagerly anticipated in the field of Deep Learning theory.

Further questions

1.

In
[
GMMM21
,
MMM22
]
, Multiple Descents is proven only when both
d
and
N
tend to infinity. It is crucial to note that when
d
varies, it leads to changes in the sample distribution, RKHS, and
f^{*}
, making it challenging to determine whether KRR in this power regime or proportional regime still approximates the initial target function we sought to approximate.
Therefore, the question arises:
Can we prove Multiple Descents in the classical context with
d
fixed?
Additionally,
[
GMMM21
,
MMM22
]
only provide proofs for the case when the design vector
X
follows a uniform distribution over the Euclidean sphere, which is highly limiting. Hence, a natural question is:
is it possible to establish Multiple descents for a fixed target function under more general stochastic assumptions?

This problem is widely recognized as highly challenging,
[
HHvLS23
,
DWY21
]
.

2.

The same question arises as well for the Gaussian Equivalent Property:
Does the Gaussian Equivalent Property hold true in non-asympotic? for distributions other than the Gaussian or uniform over the Euclidean sphere distributions? other than the proportional regime?

1.3
Structure of the paper

Starting from this section, we focus on specific statistical problems with their rigorous mathematical derivations. In Section
1.4
, we introduce various basic mathematical notations required for this paper. In Section
2.1
, we provide fundamental knowledge about RKHS. Due to the length of this paper, we provide an informal version of the main conclusions in Section
2.1
. Since the main proofs in this paper are built upon the decomposition of RKHS, we also introduce some relevant notations in this section. In Section
2.2
and Section
2.3
, we introduce the two primary mathematical tools used in this paper: a Dvoretzky-Milman theorem (with its proof in Section
6.3
in supplementary material) and a Restricted Isomorphy Property (with its proof in Section
6.5.2
in supplementary material).

In Section
3
, we present the upper bounds (with proofs in Sections
6.1
and
6.2
in supplementary material) for the estimation error of KRR.
In Section
4.1
, we apply these tools to the study of the Multiple Descents phenomenon. In Section
4.2
, we apply them to the study of smooth kernel functions (not necessarily finite-degree polynomial kernels), where we show that even for smooth kernels, the upper bound for KRR is nearly identical to that of linear ridge regression. This leads to the content of the next section, Section
4.3
, where we demonstrate that the upper side of the Gaussian Equivalence Property holds true in the context of finite-degree polynomial kernels. In Section
4.4
, we apply our results to the data-dependent conjugate kernel, obtaining an upper bound on the estimation error of the KRR defined by it.

In Section
5.2
in supplementary material, we will list several potential research directions. For the sake of adhering to the length constraint, we place the content of non-linear random matrix linearization in Section
5.1
in supplementary material. Readers focused solely on statistical questions can skip this section. The proofs for other theorems, propositions, lemmas, etc., can be found in Section
6.5
in supplementary material.

1.4
Notations

We use
c,c_{0},c_{1},\cdots,C,C_{0},C_{1},\cdots
to denote absolute constants. Usually,
C
stands for large but finite constants and
c
stands for small but non-zero constants. Such constants are always assumed to be positive, but may change from one instance to another. Given two quantities
A,B
, we write
A\lesssim B
(or
A\gtrsim B
) if there exists an absolute constant
C
such that
A\leq CB
(or
A\geq CB
). If a constant is assumed to depend on some parameter (say
K
), we use expression
C_{K}
, and write
A\lesssim_{K}B
(or
A\gtrsim_{K}B
) if there exists an absolute constant
C_{K}
such that
A\leq C_{K}B
(or
A\geq C_{K}B
). We write
A\sim B
if
B\lesssim A\lesssim B
. We use
O_{d}(\cdot)
(respectively
o_{d}(\cdot)
) for big-O (respectively small-O) notations, where
d
emphasizes the asymptotic variable. Further, if
g(d)=O_{d}(f(d))
, we write
f=\Omega_{d}(g)
and if
g(d)=o_{d}(f(d))
, we write
f=\omega_{d}(g)
. Given a sequence of random variables
(Z_{d})_{d}
and deterministic sequences
(s_{d})_{d}
, we say
Z_{d}=o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(s_{d})
if
Z_{d}/s_{d}\to 0
in probability.

Given
r\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, we let
[r]:=\{1,2,\cdots,r\}
. Let
(\Omega,\mu)
be a probability space and, for
q\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, let
L_{q}(\Omega,\mu)
be the
L_{q}
space with norm
\left\|f\right\|_{L_{q}}=\left(\int_{\Omega}\left|f(x)\right|^{q}\right)^{1/q}d\mu(x)
. When there is no ambiguity, we abbreviate
L_{q}(\Omega,\mu)
as
L_{q}(\mu)
or
L_{q}
. Given some random variables
X_{1}
, we write
{\mathbb{E}}_{X_{1}}
for the conditional expectation with respect to
X_{1}
conditionally on all other random variables. Given probability measures
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
, we denote by
\mu_{1}\times\mu_{2}
the product probability measure. We say a real-valued random variable
X
is sub-Gaussian, if
\left\|X\right\|_{\psi_{2}}:=\inf\left(t>0:{\mathbb{E}}\exp\left(X^{2}/t^{2}\right)\leq 2\right)<\infty
. We write
{\cal N}(0,1)
as the standard Gaussian random variable, and
{\cal N}(0,I_{d})
be the standard Gaussian random vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Given two Hilbert spaces
{\cal H}_{1},{\cal H}_{2}
, we characterize
{\cal H}_{1}\otimes{\cal H}_{2}
by defining
f\otimes g\in{\cal H}_{1}\otimes{\cal H}_{2}
as the mapping
(f\otimes g):h\in{\cal H}_{2}\mapsto\left\langle g,h\right\rangle_{{\cal H}_{2}}f\in{\cal H}_{1}
. We use angle brackets
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{{\cal H}}
to denote an inner product in some Hilbert space
{\cal H}
, and omit it when
{\cal H}
is a Euclidean space. We use
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\cal H}
to denote its Hilbert norm, and denote the Euclidean norm by
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
. Given a bounded linear operator
T:{\cal H}_{1}\to{\cal H}_{2}
, we denote
\left\|T\right\|_{\text{op},{\cal H}_{1}\to{\cal H}_{2}}
as the operator norm of
T
, that is,

\left\|T\right\|_{\text{op},{\cal H}_{1}\to{\cal H}_{2}}=\sup\left(\left\|Tx\right\|_{{\cal H}_{2}}:\left\|x\right\|_{{\cal H}_{1}}\leq 1\right).

When there is no ambiguity, we abbreviate the operator norm as
\left\|T\right\|_{\text{op}}
. For any ONB
(\varphi_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of
{\cal H}_{1}
, if
\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left\|T\varphi_{j}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{2}}^{2}<\infty
, we say
T
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator from
{\cal H}_{1}
to
{\cal H}_{2}
, and denote

\left\|T\right\|_{HS,{\cal H}_{1}\to{\cal H}_{2}}=\sqrt{\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\left\|T\varphi_{j}\right\|_{{\cal H}_{2}}^{2}}.

One can prove that the HS norm of
T
is independent of the choice of ONB, for instance,
[
Pis89
, pp.7]
. When there is no ambiguity, we abbreviate the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of
T
as
\left\|T\right\|_{HS}
. For any
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
\mathrm{He}_{j}(x)
be the
j
-th (probabilist) Hermite polynomial,
[
Pis89
, pp.16]
. For a Hilbert space
({\cal H},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\cal H})
, we let
B_{\cal H}=\{f\in{\cal H}:\,\left\|f\right\|_{\cal H}\leq 1\}
and
S_{\cal H}=\{f\in{\cal H}:\,\left\|f\right\|_{\cal H}=1\}
.

Let
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
for some
m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, let
\mathrm{spec}(A)=(\sigma_{1}(A),\cdots,\sigma_{m\wedge n}(A))
and
(\sigma_{i}(A))_{i\leq m\wedge n}
are singular values of
A
and
\mathbbm{1}_{N}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
is the vector with all its coordinates equal to
1
. Given
N\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, we use
(e_{i})_{i\in[N]}
to denote an arbitrary ONB of
\ell_{2}^{N}
. Denote by
{\cal I}
, a
d
-index, that is, given
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we denote
{\cal I}
as a partition of
[i]={\cal I}_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup{\cal I}_{d}
into
d
(potentially empty) groups. With a little abuse of notation, we denote
\left|{\cal I}\right|:=i
and the cardinality of an empty set is
0
.

Notations on Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space will be introduced in Section
2.1
.

2
Tools

Our analysis of KRR relies on several tools that are all exposed in this section. We start with classical tools and notation that are related to RKHS.

2.1
Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Spaces

In this section, essential RKHS background knowledge is presented. For readers who are not familiar with RKHS, they can consider the upcoming discussion of
\phi(X)
as if it were a Gaussian random vector (which is precisely the essence of the Gaussian Equivalent Property).

Structural aspect

Let
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be a compact Hausdorff space
1
1

1

We emphasize that we do not actually require
\Omega
to be compact; it suffices for
K
to have a spectral decomposition.
,
\mu
be a probability measure on
\Omega
. Let
L_{2}(\Omega,\mu)
be the space of real-valued, square-integrable functions with respect to
\mu
. Suppose
K:\Omega\times\Omega\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a positive definite continuous function, and without loss of generality, we assume that
\left\|K\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1
2
2

2

We remark that we do not really need
\left\|K\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1
, but need only the integral operator
\Gamma
defined below to be a positive, compact, symmetric trace-class operator.
. In Section
4.4
, we will present a crucial example of a RKHS that does not satisfy
\left\|K\right\|_{L_{\infty}(\mu\times\mu)}<\infty
. Nevertheless, our analysis remains valid.
We say a Hilbert space
{\cal H}\subset L_{2}(\mu)
of functions is an RKHS (with RKHS inner product
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\cal H}
and associated RKHS norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\cal H}
) over
\Omega
if for every
x\in\Omega
, there exists a constant
C_{x}>0
(depending on
x
), such that
\left|f(x)\right|\leq C_{x}\left\|f\right\|_{\cal H}
for every
f\in{\cal H}
, that is, the evaluation functional
\mathrm{ev}_{x}:f\mapsto f(x),\,\mathrm{ev}_{x}:{\cal H}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a bounded linear functional. By Riesz’s representation theorem for Hilbert space, this is equivalent to saying that the inner product of
{\cal H}
can be characterized as follows: for every
x\in\Omega
, there exists
K(x,\cdot)\in{\cal H}
such that
\left\langle f,K(x,\cdot)\right\rangle_{\cal H}=f(x)=\left\langle f,\phi(x)\right\rangle_{\cal H}
, called the
Reproducing Property
. Given a kernel
K
, the (canonical) feature map is defined as follows:
\phi:x\mapsto K(x,\cdot)\in{\cal H}
. The RKHS can be considered as a linear model on
{\cal H}
, where the design vector
X\in\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is embedded into
{\cal H}
via the feature map
\phi
; hence
\phi(X)
plays the role of a design vector and may therefore be called the RKHS design vector.

By mapping
X
from
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
to
{\cal H}
, we clarify the prediction function, covariance structure and the design matrix.
Given that
\phi(X)
now serves as the design vector, its integral operator
\Gamma={\mathbb{E}}\left[\phi(X)\otimes\phi(X)\right]
(i.e.
\Gamma:f\in{\cal H}\to{\mathbb{E}}\left[\phi(X)\left\langle\phi(X),f\right\rangle_{\cal H}\right]\in{\cal H}
) will play the role of the covariance matrix. We denote the operator norm of
\Gamma
from
{\cal H}
to
{\cal H}
as
\left\|\Gamma\right\|_{\text{op}}
. Since
\left\|K\right\|_{\infty}\leq 1
,
\Gamma
is compact and positive semi-definite, so it has a discrete spectrum of non-negative eigenvalues. By Mercer’s theorem, see, for instance
[
Wai19
, Theorem 12.20]
or
[
RW05
, section 4.3]
, for any
{\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y}\in\Omega
,
K({\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y})=\left\langle\phi({\boldsymbol{x}}),\phi(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\cal H}=\sum_{j\geq 1}\varphi_{j}({\boldsymbol{x}})\varphi_{j}(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{j\geq 1}\sigma_{j}f_{j}({\boldsymbol{x}})f_{j}(\mathbf{y})
where
(\varphi_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}}
are eigenfunctions of
\Gamma
, and
(\sigma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
are the eigenvalues of
\Gamma
associated to
(\varphi_{j})_{j\geq 1}
, and where
\varphi_{j}=\sqrt{\sigma_{j}}f_{j}
. It is possible to check that this decomposition is unique. By
[
Lax02
, section 30.5]
,
\Gamma
is a trace-class operator, that is,
\Tr\left(\Gamma\right)<\infty
where
\Tr\left(\Gamma\right)={\mathbb{E}}\left\|\phi(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}={\mathbb{E}}K(X,X)=\sum_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}}\sigma_{j}
.

It is also widely-used to embed
{\cal H}
into
\ell_{2}
by
\phi:{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\Omega\mapsto\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\sigma_{j}}f_{j}({\boldsymbol{x}}){\boldsymbol{e}}_{j}
(we use the same notation as for the feature map
\phi:x\to K(x,\cdot)
introduced above; however, which definition of
\phi(x)
we used is clear from the context). Therefore for every
{\boldsymbol{v}}\in\ell_{2}
, the corresponding element of
{\cal H}
is
f_{\boldsymbol{v}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=\left\langle\phi({\boldsymbol{x}}),{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\rangle_{\ell_{2}}
. Therefore,
{\cal H}
can be defined equivalently as the image of
\ell_{2}
under the map
{\boldsymbol{v}}\mapsto f_{\boldsymbol{v}}
, with the inner product
\left\langle f_{\boldsymbol{v}},f_{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\rangle_{\cal H}=\left\langle{\boldsymbol{v}},{\boldsymbol{u}}\right\rangle_{\ell_{2}}
when
\sigma_{j}>0
for all
j
(in case
\Gamma
is of rank
r
,
{\cal H}
is in a one-to-one correspondence with
\ell_{2}^{r}
).

We will frequently use a decomposition of
{\cal H}
so it is necessary to introduce the following notation.
Denote
\Gamma_{p:q}=\sum_{p\leq j\leq q}\sigma_{j}\varphi_{j}\otimes\varphi_{j}
. Given
k\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
and
p<q\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}
, we decompose
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1:k}+\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}
. We let
{\cal H}_{p:q}=\Span\left(\varphi_{j}:p\leq j\leq q\right)
and
P_{p:q}
be the orthogonal projection onto
{\cal H}_{p:q}
, and for any
f\in{\cal H}
, denote
P_{p:q}f
as
f_{p:q}
, for example,
\phi_{p:q}(X)=\sum_{p\leq j\leq q}\left\langle\phi(X),\varphi_{j}\right\rangle_{\cal H}\varphi_{j}
. Consequently, we decompose
{\cal H}={\cal H}_{1:k}\oplus^{\perp}{\cal H}_{k+1:\infty}
. Given
\iota\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, we denote
f_{\leq\iota}^{*}:=f_{1:\sum_{l\leq\iota}d^{l}}^{*}
,
f_{>\iota}^{*}:=f^{*}-f_{\leq\iota}^{*}
,
\Gamma_{\leq\iota}:=\Gamma_{1:\sum_{l\leq\iota}d^{l}}
and
\Gamma_{>\iota}:=\Gamma-\Gamma_{\leq\iota}
,
P_{\leq l}
the projection onto the eigen-space of
\Gamma_{\leq l}
, and we denote by
P_{>l}
its complementary.

By reproducing property, for any
f\in{\cal H}
,

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}={\mathbb{E}}\left\langle\phi(X),f\right\rangle_{\cal H}^{2}=\left\|\Gamma^{1/2}f\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\leq\left\|\Gamma\right\|_{\text{op}}\left\|f\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}.

(2)

Define the RKHS design matrix
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi}:{\cal H}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi}=\left(\begin{matrix}\phi(X_{1})^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\phi(X_{N})^{\top}\end{matrix}\right),\mbox{ so that }{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi}f=\left(\begin{matrix}\left\langle\phi(X_{1}),f\right\rangle_{\cal H}\\
\vdots\\
\left\langle\phi(X_{N}),f\right\rangle_{\cal H}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}f(X_{1})\\
\vdots\\
f(X_{N})\end{matrix}\right),

where for all
x\in\Omega
, we use
\phi^{\top}(x):{\cal H}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, that is, the operator
\phi^{\top}(x):f\mapsto\left<\phi(x),f\right>_{\cal H}=f(x)
.

Statistical model and closed-form solution to (
1
)

In this paper, we always assume
f^{*}\in{\cal H}
(except in Remark
1
). Let
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=(\xi_{i})_{i\in[N]}
be the noise vector with i.i.d. zero mean and variance
\sigma_{\xi}^{2}
coordinates that are independent with design vectors
(X_{i})_{i\in[N]}
. Now the kernel ridge estimator
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
for
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i\in[N]}\subset\left(\Omega\times{\mathbb{R}}\right)^{N}
and the tuning parameter
\lambda\geq 0
is defined as

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\lambda}\in\underset{f\in{\cal H}}{\mathrm{argmin}}\left(\left\|{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi}f-\mathbf{y}\right\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\left\|f\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\right),\mbox{ where }Y_{i}=f^{*}(X_{i})+\xi_{i},\mbox{ and }\mathbf{y}=(Y_{1},\cdots,Y_{N})^{\top},

which coincides with (
1
). By
[
Wai19
, Proposition 12.33]
,
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
has an explicit solution:

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{\lambda}={\mathbb{X}}_{\phi}^{\top}\left({\mathbb{X}}_{\phi}{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi}^{\top}+\lambda I_{N}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{y}.

As
(\phi(X_{i}))_{i\in[N]}\subset{\cal H}
, we write
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,p:q}=\left((P_{p:q}\phi(X_{1}))|\cdots|(P_{p:q}\phi(X_{N}))\right)^{\top}
thus we can decompose
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi}
into two parts for any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi}=\left(\begin{matrix}(P_{1:k}\phi(X_{1}))^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
(P_{1:k}\phi(X_{N}))^{\top}\end{matrix}\right)+\left(\begin{matrix}(P_{k+1:\infty}\phi(X_{1}))^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
(P_{k+1:\infty}\phi(X_{N}))^{\top}\end{matrix}\right)=:{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,1:k}+{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}.

We recall that for all
f\in{\cal H}
,
f^{\top}:g\in{\cal H}\mapsto\left<f,g\right>_{\cal H}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, that is,
f^{\top}g=\left<f,g\right>_{\cal H}
.

Key quantities driving the rate of convergence of KRR

We first define some key quantities related to the convergence rate of KRR, and then provide an informal version of the main conclusions of this paper. For the formal version, please refer to Theorem
3
in Section 3 and Theorem
6
in the Supplementary material. Let
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, and let
\kappa_{DM}
be some absolute constant, define

\displaystyle J_{1}:=\left\{j\in[k]:\sigma_{j}\geq\frac{\kappa_{DM}\left(4\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)\right)}{N}\right\},\,J_{2}=[k]\backslash J_{1},

(3)

and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\tilde{\Gamma}_{1,\mathrm{thre}}^{-1/2}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\left(\sigma_{j}\vee\frac{\kappa_{DM}\left(4\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)\right)}{N}\right)^{-1/2}\varphi_{j}\otimes\varphi_{j}.\end{aligned}

For the optimal choice of
k
, we will have
J_{1}=[k]
and
\tilde{\Gamma}_{1,\mathrm{thre}}^{-1/2}=\Gamma_{1,k}^{-1/2}
. The main conclusion of this paper is given now.

Theorem
3
(informal)
.

Under certain conditions on the kernel, the design vector and the noise, for any
k\leq N
that satisfies certain conditions, the following fact holds with high probability:

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{f}_{\lambda}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}\lesssim r_{\lambda,k}^{*},

where

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} r_{\lambda,k}^{*}&=\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{\frac{\left|J_{1}\right|}{N}}+\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{j\in J_{2}}\sigma_{j}}{4\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}}+\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}f_{k+1:\infty}^{*}\right\|_{\cal H}+\\
&{\left\|\tilde{\Gamma}_{1,\mathrm{thre}}^{-1/2}f_{1:k}^{*}\right\|_{\cal H}\frac{2\lambda+3\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}{N}}+\sigma_{\xi}\frac{\sqrt{N\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}}{\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}.\end{aligned}

(4)

We provide some comments on Theorem
3
.
In general, based on the choice of
\lambda
and the spectrum of
\Gamma
, KRR automatically decomposes the feature space
\mathcal{H}
into a direct sum of two subspaces:
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1:k}\oplus^{\perp}\mathcal{H}_{k+1:\infty}
. In
{\cal H}_{1:k}
, KRR estimates the target function
f^{*}
, while in
{\cal H}_{k+1:\infty}
, KRR absorbs noise. Here,
k
is a key quantity that determines the estimation error of KRR. For each
k
, KRR performs an associated decomposition of
\mathcal{H}
, utilizing different subspaces for estimation, while the remaining subspace absorbs noise, leading to varying estimation errors
r_{\lambda,k}^{*}
. Therefore, there exists an optimal
k
that minimizes the rate defined by that choice among all possible
k
. We emphasize that
k
is a free parameter; that is, for KRR, statisticians do not select a priori a specific
k
; rather, this
k
is needed for analysis. Thus, this
k
is determined by KRR itself; somehow KRR learns by itself the best features space decomposition. In the space
\mathcal{H}_{1:k}
(where estimation happens),
[k]
is further divided into
J_{1}
and
J_{2}
, which correspond to different parts in the final bound. In most applications,
J_{1}=[k]
and
J_{2}=\emptyset
. In the classical case (for instance, when
\lambda\sim N\sigma_{k}
, in Proposition
4
), the dominating term in the definition of
\tilde{\Gamma}_{1,\mathrm{thre}}^{-1/2}
is
\sigma_{j}
, which means that KRR does not filter out the largest
k
eigenvalues. However, theoretically, there may exist choices of
k
and
\lambda
such that KRR filters out only certain eigenvalues, with the number of these eigenvalues being less than
k
.
In
\mathcal{H}_{k+1:\infty}
,
f_{k+1:\infty}^{*}
is treated as noise, which leads to the term
\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}f_{k+1:\infty}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}}
in (
4
). The space
\mathcal{H}_{k+1:\infty}
is responsible for noise absorption, corresponding to the term
\sigma_{\xi}\frac{\sqrt{N\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}}{\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}
in (
4
). Here, it is necessary for
\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})
to be relatively small compared to
\lambda^{2}
and
(\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}))^{2}
in order to effectively absorb noise. When
\lambda
dominates
\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}
(which corresponds to the case of sufficiently strong regularization), this implies that
\lambda
needs to be large enough to absorb noise. Conversely, if
\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})
dominates (which corresponds to insufficient regularization), we require the spectrum of
\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}
to be well spread, meaning that the ratio of its
\ell_{2}
norm to its
\ell_{1}
norm must be sufficiently small.

Next, we introduce the main tools used to prove Theorem
3
. Following that, we compare Theorem
3
with classical bounds and discuss its applications.

2.2
A Dvoretzky-Milman theorem for RKHS

Below is the probabilistic version of the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem
[
Mil23
,
Pis89
]
.

Theorem 1
.

There are absolute constants
\kappa_{DM}\leq 1
and
c_{0}
such that the following holds. Let
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\cdot\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}
be some norm on
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and denote by
B
its unit ball and by
B^{*}
its unit dual ball. The Dvoretsky-Milman dimension of
B
is
d_{*}(B)=\left(\frac{\ell^{*}(B^{*})}{{\rm diam}(B^{*},\ell_{2}^{p})}\right)^{2}
,
where
\ell^{*}(B^{*})={\mathbb{E}}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|G\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}
,
G
is a standard Gaussian random vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
,
{\rm diam}(B^{*},\ell_{2}^{p})=\sup\left(\left\|v\right\|_{2}:v\in B^{*}\right)
.
Denote by
{\mathbb{G}}:={\mathbb{G}}^{(N\times p)}
, the
N\times p
standard Gaussian matrix with i.i.d.
{\cal N}(0,1)
Gaussian entries. Assume that
N\leq\kappa_{DM}d_{*}(B)
. Then with probability at least
1-\exp(-c_{0}d_{*}(B))
, for every
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\right\|_{2}\ell^{*}(B^{*})\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|{\mathbb{G}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}\leq\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\right\|_{2}\ell^{*}(B^{*}).

The probabilistic version of the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem posits that, with high probability, the random section of a convex body (denoted as
B
), which is generated by its intersection with the image of a Gaussian random matrix, exhibits properties that are nearly Euclidean with radius of the order of the Gaussian mean width
\ell^{*}(B)={\mathbb{E}}\sup(\left<G,{\boldsymbol{v}}\right>:\,{\boldsymbol{v}}\in B)
where
G
is a standard Gaussian random vector, under the condition that the dimension of the image is smaller than the Dvoretzky-Milman dimension
[
Pis89
]
. This note focuses on the scenario where
B
is equal to the ellipsoid
\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H}
, where
B_{\cal H}
represents the unit ball in
{\cal H}
. Therefore, in this paper, we are only concerned with the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem for ellipsoids and so in that case the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem states that
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top}
is well-conditioned, with both smallest and largest singular values of the order of
\sqrt{\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})}
, the Gaussian mean width of
\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H}
.

A warm up: Dvoretzky-Milman theorem under a strong assumption

One might question the validity of a DM theorem in a RKHS due to several challenges when compared to the Gaussian scenario. Firstly, the feature map is no longer linear in
X
, which may result in a lower concentration to counterbalance metric complexity. Secondly, the feature map is not centered. Lastly, it exhibits non-independent coordinates. The subsequent Lemma, extracted from
[
MKDM22
]
(see also
[
MMM22
]
), demonstrates that, despite the aforementioned challenges, the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem remains applicable to
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top}
, subject to a more stringent requirement.

Lemma 1

(
[
MKDM22
]
)
.

Recall the notations from Section
2.1
. Then,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left\|{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top}-\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)I_{N}\right\|_{\text{op}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2N^{2}\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)+2\sup\left(\left|K_{k+1:\infty}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}})-\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})\right|^{2}:\,{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\Omega\right).

It is easy to see from
[
Ver18
, Lemma 4.1.5]
together with Lemma
1
that if
N^{2}\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)\lesssim\Tr^{2}\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
, and if
K_{k+1:\infty}({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}})
concentrates well, the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem holds for
\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H}
with constant probability. This Lemma addresses any doubts regarding the validity of the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem for non-linear, non-centered, and dependent-coordinate features. On the other hand, we observe that
N^{2}\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)\lesssim\Tr^{2}\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
is more restrictive than the classical Dvoretzky-Milman condition
N\lesssim\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)/\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}
obtained in the Gaussian case. One may wonder whether it is possible to establish the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem under this weaker assumption.

The authors of
[
MKDM22
, Theorem 3]
establish the minimality of
N^{2}\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)\lesssim\Tr^{2}\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
by demonstrating that the condition number of
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top}
exceeds
N^{4}/d^{2}
with a high probability when the
\varphi_{j}
’s are Fourier basis functions. However, this counter-example is somewhat pessimistic; we will demonstrate that, by imposing a stronger assumption, we can broaden the applicability of the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem from
N^{2}\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)\lesssim\Tr^{2}\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
to
N\lesssim\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)/\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}
, the optimal Dvoretzky-Milman dimension appearing in the Gaussian case, and we can recover the classical condition of the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem for ellipsoids in the Gaussian case. We also obtain a DM theorem that holds with large probability instead of in expectation.

Dvoretzky-Milman theorem for ellipsoid under an
L_{2+\epsilon}-L_{2}
moment-equivalence assumption

For any
\lambda\geq 0
, define

d_{\lambda}^{*}(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H}):=\frac{\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})+\lambda}{\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}}

(5)

as the modified (by
\lambda
) Dvoretzky-Milman dimension for the RKHS associated with the projected kernel
K_{k+1:\infty}
. When
\lambda=0
,
d_{0}^{*}(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H})
is the classical Dvoretzky-Milman dimension (up to a universal constant),
[
Pis89
, pp. 42]
. We grant the following assumption.

Assumption 1
.

There are absolute constants
{C_{1}}>1
,
C_{\ref*{C_distortion_1}}>1
,
0\leq\gamma<1/16
,
0\leq\delta<1/(100\sqrt{C_{\ref*{C_distortion_1}}})
,
\bar{\delta}<C_{\ref*{C_DM}}^{-1}
,
\epsilon>0
and
\kappa>1
such that

•

With probability at least
1-\gamma
,

\max_{1\leq i\leq N}\left|\frac{\left\|\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X_{i})\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}}{(\ell^{*})^{2}}-1\right|\leq\delta,

(6)

where we define
\ell^{*}=\sqrt{{\mathbb{E}}\left\|\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}}=\sqrt{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}
.

•

For any
f\in{\cal H}_{k+1:\infty}
, we have

\left\|f\right\|_{L_{2+\epsilon}}\leq\kappa\left\|f\right\|_{L_{2}}.

(7)

•

Depending on the choice of
\epsilon
, there are two cases:

1.

if
\epsilon>2
, then no extra assumption is required.

2.

if
0<\epsilon\leq 2
, then

\displaystyle\kappa N^{\frac{2-\epsilon}{2\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}}}\log{(N)}\left(\frac{\sqrt{N\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}}{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}\right)<\bar{\delta}.

(8)

The assumption described in (
6
) asserts that the diagonal components of the kernel matrix, denoted as

\left({\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top}\right)_{ii}=K_{k+1,\infty}(X_{i},X_{i})
,
exhibit a high level of concentration around their expected value
\ell^{*}
. In the context of a translation-invariant kernel, it can be observed that (
6
) is always satisfied for
\delta=\gamma=0
(see Section
6.5.15
in supplementary material). Similarly, for an inner-product kernel, (
6
) can be validated by employing concentration inequalities of polynomials. For a data-dependent kernel, the verification of (
6
) relies on the specific properties of that kernel. We provide an example in Proposition
10
below.

When
\epsilon>2
, there is no need for an extra condition on
N
and, as in the Gaussian case, everything holds under the classical DM condition
N\leq\kappa_{DM}d_{\lambda}^{*}\left(\Gamma^{-1/2}B_{\cal H}\right)
, even though we work under a weak
L_{2+\epsilon}-L_{2}
equivalence assumption; when
0<\epsilon\leq 2
, we need the extra condition (
8
), which is an additional assumption on
N
and the spectrum of
\Gamma
.

The assertion made in (
8
) is that the convergence rate of
\sqrt{N\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}/\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
is greater than that of
\left(N^{\frac{2-\epsilon}{2\epsilon+\epsilon^{2}}}\log{N}\right)^{-1}
up to a universal constant (as
\bar{\delta}
and
\kappa
will be chosen to be constant). Condition (
8
) can also be compared with
[
MKDM22
,
MM20
]
, where the more stringent assumption of
\sqrt{N\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}/\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)\leq 1/\sqrt{N}
is granted. In fact, when
\epsilon\geq 2\sqrt{2}-2
, our result is an improvement on
[
MKDM22
,
MM20
]
. We conjecture that (
8
) can be removed even when
0<\epsilon\leq 2
(maybe by using a clever adaptation of the coloring technique from
[
Tik18
]
).

Our main result from this section is the following, and its proof may be found in Section
6.3
.

Theorem 2
.

Let
X
be a random vector distributed as
\mu
in a compact set
\Omega\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, and let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{N}
be i.i.d. copies of
X
. Let
\phi:x\in\Omega\mapsto K(x,\cdot)\in{\cal H}
be the feature map of the RKHS
{\cal H}
. Let
C_{\ref*{C_comparision_trace_lambda}}
be an absolute constant.

1.

If
\lambda\leq C_{\ref*{C_comparision_trace_lambda}}\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
. Let
0<\delta,\bar{\delta}<1
from Assumption
1
, define

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}=C_{\ref*{C_distortion_1}}\delta^{2}+C_{\ref*{C_distortion_2}}\bar{\delta}^{2}+4\sqrt{\left(3\delta+C_{\ref*{C_distortion_3}}\bar{\delta}\right)\left(1+\delta+C_{\ref*{C_distortion_4}}\bar{\delta}\right)}.

(9)

Suppose that for some
\lambda\geq 0
, we have
N\leq\kappa_{DM}\bar{\delta}^{2}d_{\lambda}^{*}\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H}\right)
for a sufficiently small constant
\kappa_{DM}<1
which depends only on
\kappa
(see (
94
) for a precise description).
We assume that
\phi_{k+1:\infty}
satisfies Assumption
1
. Then with probability at least

1-\gamma-\frac{1}{N^{2}}-\left(\frac{\kappa}{\bar{\delta}}\right)^{2+\epsilon}\left(\frac{\sqrt{N\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}}{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}\right)^{2+\epsilon}\frac{\log^{2+\epsilon}(N)}{N^{\frac{\epsilon}{2}-1}}=:1-\bar{p}_{DM},

for all
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,

\left(1-\tilde{\delta}\right)\sqrt{\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\right\|_{2}\leq\left\|{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\right\|_{\cal H}\leq\left(1+\tilde{\delta}\right)\sqrt{\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})}\left\|{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\right\|_{2}.

(10)

2.

If
\lambda>C_{\ref*{C_comparision_trace_lambda}}\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
. Suppose that
\phi_{k+1:\infty}
satisfies the first two points of Assumption
1
. Suppose that for some
\lambda\geq 0
, we have
N\leq(\kappa_{DM}/4)d_{\lambda}^{*}(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H})
. There then exist absolute constants
{C_{2}}
depending on
\epsilon,\kappa,\kappa_{DM}
, and
0<{c_{1}}<1
such that with probability at least

\displaystyle 1-\gamma-N\left(\left(\frac{\kappa_{DM}\kappa^{2}\log^{2}(N)}{N}\right)^{1+\epsilon/2}N\right)^{\lceil(12+2\epsilon)/\epsilon\rceil-1}-\frac{1}{N^{2}}=:1-\bar{p}_{DM},

we have

\left\|{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top}+\lambda I\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq C_{\ref*{C_distortion_9}}\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
and
\sigma_{N}\left({\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top}+\lambda I\right)\geq c_{\ref*{c_distortion_9}}\lambda+(1-c_{\ref*{c_distortion_9}})C_{\ref*{C_comparision_trace_lambda}}\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
.

We can make some remarks comparing Theorem
2
to Lemma
1
:

•

Theorem
2
presents an “almost-isometric” form of the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem. In the analysis of the estimation error of KRR, it suffices to employ an isomorphic variant of the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem. Specifically, by selecting negligibly small constants
\delta
and
\bar{\delta}
, we can ensure that the distortion
\tilde{\delta}
is also a small constant. In fact,
\tilde{\delta}<1/2
suffices for our purpose. In this case, we can speak about isomorphy instead of isometry.

•

Assumption
N\lesssim\frac{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}{\sqrt{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}}
from Lemma
1
needed to obtain isomorphy is much more restrictive compared to condition
N\lesssim\frac{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}{\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}}
from Theorem
2
.
Up to (
8
), we recover the same condition as in the Gaussian case.

•

When
\epsilon>2
in Assumption
1
and
N\leq\kappa_{DM}d_{\lambda}^{*}\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H}\right)
, (
8
) is automatically satisfied because
\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)\leq\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
. Theorem
2
extends the main result in
[
GLPTJ17
]
from
L_{4+\epsilon}-L_{2}
equivalence to
L_{2+\epsilon}-L_{2}
equivalence.

•

The classical version of the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem asserts that with high probability, the intersection of a convex body
B
with the image of a random Gaussian matrix, that is,
B\cap\mathrm{Range}({\mathbb{G}}^{\top})
, is almost Euclidean. In Theorem
2
,
case 1)
extends this result to the case where
B
is an ellipsoid to the scenario of random matrices under weak moment assumptions. However,
case 2)
of Theorem
2
establishes that the condition number of
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top}+\lambda I
is constant, and scales with
\lambda
in the same order. While this does not directly imply an almost Euclidean property for
B\cap\mathrm{Range}({\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top})
, for convenience, we still refer to it as the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem.

•

In
case 2)
of Theorem
2
, it is, in fact, unnecessary to compute the lower bound of the minimum (non-zero) singular values of
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}
. Therefore, even the
L_{4}
-
L_{2}
equivalence conditions of the Bai-Yin theorem is not required. Indeed, if allowing for a logarithmic factor of sub-optimality, the scenario of
case 2)
of Theorem
2
can hold for a more general kernel. This only requires replacing the upper bound in
case 2)
with Proposition
8
and noting that the lower bound in
case 2)
does not depend on the properties of
\phi(X)
, see Proposition
25
in supplementary material.

Upper bound of the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem

In this paragraph, we prove that there exists an absolute constant
{C_{3}}>0
such that with high probability, for any
{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, under no assumption on
N
,

\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\right\|_{\cal H}\leq C_{\ref*{C_DMU}}\left(\sqrt{\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})}+\sqrt{N}\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}\right)\left\|{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\right\|_{2}.

(11)

This will happen to be true, under the following
L_{4+\epsilon}-L_{2}
norm equivalent assumption and an assumption concerning diagonal terms.

Assumption 2
.

There exist absolute constants
{\gamma_{1}}\in\left(0,\frac{1}{16}\right)
,
{\delta_{1}}\geq 0
,
\epsilon>0
and
\kappa^{\prime}>1
such that

•

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq N}\frac{\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X_{i})\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}}{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}\leq 1+\delta_{\ref*{delta_DMU_L2}}\right)\geq 1-{\gamma_{\ref*{gamma_DMU_L2}}},

(12)

•

for any
f\in{\cal H}_{k+1:\infty}
,
\left\|f\right\|_{L_{4+\epsilon}}\leq\kappa^{\prime}\left\|f\right\|_{L_{2}}
.

The next result is proven in Section
6.5.1
. It is similar to
[
TB23
, Theorem 4]
and
[
GLPTJ17
]
.

Proposition 1

(
[
TB23
]
or
[
GLPTJ17
]
)
.

Suppose Assumption
2
holds. There exists a constant
{c_{2}}
such that (
11
) holds with a probability of at least
1-\frac{c_{\ref*{c_P_DMU}}}{N^{\epsilon}}-{\gamma_{\ref*{gamma_DMU_L2}}}
.
The symbol
\bar{p}_{DMU}
is defined as
\frac{c_{\ref*{c_P_DMU}}}{N^{\epsilon}}+{\gamma_{\ref*{gamma_DMU_L2}}}
.

The validity of Assumption
2
is not universal; therefore, we present an alternative assumption in the supplementary material, as discussed in Section
5.3
. The proof of Proposition
1
can be found in Section
6.5.1
.

2.3
Restricted Isomorphy Property

The Restricted Isomorphy Property characterizes the geometric properties of the RKHS design matrix restricted to
{\cal H}_{1:k}
, that is,
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,1:k}
. We will see later that this is the part of the space where estimation happens. It identifies the set on which, with high probability, the operator
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,1:k}:{\cal H}_{1:k}\to\ell_{2}^{N}
forms an isomorphism when
k\lesssim N
or restricted isomorphism when
k\gtrsim N
. This property was used for linear functionals of sub-Gaussian random vectors in
[
LS22
]
in the context of benign overfitting in linear regression. In this paper, since we need to study RKHS, we must establish a corresponding version of this restricted isomorphy property.

When
k\lesssim N

When
k\lesssim N
, the RKHS design operator
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,1:k}
behaves like an isomorphy over the entire space
{\cal H}_{1:k}
under the following assumption.

Assumption 3
.

There exist absolute constants
\kappa^{\prime\prime}\geq 1
,
{c_{3}}
depending on
\kappa^{\prime\prime}
(
c_{\ref*{c_RIP}}\leq\frac{9}{1568(\kappa^{\prime\prime})^{4}}
is sufficient),
0\leq{\gamma_{2}}<1/16
,
\epsilon>0
,
{\delta_{2}}\geq 0
such that

•

k\leq c_{\ref*{c_RIP}}N
.

•

with probability at least
1-{\gamma_{\ref*{gamma_RIP}}}
,

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq N}\left\|\Gamma_{1:k}^{-1/2}\phi_{1:k}(X_{i})\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\leq\delta_{\ref*{delta_RIP}}k,

(13)

•

for any
f\in{\cal H}_{1:k}
,
\left\|f\right\|_{L_{4+\epsilon}}\leq\kappa^{\prime\prime}\left\|f\right\|_{L_{2}}
;

The next result shows the isomorphy property of
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,1:k}
on
{\cal H}_{1:k}
under Assumption
3
.

Proposition 2
.

Under Assumption
3
, there exist absolute constants
{c_{4}}
,
c_{\ref*{c_RIP_lower}}
and
C_{\ref*{C_RIP_upper}}
such that with probability at least
1-{\gamma_{\ref*{gamma_RIP}}}-\frac{c_{\ref*{c_P_RIP}}}{N^{\epsilon}}-2\exp(-k)
,
for all
f_{1:k}\in{\cal H}_{1:k}
,

\displaystyle c_{\ref*{c_RIP_lower}}\left\|\Gamma_{1:k}^{1/2}f_{1:k}\right\|_{\cal H}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left\|{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,1:k}f_{1:k}\right\|_{2}\leq C_{\ref*{C_RIP_upper}}\left\|\Gamma_{1:k}^{1/2}f_{1:k}\right\|_{\cal H},

where
c_{\ref*{c_RIP_lower}}
can be taken as
\frac{1}{2}
and
C_{\ref*{C_RIP_upper}}
can be taken as
\sqrt{2C_{\ref*{C_DMU}}^{2}(1+c_{\ref*{c_RIP}})}
.
We denote
\bar{p}_{RIP}
as
{\gamma_{\ref*{gamma_RIP}}}+\frac{c_{\ref*{c_P_RIP}}}{N^{\epsilon}}+2\exp(-k)
.

When
k
is not necessarily smaller than
N

When
k
is not necessarily smaller than
N
, the design matrix
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,1:k}
cannot behave like an isomorphy over the entire space
{\cal H}_{1:k}
because it has a non-trivial kernel, but it can be an isomorphy restricted to a subset of
{\cal H}_{1:k}
. This set can be taken to be a cone defined below in (
15
). We refer to this property as the Restricted Isomorphy Property (RIP) as in
[
LS22
]
in reminiscence to the RIP used in Compressed sensing
[
FR13
]
. Please note that we can still use the following proposition to replace Proposition
2
when
k\lesssim N
, albeit at the cost of incurring a logarithmic factor.

Proposition 3
.

For any
R>0
, let
\bar{\Gamma}_{1:k}^{-1/2}=\sum_{j\leq k}\min\left(\frac{1}{R},\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{j}}}\right)\varphi_{j}\otimes\varphi_{j}
. For some
{c_{5}}
sufficiently small (
c_{\ref*{c_kappa_RIP}}<\frac{1}{100C_{\ref*{C_Rudelson}}^{2}C_{\ref*{C_estimate_gamma_infty}}^{2}}
is sufficient), let

\displaystyle R_{N}(c_{\ref*{c_kappa_RIP}})=\inf\left\{R>0:\,\left\|\max_{i\in[N]}\left\|\bar{\Gamma}_{1:k}^{-1/2}\phi(X_{i})\right\|_{\cal H}\right\|_{L_{\infty}}\leq c_{\ref*{c_kappa_RIP}}\frac{\sqrt{N}}{\log{N}}\right\}.

(14)

For any
0<{\delta_{3}}<1
, there exist absolute constants
c_{\ref*{c_RIP_lower}}
, and
C_{\ref*{C_RIP_upper}}
depending on
\delta_{\ref*{delta_P_RIP}}
such that when
R\geq R_{N}(c_{\ref*{c_kappa_RIP}})
, then with probability at least
1-\delta_{\ref*{delta_P_RIP}}
, for all
f\in\mathrm{cone}\left({\cal C}(R)\right)
, where

\displaystyle{\cal C}(R)=R^{-1}B_{{\cal H}_{1:k}}\cap\Gamma_{1:k}^{-1/2}S_{{\cal H}_{1:k}}\mbox{ and }\mathrm{cone}\left({\cal C}(R)\right)=\left\{f\in{\cal H}_{1:k}:\,R\left\|f\right\|_{\cal H}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{L_{2}}\right\},

(15)

we have

\displaystyle c_{\ref*{c_RIP_lower}}\left\|\Gamma_{1:k}^{1/2}f_{1:k}\right\|_{\cal H}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\left\|{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,1:k}f_{1:k}\right\|_{2}\leq C_{\ref*{C_RIP_upper}}\left\|\Gamma_{1:k}^{1/2}f_{1:k}\right\|_{\cal H}.

In contrast to Proposition
2
, we only have constant probability deviation in Proposition
3
. We refer to Proposition
3
as the “Restricted Isomorphic Property under embedding index condition” because the estimation of the fixed point
R_{N}(c_{\ref*{c_kappa_RIP}})
requires the embedding index condition. An example of the estimate of
R_{N}(c_{\ref*{c_kappa_RIP}})
may be found in Section
6.5.4
. The proof of Proposition
2
and Proposition
3
are postponed to Section
6.5.2
.

3
Main Results

In this section, we present the upper bounds on the estimation error of KRR.

3.1
Our results

As remarked in several works
[
BMR21
,
TB23
,
LS22
]
, there is a fundamental parameter
k
which is the dimension of the space endowed by the top
k
eigenvectors of
\Gamma
where ’estimation’ happens whereas, on the orthogonal space, ’absorption of the noise (and even overfitting of the noise when
\lambda=0
)’ happens. Our analysis depends on the case where this parameter is smaller or larger than the number of data
N
.

When
k\leq c_{\ref*{c_RIP}}N

In this paragraph, we provide conclusions for the case of
k\lesssim N
. This scenario is precisely what linear regression and Multiple Descents problems are most concerned with. We recall that the definition of
d_{\lambda}^{*}(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H})
is in Equation (
5
), the ones of of
\bar{p}_{RIP}
,
\bar{p}_{DM}
, and
\bar{p}_{DMU}
are in Proposition
2
, Theorem
2
, and Proposition
1
, respectively.

Theorem 3
.

Suppose Assumptions
1
,
2
and
3
hold. There then exist absolute constants
{C_{4}}
,
c_{\ref*{c_RIP}}
,
c_{\ref*{c_kappa_DM}}
,
c_{\ref*{c_P_bX_f_star}}
,
C_{\ref*{C_noise}}
(
C_{\ref*{C_noise}}
depends on
{\kappa_{\ref*{kappa_noise}}}
) and
{C_{5}}
, such that the following holds. Suppose the noise
\xi
is independent of
X
with mean zero and variance
\sigma_{\xi}^{2}
. We assume that for some
{\kappa_{\ref*{kappa_noise}}}>0
and
r>4
,
\left\|\xi\right\|_{L_{r}}\leq{\kappa_{\ref*{kappa_noise}}}\sigma_{\xi}
. Let
\lambda\geq 0
.
We assume that there exists
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
so that
C_{\ref*{C_N_lower}}\leq N\leq c_{\ref*{c_kappa_DM}}\kappa_{DM}d_{\lambda}^{*}\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H}\right)
,
\sigma_{1}N>\kappa_{DM}(4\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right))
and
k\leq c_{\ref*{c_RIP}}N
. Let
\bar{p}_{\xi}
be some probability deviation strictly less than
1
(defined later in (
66
)). Then with probability at least

\displaystyle 1-\bar{p}_{RIP}-\bar{p}_{DM}-\bar{p}_{DMU}-\frac{c_{\ref*{c_P_bX_f_star}}}{N}-\bar{p}_{\xi}-\left(\frac{C_{\ref*{C_noise}}\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})}{|J_{1}|\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})+N\left(\sum_{j\in J_{2}}\sigma_{j}\right)}\right)^{\frac{r}{4}},

we have, for
r_{\lambda,k}^{*}
is defined in (
4
), that

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{f}_{\lambda}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ref*{C_rate_upper}}r_{\lambda,k}^{*}.

When
k
is not necessarily smaller than
c_{\ref*{c_RIP}}N

In this paragraph, we provide the informal version of our conclusion for the case of
k
is not necessarily smaller than
c_{\ref*{c_RIP}}N
(the formal version may be found in Section
5.3
in supplementary material). In fact, for the context of the minimum
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
norm interpolant estimator in linear regression, the authors of
[
LS22
]
have already conducted research on this scenario. They have demonstrated that when the design vector is symmetric, the optimal value of
k
—the one that minimizes the estimation error among all possible
k
—falls precisely within the range of
k\lesssim N
if one wants benign overfitting to happen.
However, in the case of KRR, there is no lower bound indicating that the optimal
k
must satisfy
k\lesssim N
. We therefore present the following theorem.

Theorem
6
(informal)
.

Under certain conditions, for any
\lambda\geq 0
and
k
satisfying the Dvoretzky-Milman condition, with high probability, we have
\|\hat{f}_{\lambda}-f^{*}\|_{L_{2}}\lesssim r_{\lambda,k}^{*}
where
r_{\lambda,k}^{*}
is defined in (
4
).

Remark 1

(Misspecified model)
.

In practical applications, we often encounter cases where
f^{*}\notin{\cal H}
, as exemplified by our Proposition
11
. When
f^{*}\notin{\cal H}
, we define
f^{**}=\arg\min(\|f^{*}-f\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}:f\in{\cal H})
, that is, the orthogonal projection of
f^{*}
onto
{\cal H}
in the
L_{2}(\mu)
inner product (we could also choose other oracles as we have the liberty to choose the oracle). We replace the target function in Theorem
3
and Theorem
6
with
f^{**}
and replace the noise
{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
with
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}={\boldsymbol{r}}+{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
, where
{\boldsymbol{r}}=(f^{*}(X_{i})-f^{**}(X_{i}))_{i=1}^{N}
. This new noise
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}
is dependent on the kernel design matrix
{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi}
. See Proposition
28
, Proposition
29
and the subsequent discussion of this property in supplementary material and Proposition
11
for an example when
f^{*}\notin{\cal H}
. This conclusion is not contradictory to the counterexamples provided in
[
CLvdG22
]
and
[
Sha22
]
, as indicated in Remark
8
in supplementary material.

Remark 2

(Uniform results in
\lambda
)
.

It is possible to have results equivalent to Theorem
3
and Theorem
6
uniform in the regularization parameter
\lambda
. This type of results is particularly useful when one wants to use a data-dependent regularization parameter in order to achieve optimal and adaptive results. It is for instance the case, when
\lambda
is chosen according to the Lepski’s method
[
BMM19
]
. In that case, our results hold with the same probability and convergence rates however, for instance in Theorem
3
, we just need to assume that
N\lesssim d_{\lambda_{0}}^{*}\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H}\right)
for some
\lambda_{0}\geq 0
and then the result of Theorem
3
holds uniformly for all
\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}
. This may be particularly useful when
\lambda_{0}=0
.

3.2
Comparison with previous results

In this section, we primarily select conclusions from
[
MKDM22
]
, and
[
MR22
]
. Due to length constraints, we have placed the comparison with the results of
[
BMR21
, Section 4.3.3, Theorem 4.13]
in Section
5.3
of the supplementary material.

Theorem 4

(
[
MKDM22
]
)
.

Let
k\log{k}\lesssim N
. Suppose that some absolute constants
\alpha_{1}\geq\alpha_{2}>0
exist such that
\alpha_{1}I_{N}\succeq\lambda I_{N}+{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top}\succeq\alpha_{2}I_{N}
and

\frac{\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}}{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}+\frac{2}{N}\left\|{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,1:k}^{\top}{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,1:k}-NI\right\|_{\text{op},L_{2}\to L_{2}}\leq c

for some
c<1
where
I
is the identical operator on
{\cal H}_{1:k}
. Then with constant probability

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{f}_{\lambda}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}\lesssim\sigma_{\xi}^{2}\left(1+\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\alpha_{2}}\right)^{2}\left(\frac{k}{N}+\frac{4N\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}{\left(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\right)^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(\min\left\{\sqrt{\sigma_{1}},\frac{1}{1-c}\frac{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{N\sqrt{\sigma_{k}}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})},\frac{1}{1-c}\sqrt{\frac{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}{N(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})}}\right\}\right)^{2}\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{N\sigma_{k+1}(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})}{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}}\right)^{2}\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}.

In
[
MKDM22
]
, the authors use Lemma
1
and the matrix Bernstein inequality to verify the assumptions under the conditions
k\log{k}\leq cN
,
N\sqrt{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}\leq c\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
for some small constant
c
when
{\cal H}_{1:k}
is spanned by some bounded orthonormal system. We provide some comments on this result.

•

Theorem
4
applies only when the conditions
N\sqrt{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}\leq c\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
and
k\log k\lesssim N
are valid. In contrast, thanks to Proposition
2
and Theorem
2
, our results hold in the optimal Gaussian regime i.e.
k\lesssim N
and
N\lesssim d_{\lambda}^{*}(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H})
and thus for a wider range of kernels. Please refer to the discussion after Lemma
1
.

•

Consequently, in order to satisfy the inequality
N\sqrt{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}\leq c\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
, it is necessary to select a sufficiently large value for
k
. However, this choice of
k
may (1) contradict the assumption
k\log k\lesssim N
from Theorem
4
and (2) the term
k/N
appearing in the rate in Theorem
4
may be sub-optimal. Due to this limitation, the findings presented by Theorem
4
lack the necessary precision to observe numerous phenomena that will be further examined in this study.

•

Despite the utilization of Proposition
2
and Theorem
2
in the context of Theorem
4
, it is evident that the other terms in the upper bound are not optimal. As demonstrated by Theorem
3
below, the term
\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{\cal H}
can be replaced by the smaller ones
\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}f_{k+1:\infty}^{*}\right\|_{\cal H}
and
\left\|\tilde{\Gamma}_{1,\mathrm{thre}}^{-1/2}f_{1:k}^{*}\right\|_{{\cal H}}(\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right))/N
.

•

The upper bound stated in Theorem
4
is dependent on the dimension
k
. The selection of the parameter
k
is crucial as it influences the decomposition of
{\cal H}
into two orthogonal subspaces, namely
{\cal H}_{1:k}
and
{\cal H}_{k+1:\infty}
. The former is utilized for estimating the (part of) target function
f_{1:k}^{*}
, while the latter is employed for accommodating the noise, see Proposition
15
below for a clear statement of the ’feature space’ (i.e. the RKHS here) decomposition. In a general sense, when the value of
k
is increased, the estimator
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
takes into account, a larger portion of the space as containing valuable information of
f^{*}
, while allocating less space for the absorption of noise. The assumption made in Theorem
4
is that
k\log{k}\lesssim N
, which imposes a restriction. In Section
4.2
, we will explore scenarios when it is crucial for
k
to be of at least the same order as
N
.

•

The probability deviation appearing in Theorem
4
is of a constant level, whereas Theorem
3
holds with large probability tending to
1
when
N
tends to infinity.

We also notice the results presented in
[
MR22
]
. These results have been utilized by
[
BES+22
,
BBSS22
]
to analyze the estimation error of KRR defined by data-dependent kernels. We present a conclusion here, demonstrating that by selecting an appropriate value of
k
, we can recover the bounds established in
[
MR22
,
Bac24
]
. This implies that this classical result corresponds to a specific choice of
k
in our bounds even though this choice may not be the optimal one. Indeed, many examples discussed below indicate the existence of values of
k
for which our bounds are sharper than those associated with the classical bounds. This illustrates the benefits of our bounds.

Proposition 4
.

Given
\lambda>0
, let
k=\min(j\in{\mathbb{N}}:\,\lambda\gtrsim N\sigma_{j+1})
. Let
\mathrm{Proj}_{{\cal H}}f^{*}
be the orthogonal projection of
f^{*}
onto the closure of
{\cal H}
. Then

\displaystyle(r_{\lambda,k}^{*})^{2}+\left\|f^{*}-\mathrm{Proj}_{{\cal H}}f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(1+\frac{\Tr(\Gamma)}{\lambda}\right)^{2}\inf\left(\left\|f-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{N}\left\|f\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}:\,f\in{\cal H}\right)+\frac{\sigma_{\xi}^{2}}{N}\Tr\left(\Gamma\left(\Gamma+\frac{\lambda}{N}I\right)^{-1}\right).

The proof of Proposition
4
may be found in Section
6.5.6
in supplementary material. The bound on the right side of Proposition
4
is the upper bound for the generalization error of KRR provided by
[
MR22
,
Bac24
]
. Here,
\|f^{*}-\mathrm{Proj}_{\mathcal{H}}f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}
represents the approximation error. In Proposition
29
in supplementary material, we obtain an upper bound for the generalization error under a misspecified model, which corresponds to the left side of Proposition
4
. This indicates that our bound is never worse than the bound provided by
[
MR22
]
. However, it’s worth noting that the results from
[
MR22
]
are not sharp in many cases. For instance, their variance term is only valid when
\lambda\gtrsim 1
(hence it cannot cover interpolant estimators, where
\lambda=0
), and their bias term is also not sharp compared to ours, as we will demonstrate in Section
4.2
. Moreover,
[
MR22
]
can only choose the oracle as the projection of
f^{*}
onto the closure of
\mathcal{H}
, while our general bound allows for the selection of any oracle.

After the completion of the material preparation for this paper, we became aware of the work by
[
BS24
]
. We emphasize the differences between
[
BS24
]
and the current paper:

1.

Theorem 1 in
[
BS24
]
is used to control a lower bound on the smallest eigenvalue of the kernel matrix. However, their lower bound still requires
N^{2}\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})<\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})
, as seen in Equation 12 of
[
BS24
]
. This poses a similar issue to Lemma
1
, whereas our Theorem
2
does not require this assumption.

2.

The lower bound in Theorem 1 of
[
BS24
]
contains an additional logarithmic factor (their
\beta_{k}k\log(k)<n
and
\log(k+1)
terms), which leads to the need for an additional logarithmic factor in their statistical conclusion
[
BS24
, Theorem 2]
, similar to
[
MKDM22
]
. In contrast, our Theorem
3
and Theorem
6
address both cases of
k\lesssim N
and
k\gtrsim N
separately, thus not imposing requirements on the choice of
k
. It’s worth noting that the choice of
k
determines the decomposition of
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
, hence the unnecessary logarithmic factor in the final bound due to larger
k
in
[
BS24
, Theorem 2]
.

3.

Definition 2 in
[
BS24
]
requires almost sure upper bounds on
\left\|\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}
,
\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}
, and
\left\|\Gamma_{1:k}^{-1/2}\phi_{1:k}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}
. Our Assumption
1
only requires upper or lower bounds with high probability. This renders the conclusions of
[
BS24
]
inapplicable to linear regression with unbounded probability measures (for example, Gaussian/sub-Gaussian or heavy-tailed distributions). More importantly, this also renders their findings inapplicable to data-dependent conjugate kernels, as discussed in Section
4.4
and Lemma
15
. In Section
4.4
, we will demonstrate the significance of such kernels for deep learning theory.

4
Applications

In this section, we apply our main results (Theorems
3
and
6
) to the study of four phenomenons that have been observed in empirical and theoretical studies on neural networks. Our aim is to show that sharp upper bounds on the convergence properties of KRR are relevant to the understanding of phenomenons in deep learning.

4.1
Multiple Descents of the minimum RKHS-norm interpolant estimator

In this section, we apply our Theorem
3
to the inner product kernel, obtaining the phenomenon of Multiple Descents, a concept that will be explained later in this text.
Imagine that one is training an estimator, and when the number of samples increases, the test error does not decrease and, in fact, may even increase. How can we explain this phenomenon, and should we stop adding more samples? The Multiple Descents phenomenon is closely related to this scenario.

The phenomenon of Multiple Descents, as far as current knowledge indicates, was initially identified by the authors of
[
LRZ20
,
GMMM21
]
. The observation is made that the estimation error
\left\|\hat{f}_{0}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}
, where
\hat{f}_{0}
is KRR for
\lambda=0
, that is, it is the minimum
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\cal H}
-norm interpolant estimator, exhibits numerous decreases as the value of
N
grows. It is important to note that in the proof of the multiple Descents phenomenon, two setups have been considered: one where
d
is fixed and
d\ll N
and the other where
d\to\infty
,
d\ll N
. Our results pertain to both cases where
d
is fixed and where
d\to\infty
. In fact,
[
DWY21
, Lemma 2.1]
demonstrated that for a fairly broad class of
{\cal H}
(including Laplace kernel and exponential inner product kernel), as
d\to\infty
,
{\cal H}
does not even contain relatively simple functions as defined in the following Lemma:

Lemma 2

(
[
DWY21
]
)
.

Let
K:({\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto\prod_{j=1}^{d}q(x_{j},y_{j})
where
q
is some kernel function which may change with
d
. Assume
\int q(x,x)d\mu(x)\leq 1
. Then for any
f:{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto\prod_{j=1}^{m}f_{j}(x_{j})\in{\mathbb{R}}
for some fixed
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
(in particular,
m
can be taken to be
1
) and each
f_{j}
is a function in the RKHS generated by
q
, we have
\left\|f\right\|_{\cal H}\to\infty
as
d\to\infty
.

Let us once again emphasize the motivation for fixed
d
: the variation in
d
makes it challenging to determine which
f^{*}
,
\hat{f}_{0}
is approximating (each
d
defines a distinct
f^{*}
, see Theorem
9
below), and
{\cal H}
itself becomes very small as
d\to\infty
, as indicated by Lemma
2
– therefore restricting the possible choices of
f^{*}
as
d
increases. The specific problem of Multiple Descents pertains to the properties of the inner product kernel, that is, there exists a scalar function
h:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that for any
{\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle K({\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y})=h\left(\frac{\left\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle}{d}\right).

(16)

The phenomenon of Multiple Descents holds significance in both practical and theoretical contexts:

1.

In practical terms, the phenomenon of Multiple Descents informs us that there is no need to be concerned about an increase in estimation error when the number of samples is increased. This is because, once we surpass this increase, our estimator is capable of learning a more accurate approximation of the signal of higher degree. In fact, an increase in
N
will lead to a reduction of the dimension of the space available for accommodating noise. This, in turn, results in an increase in the variance in the estimation error, which is the cause of the rise in estimation error.

2.
