On the other hand, in theoretical terms, the Multiple Descents phenomenon sheds light on the “degree of non-parametricness” of the estimator
\hat{f}_{0}
. This refers to the effectiveness of
\hat{f}_{0}
in learning
f^{*}
when the number of parameters is infinite.

In this section, by applying our general bounds from Theorem
3
, we extend
[
LRZ20
]
(see Theorem
8
in supplementary material) to the optimal interval of
d^{\iota}\lesssim N\lesssim d^{\iota+1}
within the non-asymptotic regime. Moreover, we prove that Multiple Descents can still occur under the condition of sub-Gaussian random vectors. This extends the conclusions of
[
GMMM21
]
to the sub-Gaussian setting.

Proposition
9
(informal)
.

If
h
is a polynomial of degree
L
, under certain conditions (particularly when
X
is a sub-Gaussian random vector), for any
0\leq\iota\leq L-1
, with high probability, we have
\left|\|\hat{f}_{0}-f^{*}\|_{L_{2}}-\|\Gamma_{>\iota}^{1/2}f_{>\iota}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\right|\lesssim r_{0,k}^{*}
, where
r_{0,k}^{*}
is equivalent to
\max\left\{\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{\frac{d^{\iota}}{N}},\frac{1}{N}\|\Gamma_{\leq\iota}^{-1/2}f_{\leq\iota}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}},\|\Gamma_{>\iota}^{1/2}f_{>\iota}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}},\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{\frac{N}{d^{\iota+1}}}\right\}
. Moreover, as
N,d\to\infty
with
\omega(d^{\iota})\leq N\leq o(d^{\iota+1})
, we have
\left|\|\hat{f}_{0}-f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}-\|\Gamma_{>\iota}^{1/2}f_{>\iota}^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}}\right|=o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)(\sigma_{\xi}+\|f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}}).

The formal version of Proposition
9
can be found in Section
5.3
of the supplementary material, and its proof is presented in Section
6.5.12
and Section
6.5.23
. Actually, our bound also holds even with
o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\sigma_{\xi}+O_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)\|\Gamma_{>\iota}^{1/2}f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}}
in place of
o_{d,\mathbb{P}}(1)(\sigma_{\xi}+\|f^{*}\|_{\mathcal{H}})
.

To establish Proposition
9
, we have developed a new Hanson-Wright-type concentration inequality, as presented in Theorem
10
in supplementary material. Below is an informal version of it. This inequality may be of independent interest. For instance, Theorem
10
verifies the assumptions in
[
MKDM22
, Lemma 1]
and
[
MMM22
, Assumption 1(d)]
.

Theorem
10
(informal)
.

Suppose
X
is a sub-Gaussian random vector with i.i.d. mean-zero sub-Gaussian coordinates
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
. Suppose
h
defined in (
16
) is a degree
L
polynomial.
There exists an absolute constant
C_{\ref*{C_non_linear_HS}}
depending on
L
and
\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}
, such that for any
t>0
, we have: with high probability (which is exponential on
-t^{1/L}
), we have
\left|K(X,X)-{\mathbb{E}}K(X,X)\right|<t
.

To our knowledge, Theorem
10
is the first result proving the concentration of kernel functions for sub-Gaussian random vectors with inner product kernels in the non-asymptotic regime. In fact, for uniform distributions on spheres, this is trivial (see Section
6.5.15
of the supplementary material), as the diagonal concentration in this case is equivalent to the diagonal concentration of translation-invariant kernels. This poses a challenge that restricts the applicability of a series of works by
[
GMMM21
,
MMM22
]
to general probability measures. In the asymptotic case, such as in
[
EK10
]
, verifying diagonal concentration is also trivial (see Section
5.1
of the supplementary material) and only requires the use of Lagrange’s theorem. However, in the non-asymptotic case, the situation is more complex, which is why we present Theorem
10
.

Several remarks on Proposition
9
are presented subsequently. Due to length constraints, we have included the results of
[
LRZ20
]
and
[
GMMM21
]
in Section
5.3
of the supplementary material, see Theorem
8
and Theorem
9
there.

1.

In the high-dimensional asymptotic limit, Proposition
9
extends the results of
[
GMMM21
]
to sub-Gaussian designs. To our knowledge, this is the first such extension in the literature, addressing a well-known challenging problem. This establishes the universality of Multiple Descents under sub-Gaussian designs and in the high-dimensional asymptotic limit. Moreover, the interval in which Multiple Descents occurs is characterized by
\omega_{d}(d^{\iota})\leq N\leq o_{d}(d^{\iota+1})
, which improves upon the results of
[
LRZ20
]
and
[
GMMM21
]
. Furthermore, we do not require the assumptions
\mathbb{E}h_{d}(X)=\mathbb{E}f_{d}^{*}(X)=0
as in
[
GMMM21
]
.

2.

When
L=2
, Proposition
9
provides evidence that the upper bound of the absolute value of
\|\hat{f}_{0}-f^{*}\|_{L_{2}}-\|\Gamma_{>1}^{1/2}f_{>1}^{*}\|_{{\cal H}}
exhibits a descent when
d\lesssim N\lesssim d^{2}
. When
d,N\to\infty
with
\omega(d)\leq N\leq o(d^{2})
, Proposition
9
provides sharp asymptotics, not just an upper bound, and this descent being referred to as the second descent within the context of the “double descents” phenomenon, as demonstrated in
[
MM20
]
. Nevertheless, it is important to highlight the superiority of Proposition
9
in comparison to the findings presented in
[
MM20
]
:

(a)

The underlying assumption in our analysis is that of a sub-Gaussian design. However, it is worth noting that in the work by
[
MM20
]
, they need the random variable
X
to be uniformly distributed over
\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1}
, which is more restrictive and unrealistic.

(b)

In
[
MM20
]
, it is demonstrated that KRR defined by the “random feature kernel” exhibits the double descents phenomenon, whereas what we establish here is the double descents phenomenon for KRR defined by the polynomial kernel. We emphasize that our approach is also applicable to the random feature kernel (even data-dependent kernels), as seen in Proposition
10
, but we leave this for future work.

3.

[
GMMM21
,
MMM22
]
proved that when
X
is uniformly distributed over
\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1}
and under some assumptions on
h
,
\|\hat{f}_{0}-f_{d}^{*}\|_{L_{2}}-\left\|P_{>\iota}f_{d}^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}
is infinitesimal compared to
\left\|f_{d}^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}+\sigma_{\xi}
when
\omega_{d}\left(d^{\iota}\log{d}\right)\leq N\leq O_{d}\left(d^{\iota+1-\delta_{0}}\right)
when
N,d\to\infty
. This result is recently improved in the asymptotic regime. In fact,
[
Mis22
]
proved that in asymptotic regime, that is, when both
N,d\to\infty
and when
X
is uniformly distributed over
\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1}
and under some assumptions on
h
and when
f_{d}^{*}
satisfies a kind of randomness property, the Multiple Descents happens when
d^{\iota}\lesssim N\lesssim d^{\iota+1}
for each
0\leq\iota\leq L
. We place significant emphasis on the superiority of our non-asymptotic results, particularly in the context of the “fixed
d
” situation, as it allows us to avoid the ambiguity that arises from altering
f^{*}
and
{\cal H}
as
d
grows, as indicated by Lemma
2
and the follow-up discussion.

4.

We unify the two theories developed by
[
GMMM21
,
MM20
,
MMM22
,
Mis22
]
and
[
LRZ20
]
, whose proof methods are very different. Proposition
9
indicates that they are in fact of the same nature. This is believed to be a challenging task
[
DWY21
]
. This proves that the geometrical viewpoint underlying our approach captures the phenomenon of Multiple Descents appearing in different scenarii that needed before specific tools.

5.

We have only provided results for
L<\infty
, while in the case where
h
is a smooth function, it is necessary to investigate how to approximate it using finite-degree polynomials. Specifically, if we denote
{\mathbb{K}}^{[\leq L]}_{N}=(K^{[\leq L]}(X_{i},X_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
as the kernel matrix defined by the kernel function
K^{[\leq L]}({\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y})=h^{[\leq L]}(\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y}\rangle/d)
, where
h^{[\leq L]}
is a polynomial of degree
L
, then to prove Multiple Descents, we need to examine whether
\|{\mathbb{K}}_{N}-{\mathbb{K}}_{N}^{[\leq L]}\|_{\text{op}}\to 0
as
L,d,N\to\infty
, where
{\mathbb{K}}_{N}
is the kernel matrix defined by
K
. Since this paper primarily focuses on non-asymptotic theory, this issue is beyond the scope of our current work.

4.2
Estimation error of KRR with smooth kernel in the non-asymptotic regime

In Section
4.1
, we considered the polynomial kernels with finite degree, that is, when there exists
h:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
being a finite-degree polynomial function such that for any
{\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
K({\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y})=h\left(\left\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle/d\right)
. One may question whether our general bounds are limited to these specific kernels. In this section, we will demonstrate that this is not the case.
In this section, we study the upper bound for the estimation error of KRR under the assumption that the kernel function
K
is smooth in the non-asymptotic regime. In
[
BM19
,
HHvLS23
]
, it is proven that when
X
is uniform over the Euclidean sphere, the eigenvalues of NTK have a power decay. Motivated by this observation, in this section, we grant the following assumption:

Assumption 4
.

•

X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is distributed uniformly over
\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1}
, the noise
\xi
is distributed according to a centered Gaussian variable with variance
\sigma_{\xi}^{2}
and is independent of
X
’s.

•

h\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
,
K({\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y})=h(\left\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle/d)
for any
{\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and
\left\|K\right\|_{L_{\infty}(\mu)}<\infty
. Moreover,
h(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}t^{i}
with
\alpha_{i}>0
for any
i
.

•

For any
j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
,
\sigma_{j}\sim j^{-\alpha}
for some
\alpha>1
3
3

3

We allow
\alpha
to depend on
d
, for example,
\alpha=\frac{2r}{d}
when
\mathcal{H}
is an
r
-th order Sobolev space (where
r>d/2
), as discussed in
[
Bac24
, Chapter 7]
.
.

We further grant the source condition and the embedding index condition. The source condition is often assumed in the context of inverse problems and in the study of RKHS from the perspective of classical integral operators, and is often verified in application, for example,
[
PVB23
, Section 4.2]
. It is employed to provide insights into the “smoothness” of the unknown function
f^{*}
, as seen in, for instance,
[
BPR07
, Equation 11]
,
[
BM16
, section 2]
and
[
ZLL23
]
. We assume the following assumption holds:

Assumption 5
.

There exist
s\geq 1
and a sequence
(a_{j})_{j\in{\mathbb{N}}}\in\ell_{2}
such that
f^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}a_{j}\sigma_{j}^{1/2}f_{j}
and
\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{j}^{1-s}a_{j}^{2}<\infty
.

The relationship between Assumption
4
and the source condition in the language of interpolation spaces can be found in Section
5.3
.

The embedding index condition in the study of RKHS and spectral algorithms, to the best of our knowledge, originated from
[
MN10
]
and was later refined by
[
SHS09
,
FS20
]
and others. Specifically, we make the assumption that there exist absolute constants
C_{\ref*{C_embedding}}
and
0<{\theta_{\ref*{theta_embedding}}}<1
such that for any
f\in{\cal H}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{L_{\infty}}\leq C_{\ref*{C_embedding}}\left\|f\right\|_{\cal H}^{{\theta_{\ref*{theta_embedding}}}}\left\|f\right\|_{L_{2}}^{1-{\theta_{\ref*{theta_embedding}}}}.

(17)

Under Assumption
4
, it is proven that
{\theta_{\ref*{theta_embedding}}}
can be taken to be
1/\alpha
,
[
MN10
]
.

Proposition 5
.

Suppose that Assumption
4
, Assumption
5
and (
17
) hold.
If
(s\wedge 2)\alpha>1
, then for
k=N^{\frac{1}{1+(s\wedge 2)\alpha}}
and
\lambda\sim N\sigma_{k}
, with constant probability,

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{f}_{\lambda}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}\lesssim_{\sigma_{\xi}}\log^{3}(N)N^{-\frac{\alpha(s\wedge 2)}{2(1+\alpha(s\wedge 2))}}.

(18)

Proposition
5
is an unpublished argument by Zhifan Li
[
Li
]
. The authors would like to express their gratitude to Zhifan Li for allowing them to cite his conclusion. The proof of Proposition
5
can be found in Section
6.5.5
.

Some comments are in order.

•

Up to logarithmic factors, Proposition
5
achieves the minimax optimal rate established in
[
CDV07
]
under the source condition and embedding index conditions.
[
MR22
]
also provides a general upper bound on the estimation error of KRR. However, their results fall short of achieving the minimax optimal rate, as discussed in
[
MR22
, Section 3]
. Therefore, our general bounds are more accurate compared to that of
[
MR22
]
.

•

As
\alpha
increases,
{\cal H}
contains increasingly smoother functions. Similarly, as
s
increases, the smoothness of the optimal function
f^{*}
also increases, resulting in a faster convergence rate. When
s\geq 2
, KRR is unable to further capture the smoothness of
f^{*}
, a phenomenon referred to as the saturation effect,
[
BPR07
]
. Our geometric perspective further indicates that the saturation effect occurs within the space
{\cal H}_{1:k}
.

•

Additionally, we highlight that, due to the geometric nature of our general bounds, we can clearly demonstrate that for any sufficiently large
N
,
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
captures a degree
\lfloor\frac{1}{1+(s\wedge 2)\alpha}\frac{\log{N}}{\log{d}}\rfloor
-polynomial approximation of
f^{*}
while treating the higher-order terms as noise.
[
DWY21
]
proved that for a more general rotational invariant kernel,
f^{*}
can be approximated by a polynomial of degree
\lfloor 2\frac{\log{N}}{\log{d}}\rfloor
, while
[
GMMM21
]
provided a result for degree
\lfloor\frac{\log{N}}{\log{d}}\rfloor
. Their results are asymptotic. In contrast, our conclusion is not only non-asymptotic, but also requires lower-degree polynomials to approximate
f^{*}
. Our results are not contradictory to
[
GMMM21
]
because we assume eigenvalue decay and the source condition of
f^{*}
. This implies that
f^{*}
is smoother under these conditions, hence it can be approximated by lower-degree polynomials. Moreover, to our knowledge, we are the first to demonstrate how the eigenvalue decay and the source condition of
f^{*}
affect the degree of polynomial approximation.

The polynomial barrier

In a rough sense, the polynomial barrier phenomenon asserts that in the power regime where
N\sim d^{\iota}
for some
\iota\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
and as both
d
and
N
approach infinity, regardless of the choice of
\lambda
,
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
can only approximate a degree-
\iota
polynomial approximation of
f^{*}
,
[
DWY21
]
.
We note that, as discussed in Section
6.5.12
in supplementary material,
{\cal H}_{1:k}\subset\oplus_{l\in[k]}^{\perp}V_{d,l}
for some spaces
V_{d,l}
spanned by spherical harmonics of dimension
d
and whose degree is less than
l
with
\dim(V_{d,l})\sim d^{l}
. Since
\mathcal{H}_{1:k}
is
k
-dimensional, it is contained in the space spanned by
\log_{d}(k)
order spherical harmonics. Hence, our choice of
k=N^{\frac{1}{1+(s\wedge 2)\alpha}}
indicates that
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
with optimal choice of
\lambda
approximates
f_{1:N^{\frac{1}{1+(s\wedge 2)\alpha}}}^{*}
and treats
f_{N^{\frac{1}{1+(s\wedge 2)\alpha}}+1:\infty}^{*}
as part of the noise, which corresponds to approximating
f^{*}
as a degree-
\lfloor\frac{1}{1+(s\wedge 2)\alpha}\frac{\log{N}}{\log{d}}\rfloor
polynomial, with the remaining higher-order terms considered as noise.

Breaking the polynomial barrier is a good motivation for studying non-rotationally invariant kernels. For instance, one such kernel function is the conjugate kernel employed in the context of feature learning, as discussed in
[
BES+22
,
MLHD23
]
, see also Section
4.4
. We leave this question opens even though we have no doubt that Theorem
3
and
6
could be useful tools to answer this question.

4.3
The Gaussian Equivalence Property

Up to this point, we apply our general bounds to some specific choices of kernels such as polynomial or smooth ones. However, as mentioned in Section
2.1
, “readers unfamiliar with RKHS theory can think of the feature map
\phi(X)
as a Gaussian random vector, even though their nature is fundamentally different.” One might have noticed a phenomenon: when treating
\phi(X)
as a centered Gaussian random vector with covariance operator
\Gamma
and calculating the estimation error by substituting it into Theorem
3
and Theorem
6
, the results seem to be almost identical to what we obtained through our “non-Gaussian” theory. This observation is known as the Gaussian equivalence property.

In order to prove such a property, we first need a sharp, i.e. matching upper and lower bounds for KRR in the Gaussian setup. In order to do so, we apply Theorem
3
to
\phi:x\mapsto x
and
X
is a centered Gaussian random vector with covariance operator
\Gamma
. This provides an upper bound that is given in the result below. For the matching lower bound (which is also presented in this result), see Section
5.3
in supplementary material for its formal version; its proof can be found in Section
6.5.7
in supplementary material.

Proposition
12
(informal)
.

For the Gaussian linear regression problem, if we choose
k
as
k_{b,\lambda}^{*}=\min\left(k\in{\mathbb{N}}:\,\frac{\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}{\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}}\geq bN\right)
, then, when
r_{\lambda,k_{b,\lambda}^{*}}^{*}\lesssim N
, we have
\mathbb{E}\|\Gamma^{1/2}(\hat{f}_{\lambda}-f^{*})\|_{2}\gtrsim r_{\lambda,k_{b,\lambda}^{*}}^{*}
and with high probability, we have
\|\Gamma^{1/2}(\hat{f}_{\lambda}-f^{*})\|_{2}\lesssim r_{\lambda,k_{b,\lambda}^{*}}^{*}
.

From Proposition
12
we can see that the estimation error of KRR in the linear Gaussian case is (up to a multiplicative constant) equivalent to that of Theorem
3
, i.e., of KRR for a large class of kernels and design vectors. It therefore shows the Gaussian equivalence property: we can indeed ’replace’
\phi(X)
by a centered Gaussian vector with covariance operator identical to the integral operator of
\phi(X)
, i.e.,
\Gamma
.

This is a counter-intuitive and practical property that should hold in the non-asymptotic regime, called the (one-sided)
Gaussian Equivalent Property
that we can formalize in the following definition.

Definition 1
.

Let
K
be a kernel on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
(with associated RKHS
{\cal H}
and feature map
\phi
),
X
be a random variable with values in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,
\xi
be a real valued random variable independent of
X
and
Y=\bigl<f^{*},\phi(X)\bigr>_{\cal H}+\xi
for some
f^{*}\in{\cal H}
. We say that the triple
(K,X,\xi)
satisfies the (one-sided)
Gaussian equivalence property (GEP)
when the convergence rate of the KRR constructed from
N
i.i.d. copies of
(X,Y)
and with regularization parameter
\lambda
is smaller than
r_{\lambda,k_{b,\lambda}^{*}}^{*}
, up to some absolute multiplicative constant, for
b\sim\max\{C_{\ref*{C_lower_kappa_DM}}\kappa_{DM}^{-1},2c_{\ref*{c_lower_7}}^{-1}\}
where
k_{b,\lambda}^{*}
is defined in (
26
) and
r^{*}_{\lambda,k}
is defined in (
31
).

Remark 3
.

1.

Our Definition
1
only provides that if we replace the feature map with a Gaussian random vector, the resulting excess risk can serve as an upper bound for the excess risk of the original problem. In
[
MS22
,
HL22
]
, the GEP is typically regarded as a two-sided bound. However, in this paper, we focus solely on the upper bound.

2.

We notice that in some papers, the requirements for the Gaussian Equivalent Conjecture are stricter; they necessitate the explicit construction of an operator
\Gamma_{0}
to approximate
\Gamma
(in the sense of operator norm), as seen in
[
MLHD23
,
BES+22
,
HL22
]
. This approach often involves a specific analysis of the kernel function, for example, the random feature kernel. In the next section, we aim to provide a more universally applicable method, so we only provide the following insight: for the conjugate kernel defined later, potentially data-dependent,
[
WZ23
, Lemma 5.2]
offers a way to construct such an approximating operator
\Gamma_{0}
.

This property was rigorously proven by
[
MS22
,
HL22
,
BES+22
]
but in the limited range of the proportional asymptotic regime and a Gaussian design assumption (i.e.
X
is assumed to be Gaussian). Given the inherent appeal and practical value of this property, natural questions arise:
does the Gaussian Equivalence Property hold in non-asymptotic scenarios? Or for sub-Gaussian designs?
We emphasize that the significance of non-asymptotic theory and power regimes in the context of deep learning theory has been thoroughly expounded upon in Section
4.1
. Thanks to Proposition
6
and Theorem
3
, we are in a position to show the (one-sided) Gaussian equivalence property as defined above for a large class of kernels, design vectors
X
and noises
\xi
.

Proposition 6
.

If Assumption
1
, Assumption
2
and Assumption
3
are valid,
(K,X,\xi)
satisfies the (one-sided) Gaussian Equivalence Property.

This finding is applicable in a broad range of kernels, extending to non-asymptotic scenarios, and allowing for general design vectors and noises. This represents a significant improvement compared to previous results
[
MS22
,
HL22
,
BES+22
]
. In particular, the non-asymptotic nature of Proposition
6
is important to us because the GEP may be seen as a ’non-asymptotic CLT’ for KRR which is one aspect of the ’surprising’ nature of this property.

4.4
Data-dependent conjugate kernel

In recent years, research in Deep Learning Theory has increasingly focused on data-dependent kernels due to the fact that rotationally invariant kernels exhibit a polynomial barrier,
[
GMMM21
,
DWY21
]
. One approach involves training a neural network on a subset of samples using gradient descent and using the obtained “representation feature” (which will be defined later) as a feature map to define a (data-dependent) kernel. The advantage of the associated RKHS lies in its data-dependent feature map, enabling the alignment of its eigenfunctions with
f^{*}
for a better approximation of
f^{*}
. This alignment can significantly reduce the estimation error and is sometimes call feature learning.

For instance,
[
BES+22
, section 4.3]
established that under certain conditions, performing a single, sufficiently large step in gradient descent followed by data-dependent kernel KRR, results in an estimation error smaller than that of any inner product kernel at any tuning parameter. Moreover, this alignment can also learn hidden subspaces, thereby mitigating the “curse of dimensionality”, as discussed in
[
DKL+23
,
DLS22
,
Bac17
]
. This field has seen rapid development in the recent years, as evidenced by papers such as
[
BES+22
,
DKL+23
,
DLS22
,
MLHD23
,
BAGJ21
,
BBSS22
,
BBPV23
]
. However, due to the complexity of these data-dependent kernels, especially their lack of rotational invariance and the absence of specific spectral decay, researchers in Deep Learning Theory often find it challenging to study the estimation error of KRR defined over these RKHS, resorting to rough bounds (
[
BES+22
, Theorem 11]
,
[
DLS22
,
MHPG+23
,
BBSS22
]
) that does not lead to benign overfitting, or limited investigation in asymptotic regimes
[
BES+22
]
, or they may have to assume the validity of a certain Gaussian Equivalent Conjecture,
[
MLHD23
,
DKL+23
]
. This poses difficulties for the development of DNN approximation and feature learning via data-dependent kernels. In this section, we apply Theorem
3
to such data-dependent kernels to obtain upper bounds on the estimation error of the associated KRR estimator
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
.

In this section, we are concerned with a fully-connected shallow neural network with
m
neurons (for deep neural networks, we can similarly define its conjugate kernel),

\displaystyle f:{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\left\langle{\boldsymbol{w}},\sigma\left(W{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)\right\rangle_{\ell_{2}^{m}}\in{\mathbb{R}},

(19)

where
{\boldsymbol{w}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
,
W\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times d}
, and
\sigma:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is the non-linearity, applied coordinate-wisely, that is,
\sigma(W\cdot)=(\sigma(\left\langle W_{j},\cdot\right\rangle_{\ell_{2}^{d}}))_{j\in[m]}
, where
(W_{i})
’s are row vectors of
W
. The RKHS
{\cal H}
generated by the feature map
\phi:{\boldsymbol{x}}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sigma\left(W{\boldsymbol{x}}\right)
is called the conjugate kernel of shallow neural network with non-linearity
\sigma
and first layer
W
.
We identify
{\cal H}
with
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
and
\phi
with
m^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sigma(\left<W_{j},{\boldsymbol{x}}\right>){\boldsymbol{e}}_{j}
where
({\boldsymbol{e}}_{j})_{j=1}^{m}
is the canonical basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
.
In other words,
{\cal H}=\{f_{\boldsymbol{w}}(\cdot)=\left<{\boldsymbol{w}},\phi(\cdot)\right>:{\boldsymbol{w}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}\}
is composed of all shallow neural networks with
\sigma
as the activation function and
W
as the weight matrix of the first layer and each
f_{\boldsymbol{w}}
is identified by
{\boldsymbol{w}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
. At this point,
\Gamma={\mathbb{E}}[\phi(X)\otimes\phi(X)]
is identified as a linear operator on
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and its eigenfunctions are identified as the vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
given by
\varphi_{j}
.
Using the spectral decomposition of
\Gamma
, there exists a unitary operator
U=[\varphi_{1}|\varphi_{2}|\cdots]
such that for any
j\in[m]
,
\varphi_{j}=U{\boldsymbol{e}}_{j}
.

As the analysis of KRR depends on the decomposition of
{\cal H}
into two orthogonal subspaces
{\cal H}_{1:k}\oplus^{\perp}{\cal H}_{k+1:m}
for some
k\in[m]
, we need to introduce the decomposition of
\phi
. Recall that
{\cal H}_{1:k}=\Span(\varphi_{j}:1\leq j\leq k)
, we rewrite
\phi({\boldsymbol{x}})
as
\phi({\boldsymbol{x}})=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sigma\left(\left\langle W_{j},{\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle\right){\boldsymbol{e}}_{j}=\sum_{j\in J}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sigma\left(\left\langle W_{j},{\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle\right){\boldsymbol{e}}_{j}+\sum_{j\in J^{c}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sigma\left(\left\langle W_{j},{\boldsymbol{x}}\right\rangle\right){\boldsymbol{e}}_{j}=:\phi_{J}({\boldsymbol{x}})+\phi_{J^{c}}({\boldsymbol{x}})
for
J=[k]
and
\phi_{J}=P_{{\cal H}_{1:k}}\phi
and
\phi_{J^{c}}=P_{{\cal H}_{k+1:m}}\phi
. Then
{\cal H}_{1:k}=U\Span({\boldsymbol{e}}_{j}:j\in J)
and
{\cal H}_{k+1:m}=U\Span({\boldsymbol{e}}_{j}:j\in J^{c})
. One can check that they are orthogonal spaces.
We write the restriction of
W
onto
J
as
W_{J}
, that is, the
j
-th row of
W_{J}
, denoted as
(W_{J})_{j}
, equals
W_{j}
, if
j\in J
, and
(W_{J})_{j}=0
, if
j\in J^{c}
. Intuitively, this can be understood as using the neurons associated with the subset
J
to estimate
f^{*}
, while the neurons corresponding to
J^{c}
are utilized to absorb noise.

Given the samples
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i\in[N_{1}+N_{2}]}
for some
N_{1},N_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, we use
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i\in[N_{1}]}
to perform gradient descent with respect to the squared loss and with step size
\eta\sqrt{m}
for training the weight matrix
W
.
After the completion of this gradient descent, we employ
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{N_{1}+1\leq i\leq N_{1}+N_{2}}
to perform KRR on the obtained conjugate kernel with non-linearity
\sigma
and first layer
W
obtained by gradient descent in the previous step – so that
W
depends on
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i=1}^{N_{1}}
. We call this RKHS
data-dependent conjugate kernel
. Due to the independence of the samples, in the second stage, we can treat
W
as a deterministic matrix (though
W
has undergone alignment with respect to
f^{*}
through gradient descent).
In the remaining part of this section, we always take condition on random vectors
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i\in[N_{1}]}
(that is, trained
W
) because we want to study the features learning property of gradient descent. Hence, we assume that the gradient descent step is finished and we then want to study the features that have been learned during that step. Before introducing our results, we emphasize that through similar analysis, our results can be extended to neural networks with bias, or even deep neural networks. Here, we only present the simplest case.
In this section, we acknowledge the following assumption.

Assumption 6
.

There exist
B,D>0
depending on the gradient descent procedure, such that the following holds.

1.

For each
i\in[N_{1}+N_{2}]
,
X_{i}
are i.i.d.
{\cal N}(0,I_{d})
, the noise
\xi
is independent of
X
with mean zero and variance
\sigma_{\xi}^{2}
. We assume that there exist absolute constants
{\kappa_{\ref*{kappa_noise}}}>0
and
r>4
such that
\left\|\xi\right\|_{L_{r}}\leq{\kappa_{\ref*{kappa_noise}}}\sigma_{\xi}
.

2.

\sigma
is Lipschitz on
{\mathbb{R}}
with Lipschitz constant
\left\|\sigma\right\|_{Lip}
. Moreover,
\sup_{j\in[m]}\left\|\sigma(\left\langle W_{j},\cdot\right\rangle)\right\|_{L_{2}}<D
.

3.

The first layer
W
after gradient descent satisfies the following equations: for any
i,j\in J^{c}
,
\left<W_{i},W_{j}\right>=\delta_{ij}
,
\left\|W_{J}\right\|_{\text{op}}\leq B
and
\sum_{j\in J}(\left\|W_{j}\right\|_{2}^{2}-1)^{2}\leq B^{2}
.

4.

The choice of
k
for
k\lesssim N_{2}
and tuning parameter
\lambda\geq 0
satisfies the following equations (we recall that
\Gamma={\mathbb{E}}\phi(X)\phi(X)^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times m}
):

(a)

m\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:m}^{2}\right)\gtrsim\lambda_{\sigma}^{2}\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:m}\right\|_{\text{op}}
,

(b)

(\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:m}\right)+\lambda)\left\|\sigma\right\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}^{2}\gtrsim\lambda_{\sigma}^{2}N_{2}\|\Gamma_{k+1:m}\|_{\text{op}}
,

(c)

m\sigma_{k}\sqrt{N_{2}}\geq 2\lambda_{\sigma}^{2}B^{2}
.

Points
1.
and
2.
in the context of feature learning and conjugate kernel research are the most common settings
[
WZ23
,
FW20
,
MMM22
]
. When
X
has covariance matrix
\Sigma
, the hidden layer matrix
W
can be replaced by
W\Sigma^{1/2}
. For a general probability distribution, such as the sub-Gaussian distribution, refer to Remark
6
in the supplementary material. Points
3.
and
4.
, on the other hand, have been relatively rare in prior studies, primarily because we characterize which subspace of the RKHS absorbs noise when using
\hat{f}_{\lambda}
, a perspective that previous work did not investigate (except
[
BES+22
, Appendix D]
). In
3.
, there is a partitioning of
[m]
. Neurons belonging to
J
are used for prediction, while neurons belonging to
J^{c}
are used to absorb noise. In many cases,
\|W_{j}\|_{2}=1
holds for all
j\in[m]
, for example, when using Stiefel gradient flow to learn the hidden layer (in this case, the hidden layer matrix
W
consists of orthonormal row vectors of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
), as seen in
[
BBPV23
]
. This model yields a typical weight matrix produced by gradient descent reported in many works, such as
[
BF24
]
and references therein.
However, upper bounds on
\|W_{J}\|_{\text{op}}
and
\sum_{j\in J}(\|W_{j}\|_{2}^{2}-1)^{2}
as well as verifying condition
4.
require a more detailed examination of the optimization algorithm, particularly with regard to the feature learning it induces. We provide some examples when the spectrum and eigenvectors of
\Gamma
may be obtained in Section
6.5.9
in supplementary material. We assume
k\lesssim N_{2}
for simplicity.
We provide an example satisfying Assumption
6
in Proposition
11
after the statement on the performance of KRR for this data-dependent kernel learned by gradient descent that follows from our general bounds.

Proposition
10
(informal)
.

Grant Assumption
6
. If
k\lesssim N
, for any
\lambda\geq 0
, the same conclusions as in Theorem
3
hold.

The formal version of Proposition
10
may be found in Section
5.3.6
in supplementary material, and the proof of Proposition
10
can be found in Section
6.5.8
in the supplementary material. As far as we know, Proposition
10
is the first result on the estimation error of KRR on data-dependent conjugate kernels.

Below, we provide a toy example to validate Assumption
6
and utilize this example to illustrate how gradient flow performs feature learning.

Proposition
11
(informal)
.

Under certain conditions, there exists a learning algorithm such that after training the first layer with
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i=1}^{N_{1}}
, minimum data-dependent conjugate RKHS norm interpolant estimator applied to the samples
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N_{1}+N_{2}}
yields
\hat{f}_{0}
that satisfies, with probability at least
1/2
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left\|\hat{f}_{0}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}&\lesssim\left|a^{*}\right|\left\|\sigma\right\|_{Lip}\tilde{O}\left(\max\left\{\sqrt{\frac{d+m}{N_{1}}},\frac{d^{2}}{N_{1}}\right\}\right)+\frac{\sigma_{\xi}}{\sqrt{N_{2}}}+\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{\frac{N_{2}}{m}}+\frac{\left|a^{*}\right|}{\|\sigma\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}}\frac{1}{N_{2}}\end{aligned}

(20)

in the single neuron model, i.e. when
f^{*}:x\to a^{*}\sigma(\bigl<w^{*},x\bigr>)
for
a^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
w^{*}\in S_{2}^{d-1}
.

The formal version of Proposition
11
may be found in Section
5.3
in supplementary material and the proof of it may be found in Section
6.5.10
. Some comments are in order.

We utilize the conclusions of feature learning provided in
[
BBSS22
]
. The probability deviation
1/2
in Proposition
11
arises from the special “random feature approximation” technique utilized in
[
BBSS22
]
, as discussed in
[
BBSS22
, Appendix D.4]
. Since feature learning is beyond the scope of this paper, we directly rely on the conclusions from
[
BBSS22
]
instead of attempting to improve upon them.
In Proposition
11
, the excess risk is the approximation error (the first term in the right-hand-side of Equation (
20
)) plus estimation error (other terms). The latter term becomes very small when the layer width
m
is sufficiently large relative to the number of samples
N_{2}
used to train the second layer. In
[
BBSS22
, Section 6]
, the authors conjecture that
\sqrt{d+m}
can be improved to
\sqrt{d}
. If this conjecture holds true, then in our Proposition
11
, after learning the hidden layer through gradient descent, the excess risk is decreasing with the layer width
m
. This implies that even when the parameters of the shallow neural network are numerous (indicating a large layer width
m
), resulting in small approximation error for the shallow neural network, the estimation error obtained through stochastic gradient descent (where feature learning happens) and the minimum data-dependent conjugate RKHS norm interpolant estimator remains small. This contradicts the intuition of the classical approximation-estimation trade-off. However, we emphasize that a large value of
m
will lead to computational difficulties.

.

and Assumption

.

and

.

.

5.1
Linearization of non-linear kernel matrix.

Despite Theorem
2
being of a non-asymptotic nature, one can question the insights that Theorem
2
can provide in the asymptotic regime.
A series of papers
[
EK10
,
DV13
,
CS13
,
FM19
]
investigated the spectral properties of
(K_{k+1:\infty}(X_{i},X_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
as the dimension
d
(recall that
K_{k+1:\infty}
is a kernel defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
) and number of samples
N
tend to infinity, while maintaining the ratio
d/N
to be a constant level. As
(K_{k+1:\infty}(X_{i},X_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}={\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}{\mathbb{X}}_{\phi,k+1:\infty}^{\top}
, our Theorem
2
also produces results on the spectrum of
(K_{k+1:\infty}(X_{i},X_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
. The reader may find it of interest to compare Theorem
2
with the theorems presented in the works of
[
EK10
,
DV13
,
CS13
,
FM19
]
. In the following, we consider a general
(K(X_{i},X_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
instead of
(K_{k+1:\infty}(X_{i},X_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
. The following theorem is the main result of
[
EK10
]
applied to isotropic design vector.

Theorem 5

(
[
EK10
]
)
.

Assume that there exist some absolute constants
c,C
such that
c\leq d/N\leq C
and
(X_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
are i.i.d. isotropic random vectors. Assume the coordinates of
X_{i}
are
(x_{ij})_{j\leq d}
, and assume that
x_{ij}
’s have mean
0
, variance
1
and
(4+\varepsilon)
moment for some
\varepsilon>0
. Let
K
be an inner product kernel as in (
16
) with
h:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a
C^{1}
function in a neighborhood of
1
and a
C^{3}
function in a neighborhood of
0
. There exists a matrix
M
defined as

\displaystyle M=\left(h(0)+h^{\prime\prime}(0)\frac{1}{2d}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{N}\mathbbm{1}_{N}^{\top}+\frac{h^{\prime}(0)}{d}{\mathbb{X}}{\mathbb{X}}^{\top}+\left(h(1)-h(0)-h^{\prime}(0)\right)I_{N},

where
{\mathbb{X}}
is the design matrix
[X_{1}|X_{2}|\cdots|X_{N}]^{\top}
, such that as
N,d\to\infty
while
d/N
remain bounded, we have
\left\|\left(K(X_{i},X_{j})\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq N}-M\right\|_{\text{op}}\to 0
in probability.

Theorem
5
suggests that when both
N
and
d
tend to infinity, while
d/N
remains at a constant level, the spectrum of the kernel matrix
(K(X_{i},X_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
can be approximated by that of a linearized kernel, referred to as the linearization of the kernel matrix with non-linear kernel. The regularity of
h
in Theorem
5
is relaxed by
[
CS13
]
to
C^{1}
near
0
and by
[
DV13
]
to be differentiable at
0
and continuous at
1
.
The results from
[
CS13
,
FM19
]
demand
X
to be a Gaussian random vector. Moreover,
[
EK10
, Theorem 2.1]
establishes convergence in probability, while
[
CS13
,
DV13
,
FM19
]
only achieve convergence in distribution using the Stieltjes transform or Free Probability techniques.

One might ask the question: what does the linearization of non-linear kernel random matrices entail? From a statistical perspective, linearization offers a means to analyze KRR through a kernel matrix of a linear kernel. By examining the spectrum of the limit matrix
M
defined in Theorem
5
, and applying Weyl’s inequality, we can gain insights into the spectrum of the kernel matrix. This, in turn, allows us to determine the estimation error of KRR. In fact, this approach is commonly adopted in many works within the field of Deep Learning theory,
[
LR20
,
HMRT22
]
.

However, as we have observed in Section
4.1
, Theorem
5
only holds in the proportional regime, specifically when
d\sim N
. This limitation implies that we cannot observe 1) the multiple descent phenomenon through Theorem
5
and 2) the higher degree approximation of
f^{*}
that KRR performs – an important property of deep neural networks . Natural questions therefore arise: can we encounter the linearization of kernel matrices with non-linear kernel beyond the proportional regime? can we get quantitative (i.e. non-asymptotic) results concerning this approximation?

Our subsequent conclusions reveals that although we have not been able to establish the approximation of a kernel matrix
(K(X_{i},X_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
by the kernel matrix of a linearized kernel in the topology generated by the operator norm as in Theorem
5
, we have demonstrated that the spectrum of the kernel matrix converges in probability to a considerably smaller region (this convergence pertains to the topology on
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
).
However, given the interest in the theoretical foundations of deep learning community in results such as Theorem
5
(despite its non-quantitative and proportional regimes range of applications), we expect that these findings will prove to be useful in future research within the domain of statistical deep learning Theory, for instance,
[
DWY21
]
.

The following is the assumption that will be granted in this section.

Assumption 7
.

1.

N,d\to\infty
, while
d^{\iota}=o(N)
,
N=o(d^{\iota+1})
for some
\iota\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
.

2.

X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is a mean-zero random vector with i.i.d. sub-Gaussian coordinates. Moreover, for any finite set
S
, the first coordinate (denoted as
x_{1}
) of
X
satisfies
{\mathbb{P}}(x_{1}\in S)<1
and
{\mathbb{P}}(x_{1}=0)>0
. We denote
\mu_{1}
as the distribution of
x_{1}
, thus
\mu=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mu_{1}
.

3.

For some
\iota,L\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, there exists a function
h:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\sum_{i=\iota}^{L}\alpha_{i}t^{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
for some
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
\alpha_{i}\sim 1
for all
i\in[L]
, such that (
16
) holds.

The following proposition is derived from a combination of Theorem
2
and Lemma
22
.

Proposition 7
.

Suppose Assumption
7
holds. There exist absolute constant
{c_{6}}
such that the following holds. Then

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} {\mathbb{P}}\bigg\{&(1-c_{\ref*{c_distortion_asymptotic}})h(1)\leq\sigma_{N}\left({(K(X_{i},X_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}}\right)\leq\\
&\sigma_{1}\left({(K(X_{i},X_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}}\right)\leq(1+c_{\ref*{c_distortion_asymptotic}})h(1)\bigg\}\to 1\end{aligned}

(21)

as
N,d\to\infty
, where we recall that
\sigma_{N}(A)
and
\sigma_{1}(A)
represents the smallest and the largest singular value of some matrix
A
.

The proof of Proposition
7
can be found in Section
6.5.3
. We now provide some comments on Proposition
7
.

1.

Theorem
5
and Proposition
7
both characterize the properties of the spectrum of the kernel matrix as
d
and
N
approach infinity. However, these two conclusions exhibit several differences. Firstly, Theorem
5
states that the kernel matrix can be approximated by a matrix in the operator norm on
({\mathbb{R}}^{N},\ell_{2})\to({\mathbb{R}}^{N},\ell_{2})
, but it does not provide information about whether the condition number of the kernel matrix is bounded. In fact, if one uses the conclusion of Theorem
5
, further analysis of the spectrum of the matrix
M
is necessary. On the other hand, Proposition
7
directly indicates that the condition number of the kernel matrix is nearly 1, and all eigenvalues are concentrated around
h(1)
. This type of results are useful for the analysis of KRR via the geometric method.

2.

Furthermore, Proposition
7
holds beyond the proportional regime
N\sim d
in the power regime
N\sim d^{\iota}
, whereas Theorem
5
only applies to the proportional regime. Even if we set
\iota=1
, the range of validity of Proposition
7
is approximately
d\lesssim N\lesssim d^{2}
, which is notably broader than the range of validity of Theorem
5
(which is
N\sim d
).

3.

However, it must be emphasized that the aforementioned advantages of Proposition
7
come at a cost. It’s worth noting that Proposition
7
is applicable only to finite-degree polynomial kernels, and the Taylor coefficients of the function
h
must be positive. In contrast, Theorem
5
has a much broader range of applicability regarding the choice of kernels. One may wish to extend Proposition
7
using the Stone-Weierstrass theorem; however, this is not feasible. This is because in Proposition
7
, the constants associated with the condition
d^{\iota}\lesssim N\lesssim d^{\iota+1}
conceal information about the Taylor coefficients of
h
. Readers can verify for themselves that when
\iota=1
, we require
N\leq 4\frac{\sum_{i\leq L}\alpha_{i}}{a_{1}}\kappa_{DM}d
. We conjecture that methods similar to those in
[
LY23
,
DLMY23
]
may help extend Proposition
7
to smooth kernel functions.

4.

One may observe that Proposition
7
aligns with the “diagonal” component as discussed in
[
EK10
, section 2.2 (B)]
. By “diagonal components,” we are referring to the elements along the diagonal of the kernel matrix
(K(X_{i},X_{j}))_{1\leq i,j\leq N}
. One may notice that this precisely corresponds to (
6
) in Assumption
1
.
This phenomenon occurs in the Dvoretzky-Milman regime, specifically when the value of
N
is less than
\kappa_{DM}d_{0}^{*}
. In this regime, the off-diagonal or cross terms are overshadowed by the diagonal part. This observation is also evident in the proof of Theorem
2
. The aforementioned phenomena suggests that the Dvoretzky-Milman theorem serves as a characterization of the “diagonal dominance” in the context of asymptotic non-linear random matrix theory. This observation has not been discerned using the Stieltjes transform or Free Probability approaches.

5.2
Further work

Beyond the rotational invariant kernel

In Section
4.4
, we provide an upper bound on the estimation error of KRR on data-dependent conjugate kernels and a toy example. However, for more general cases, such as the multi-index model, computing the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of
\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}
remains a challenge.
An intriguing avenue of research involves investigating the approximation and estimation error of KRR with the kernel discussed above, specifically when the weight matrix
W
is obtained from one
[
BES+22
,
DLS22
,
MLHD23
,
BES+23
]
or many gradient steps
[
BBPV23
,
MHPG+23
]
. The objective is to understand how the data-dependent kernel aligns with the target function and how its spectrum and eigenvectors change, ultimately leading to a reduction in the estimation error even when overfitting happens (
\lambda=0
) or near overfitting happens (
\lambda
is close to
0
), especially when the width of the neural network exceeds the sample size. In a word, we would like to understand the feature learning for this kernel after some gradient descent steps.

The Dvoretzky-Milman theorem generated by an RKHS feature map

In order to satisfy the classical Dvoretzky-Milman criterion, Theorem
2
requires
\epsilon
to be greater than 2 if one wants to avoid the extra assumption (
8
). A question that poses a significant math challenge is the appropriate application of the coloring technique proposed in the work of
[
Tik18
]
in order to enhance the value of
\epsilon
from being greater than 2 to
\epsilon>0
.

Point-wise lower bound

The lower bound proven in Proposition
12
for the linear Gaussian case holds for every target function, hence it is referred to as a “point-wise” lower bound, and sometimes also known as an optimistic lower bound (in contrast to the minimax lower bound),
[
Bel17
,
KR21
]
.

A challenging problem is to obtain a point-wise lower bound for the estimation error of a general KRR. In this context, we aim to derive results that do not rely on the spectrum of the RKHS (such as power decay) or specific properties of the kernel function (e.g., inner-product or translation-invariant kernels).

5.3
The parts omitted in the main text

Upper bound of the estimation error of KRR when
k
is not necessarily smaller than
N

In this section, we introduce the formal version of Theorem

.

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\sum_{j\in J_{2}}\sigma_{j}\leq\kappa_{DM}(4\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right))\left(1-\frac{\left|J_{1}\right|}{N}\right),&\mbox{ when }N\sigma_{1}\geq\kappa_{DM}(4\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right))\\

(22)

we have

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{f}_{\lambda}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}\leq C_{\ref*{C_rate_2}}r_{\lambda,k}^{*}.

5.3.3
Comparison with
[
BMR21
]

The following Theorem is taken from
[
BMR21
, section 4.3.3, Theorem 4.13]
. Theorem
7
is non-asymptotic, because they use the non-asymptotic version of
[
EK10
]
developed in
[
LR20
]
, see
[
BMR21
, Lemma A.6]
.

Theorem 7

(
[
BMR21
]
)
.

Suppose that
d\sim N
. Let
\Sigma
be a semi-positive definite matrix and let
X=\Sigma^{1/2}Z
where
Z
has i.i.d., variance
1
, centered sub-Gaussian coordinates. Denote by
M
an upper bound on the sub-Gaussian norm of the coordinates of
Z
, on
\left\|\Sigma\right\|_{\text{op}}
, and on
(1/d)\sum_{i=1}^{d}\sigma_{i}^{-1}(\Sigma)
. Assume that
k
is a continuous function over
{\mathbb{R}}
and is smooth in a neighborhood of
0
with
k(0),k^{\prime}(0)>0
, and let
K({\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y})=k\left(\left\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y}\right\rangle/d\right)
. Let
Y=f^{*}(X)+\xi
, where
\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{4+\eta}}\leq M
for some
\eta>0
and
\xi\sim{\cal N}(0,\sigma_{\xi}^{2})
. Denote
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}=\Sigma^{-1}{\mathbb{E}}\left[Xf^{*}(X)\right]
and
\lambda_{*}>0
be the unique positive solution of

\displaystyle N\left(1-\frac{\gamma}{\lambda_{*}}\right)=\Tr\left(\Sigma\left(\Sigma+\lambda_{*}I\right)^{-1}\right),\mbox{ where }\gamma=k\left(\frac{\Tr\left(\Sigma\right)}{d}\right)-k(0)-k^{\prime}(0)\left(\frac{\Tr\left(\Sigma\right)}{d}\right).

Then, there exist absolute constants
c,C
such that with probability at least
1-CN^{-1/4}
, the bias term
\mathrm{bias}=\big({\mathbb{E}}_{X}[(f^{*}(X)-{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}\hat{f}_{0}(X))^{2}\big|{\mathbb{X}}]\big)^{1/2}
and the variance term
\mathrm{var}={\mathbb{E}}_{X,\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{N}}[(\hat{f}_{0}(X)-{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}\hat{f}_{0}(X))^{2}\big|{\mathbb{X}}]
satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{bias}^{2}-{\cal B}(\Sigma,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0})-\left\|P_{>1}f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}(1+{\cal V}(\Sigma))\right|\lesssim N^{-c},\quad\left|\mathrm{var}-\sigma_{\xi}^{2}{\cal V}(\Sigma)\right|\lesssim N^{-c},

where

\displaystyle{\cal V}(\Sigma)=\frac{\Tr\left(\Sigma^{2}\left(\Sigma+\lambda_{*}I\right)^{-2}\right)}{N-\Tr\left(\Sigma^{2}\left(\Sigma+\lambda_{*}I\right)^{-2}\right)},\quad{\cal B}(\Sigma,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0})=\frac{\lambda_{*}^{2}\left\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0},\Sigma^{2}\left(\Sigma+\lambda_{*}I\right)^{-2}\Sigma{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}\right\rangle}{1-N^{-1}\Tr\left(\Sigma^{2}\left(\Sigma+\lambda_{*}I\right)^{-2}\right)}.

•

In the scenario where
k(t)=t
(in which KRR reduces to a linear ridge regression), see
[
BMR21
, Corollary 4.14]
, Theorem
7
provides a unified bound of the estimation error in the context of linear regression
[
BMR21
, section 4.3.3]
, as discussed in the works of
[
TB23
,
LS22
]
. Nevertheless, the estimation error is exacerbated due to the inclusion of an additional term
N^{-c}
. Moreover, there is a deterioration of the probability deviation. In contrast, our Theorem
3
demonstrates that our estimation error is equivalent to that of the Gaussian case. In other words, we prove that the kernel ridge regression has the (one-sided) Gaussian Equivalence Property, see Section
4.3
for more details. Moreover, if we substitute Proposition
2
and Theorem
2
with the Gaussian counterparts proposed in
[
LS22
]
, we achieve the optimal probability deviation in the linear case under a Gaussian assumption concerning the design vector and noise.

•

Theorem
7
is limited to the case where
d\sim N
, and hence it is unable to demonstrate the occurrence of the multiple descent of the upper bound for the estimation error of KRR. This phenomenon will be established as a corollary of our Theorem
3
in subsequent sections of this work. It is crucial to observe that Theorem
7
highlights the learning of solely the linear approximation of
f^{*}
, whereas in the context of multiple descent,
\hat{f}_{0}
encompasses the higher-degree approximation of
f^{*}
, hence resulting in a significant decrease in the estimation error.

•

The fixed point
\lambda_{*}
mentioned in Theorem
7
presents challenges in terms of computation. The analysis of the two terms
{\cal V}(\Sigma)
and
{\cal B}(\Sigma,{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0})
is also challenging for this reason. In contrast, Theorem
3
only relies on the spectrum of
\Gamma
as well as the decomposition of
f^{*}
in its eigenbasis, thereby rendering it more feasible in practical applications. The reader is directed to Section
4.1
for specific examples.

5.3.4
Previous results on multiple descent

Theorem 8

(
[
LRZ20
]
)
.

Let
\iota\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
and consider
d=N^{\alpha}
where
(\iota+1)^{-1}\leq\alpha<\iota^{-1}
. Consider a general function
h(t)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}t^{i}
with corresponding Taylor coefficients
\{\alpha_{i}\}_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}
and a kernel
K
such that (
16
) holds. Suppose the first bullet of Assumption
10
below holds. Suppose
Y=f^{*}(X)+\xi
, where
f^{*}\in{\cal H}
,
\xi
is independent of
X
, and the variance of
\xi
is
\sigma_{\xi}^{2}
. Suppose there exists an absolute constant
C>0
such that
\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{L_{4}(\mu)}\leq C
. Then

1.

if
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{\iota}>0
and there exists
\iota^{\prime}\geq 2\iota+3
such that
\alpha_{\iota^{\prime}}>0
. Suppose
d^{\iota}\log{d}\lesssim N\lesssim d^{\iota+1}
, then with constant probability ,

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{f}_{0}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{d^{\iota}}{N}+\frac{N}{d^{\iota+1}},

2.

if for some
\iota>0
,
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{\iota}>0
and for all
\iota^{\prime}>\iota
,
\alpha_{\iota^{\prime}}=0
. Suppose
d^{\iota}\log{d}\lesssim N
. Then with constant probability,

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{f}_{0}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{d^{\iota}}{N}+\frac{1}{N}.

Let us provide some comments on this theorem.

•

The prediction for the interval of
N
, that is,
d^{\iota}\log{d}\lesssim N\lesssim d^{\iota+1}
, where multiple descent happens provided by Theorem
8
is not sharp, as proved by our Proposition
9
which provides a sharp interval of
N
, that is,
d^{\iota}\lesssim N\lesssim d^{\iota+1}
.

•

The dependence of the estimation error on
\sigma_{\xi}
and
f^{*}
is not explicitly stated in Theorem
8
. On the contrary, our Proposition
9
stated the explicit dependence on
\sigma_{\xi}
and
f^{*}
.

•

The probability deviation in Theorem
8
is a constant probability, which is because Proposition 4 in
[
LRZ20
]
only provides a constant-level probability deviation (that is, this deviation does not converge to
0
as
N
increases). Our Proposition
9
provides a better probability deviation, which converges to
0
as
N
increases.

Another result on the multiple descent phenomenon comes from
[
GMMM21
]
.
We emphasize that the following result, as well as other results such as in
[
MMM22
,
Mis22
,
XHM+22
]
, holds as
d\to\infty
.

Theorem 9

(Theorem 4 of
[
GMMM21
]
)
.

Let
\{f_{d}^{*}\in L_{2}(\Omega_{d},\mu_{d})\}_{d\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}}
be a sequence of target functions. Let
(X_{i})_{i\in[N]}\sim\mu_{d}
be a sequence of i.i.d. random vectors, which are uniformly distributed over
\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1}
. Let
Y_{i}=f_{d}^{*}(X_{i})+\xi_{i}
where
\xi_{i}\sim{\cal N}(0,\sigma_{\xi}^{2})
for some
\sigma_{\xi}>0
. For some
\iota\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
and
\delta_{0}>0
, assume that
\omega_{d}\left(d^{\iota}\log{d}\right)\leq N\leq O_{d}\left(d^{\iota+1-\delta_{0}}\right)

.
Let
\{h_{d}\}_{d\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}}
,
h_{d}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a sequence of real-valued functions such that

1.

h_{d}(\cdot/\sqrt{d})\in L_{2}([-\sqrt{d},\sqrt{d}],\mu_{d-1}^{1})
where
\mu_{d-1}^{1}
is the distribution of
\left\langle X_{1},{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}\right\rangle
.

2.

Let
u(\iota)=\sum_{l\leq\iota}d^{l}
. There exists a constant
c_{\iota}>0
such that
\lambda_{*}(d,\iota):=d^{\iota}\min_{k\leq\iota}\sigma_{u(\iota)}(\Gamma)\geq c_{\iota}\Tr\left(\Gamma_{u(\iota)+1:\infty}\right)
.

Suppose
{\mathbb{E}}h_{d}(X)={\mathbb{E}}f_{d}^{*}(X)=0
. Denote the KRR with tuning parameter
\lambda
by
\hat{f}_{d,\lambda}
. Then for any
0<\lambda<\lambda_{*}(d,\iota)
,

\displaystyle\left|\left\|f_{d}^{*}-\hat{f}_{d,\lambda}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu_{d})}^{2}-\left\|P_{>\iota}f_{d}^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu_{d})}^{2}\right|=o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\left(\left\|f_{d}^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu_{d})}^{2}+\sigma_{\xi}^{2}\right).

Theorem
9
requires that the design vector
X
is uniformly distributed over a Euclidean sphere
\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1}
, which is very restrictive. On the contrary, our Proposition
9
holds for sub-Gaussian design. Moreover, the validity of Theorem
9
hinges on the assumption that
d
tends towards infinity. As we saw at the beginning of Section
4.1
, this assumption is not convenient (it requires to consider a sequence of target functions
f_{d}^{*}
). Our result holds for fixed
d
.
The multiple descent appears when
\omega_{d}\left(d^{\iota}\log{d}\right)\leq N\leq O_{d}\left(d^{\iota+1-\delta_{0}}\right)
. On the contrary, we only need
Cd^{\iota}\leq N\leq cd^{\iota+1}
in Proposition
9
.

Recently,
[
Mis22
]
proves multiple descent when the target function
f_{d}^{*}
is a certain type of random function (see Assumption 2 in
[
Mis22
]
), when
X
follows a uniform distribution over
\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1}
, and when both the dimension
d
and the sample size
N
go to infinity. In a more precise manner, it can be shown that varying values of
\lfloor\log{N}/\log{d}\rfloor
result in distinct descents. Within each descent, the function
\hat{f}_{0}
acquires a polynomial approximation of degree
\lfloor\log{N}/\log{d}\rfloor
of the true function
f^{*}
.

5.3.5
Formal version of Proposition
9
(multiple descent)

To align with the setups of
[
LRZ20
]
and
[
GMMM21
]
, we introduce two assumptions:

Assumption 10
.

1.

X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
is a random vector with i.i.d. zero-mean sub-Gaussian coordinates. Moreover, for any finite set
S
, the first coordinate (denoted as
x_{1}
) of
X
satisfies
{\mathbb{P}}(x_{1}\in S)<1
and
{\mathbb{P}}(x_{1}=0)>0
. We denote

, thus

.

2.

Suppose the noise

and

.

3.

There exists a polynomial function

for some

,

for all

) holds.

4.

d\gtrsim_{\mu_{1}}(\log{N})^{2L}L^{6L}
, where

is the number of samples.

Assumption 11
.

1.

is distributed uniformly over

.

Applying Theorem

, we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 9
.

There exist absolute constants

Remark 4
.

Under Assumption
11
and Assumption
10
, we assume
\alpha_{i}>0
. If there exists
\gamma<L-1
such that

, then by recalculating (

) and (

) below, Proposition

still holds. However, the range for

will change from

to
C_{\ref*{C_multiple_lower}}d^{\gamma}\leq N\leq c_{\ref*{c_multiple_upper}}d^{\gamma+2}
. Moreover, in

,

will be replaced by

, and

will be replaced by
\left\|\Gamma_{>\gamma+1}^{1/2}f_{>\gamma+1}^{*}\right\|_{\cal H}
. Similarly, we can handle cases where several
\alpha_{i}
are equal to 0.

5.3.7
An example of feature learning

The following assumption is from
[
BBSS22
]
. It is easy to verify that the activation functions
\sigma:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\tanh(t)
and
\sigma:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto 1/(1+\exp(-t))-1/2
satisfy
[
BBSS22
, Assumption 5.2]
, with
s=1
in
[
BBSS22
, Assumption 5.2]
.

Assumption 12
.

There exists an activation function
\sigma
and a vector
{\boldsymbol{w}}^{*}\in S_{2}^{d-1}
such that
y=f^{*}({\boldsymbol{x}})+\xi
, where
f^{*}({\boldsymbol{x}})=a^{*}\sigma(\left<{\boldsymbol{w}}^{*},{\boldsymbol{x}}\right>)
,
a^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}\backslash\{0\}
,
\xi\sim{\cal N}(0,\sigma_{\xi}^{2})
for some
\sigma_{\xi}>0
, and is independent with
{\boldsymbol{x}}
, and
{\boldsymbol{x}}\sim{\cal N}({\boldsymbol{0}},I_{d})
. Suppose
\sigma:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\tanh(t)
or
\sigma:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto 1/(1+\exp(-t))-1/2
.

Let
X_{1},\cdots,X_{N_{1}+N_{2}}
be independent copies of
{\boldsymbol{x}}
, and
Y_{1},\cdots,Y_{N_{1}+N_{2}}
be independent copies of
y
. Let
\varepsilon_{j}
’s are Rademacher random variables independent with
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}
’s,
b_{j}\sim{\cal N}(0,\tau^{2})
for some
\tau>0
independent with other random variables. Define

\displaystyle L_{N}({\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{w}})=\frac{1}{N_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{1}}\left(\left<{\boldsymbol{a}},\Phi(\left<{\boldsymbol{w}},X_{i}\right>)\right>-Y_{i}\right)^{2}+\eta\left\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\right\|_{2}^{2},\mbox{ where }\Phi(u)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}(\sigma(\varepsilon_{j}u-b_{j}))_{j\in[m]}.

Suppose
N_{1}\gtrsim m+d
. Let
\eta=O(1)
and
\eta=\Omega(\sqrt{\Delta_{crit}})
where

\displaystyle\Delta_{crit}:=\max\bigg\{\sqrt{(d+m)/N_{1}},\left(\frac{d^{2}}{N_{1}}\right)^{2}\bigg\}.

Let
N_{1}=\tilde{\Omega}(\max\{\frac{(d+m)}{\eta^{4}},\frac{d^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\})
. Let
m=\Omega(\frac{1}{\eta}\log(\frac{1}{\eta\delta}))
and
m=\tilde{O}(\eta\Delta_{crit}^{-1})
. Let
m_{0}=\Theta(\log(\frac{1}{\delta}))
and
\rho=\Theta(\sqrt{m}m_{0}^{-\frac{3}{2}}(\tau^{2}+\frac{\eta m}{m_{0}})^{-1})
. Let
T_{0}=\tilde{\Theta}(1)
, and
T_{1}=\tilde{\Theta}(\frac{\eta^{4}N}{d+m})
.

In Assumption
12
, we assume that
f^{*}
is a single-neuron function, a class of target functions that has been extensively studied in the field of deep learning theory, as seen in works such as
[
MBM18
,
OS19
,
VS21
,
TV23
]
and references therein.

Given
W(T)
be some vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we construct a weight matrix
W
by performing Gram-Schmidt process under the inner product
\ell_{2}^{d}
starting from
W(T)
. That is, we obtain
{\boldsymbol{w}}_{1},\cdots,{\boldsymbol{w}}_{m}
such that for any
i,j\in[m]
,
\left<{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j}\right>=\delta_{ij}
, and
{\boldsymbol{w}}_{1}=W(T)
. Take
{\delta_{11}}<1
such that
\|\sigma(\delta_{\ref*{delta_feature_learning}}\cdot)\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}^{2}\sim\frac{1}{m}
. Such
\delta_{\ref*{delta_feature_learning}}
always exists since
\delta\in\{0\leq\delta<1\}\mapsto\|\sigma(\delta\cdot)\|_{L_{2}(\gamma)}
is a continuously increasing function, and
\|\sigma(0\cdot)\|_{L_{2}(\gamma)}=0
. We let
W=[{\boldsymbol{w}}_{1}|\delta_{\ref*{delta_feature_learning}}{\boldsymbol{w}}_{2}|\delta_{\ref*{delta_feature_learning}}{\boldsymbol{w}}_{3}|\cdots|\delta_{\ref*{delta_feature_learning}}{\boldsymbol{w}}_{m}]^{\top}=:[W_{1}|W_{2}|\cdots|W_{m}]^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times d}
.

Proposition 11
.

Grant Assumption
12
. Initialize
{\boldsymbol{w}}(0)
to be uniformly distributed over
S_{2}^{d-1}
, and
{\boldsymbol{a}}(0)
uniformly distributed over
\{{\boldsymbol{a}}\in\rho S_{2}^{m-1}:\left\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\right\|_{0}=m_{0}\}
, where
\left\|{\boldsymbol{a}}\right\|_{0}=\left|\{j\in[m]:a_{j}\neq 0\}\right|
.
Run the following gradient flow algorithm up to time
T=T_{0}+T_{1}
:

\displaystyle\dot{{\boldsymbol{a}}}(t)=-\mathbbm{1}_{\{t>T_{0}\}}\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{a}}}L_{N}({\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{w}}),\mbox{ and }\dot{{\boldsymbol{w}}}(t)=-\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\perp}}\nabla_{{\boldsymbol{w}}}L_{N}({\boldsymbol{a}},{\boldsymbol{w}}),

where
\Pi_{{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\perp}}:{\boldsymbol{v}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto{\boldsymbol{v}}-\left<{\boldsymbol{v}},{\boldsymbol{w}}\right>{\boldsymbol{w}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Let
W(T)=\mathrm{sgn}(\left<{\boldsymbol{w}}(T),{\boldsymbol{w}}^{*}\right>){\boldsymbol{w}}(T)
where where
\mathrm{sgn}(t)=1
if
t\geq 0
and
-1
otherwise. Let
\phi:{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sigma(W{\boldsymbol{x}})
. Denote the data-dependent conjugate RKHS by
({\cal H}_{T},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{{\cal H}_{T}})
. For any
\delta>0
, with probability at least
\frac{1}{2}-\delta
, the minimum
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{{\cal H}_{T}}
norm interpolant estimator (interpolating
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N_{1}+N_{2}}
)
\hat{f}_{0}
constructed on sample
(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i=N_{1}+1}^{N_{1}+N_{2}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{f}_{0}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|a^{*}\right|\left\|\sigma\right\|_{Lip}\tilde{O}\left(\eta^{-2}\max\left\{\sqrt{\frac{d+m}{N_{1}}},\frac{d^{2}}{N_{1}}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{\sigma_{\xi}}{\sqrt{N}}+\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{\frac{N_{2}}{m}}+\frac{\left|a^{*}\right|}{\|\sigma\|_{L_{2}(\gamma_{d})}}\frac{1}{N_{2}}.

The proof of Proposition
11
may be found in Section
6.5.10
.

Estimation error of KRR with smooth kernel in non-asymptotic regime

In this paragraph, we apply our general bounds from Theorem

and Theorem

and

).

Proposition 13
.

Suppose that
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\lambda\geq 0
satisfy
N\leq c_{\ref*{c_kappa_DM}}\kappa_{DM}d_{\lambda}^{*}(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{-1/2}B_{\cal H})
,
\sum_{j\in J_{2}}\sigma_{j}\leq\kappa_{DM}(4\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right))\left(1-\frac{\left|J_{1}\right|}{N}\right)
, and
\kappa_{DM}\left(4\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)\right)\geq N\left(R_{N}^{*}(c_{\ref*{c_kappa_RIP}})\right)^{2}
.
Under Assumption
4
, if
(2k)^{\frac{1}{\log{d}}}\lesssim N/\log^{8}(N)
. Then, with constant probability,
\left\|\hat{f}_{\lambda}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}
is smaller than (up to an absolute multiplicative constant)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \log^{3}(N)&\max\bigg\{\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{\frac{\left|J_{1}\right|}{N}},\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{j\in J_{2}}\sigma_{j}}{4\lambda+k^{1-\alpha}}},\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}f_{k+1:\infty}^{*}\right\|_{\cal H},\\
&{\left\|\tilde{\Gamma}_{1,\mathrm{thre}}^{-1/2}f_{1:k}^{*}\right\|_{\cal H}\frac{\lambda+k^{1-\alpha}}{N}},\sigma_{\xi}\frac{\sqrt{Nk^{1-2\alpha}}}{\lambda+k^{1-\alpha}}\bigg\}.\end{aligned}
