\sqrt{N}\left\|f^{*}-f^{**}\right\|_{L_{2}}
.

Similarly, let
\Omega_{\text{noise},2}
as the random event on which

\displaystyle\Tr(A)\leq\frac{16C_{\ref*{C_sum_Gamma_phi}}N\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})}{\left(4\lambda+\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})\right)^{2}}\mbox{ and }\left\|A\right\|_{\text{op}}^{1/2}\leq\frac{4C_{\ref*{C_DMU}}}{4\lambda+\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})}\left(\sqrt{\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})}+\sqrt{N}\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}\right).

By (
64
) and (
65
),
{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega_{\text{noise},2})\geq 1-{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega_{0}^{c})
. Repeat the above arguments, we obtain that for any
t_{1},t_{2}\geq 0
and
t_{3}>2
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\bigg({\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{\top}A{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\leq(1+t_{1})\sigma_{\xi}^{2}\frac{16C_{\ref*{C_sum_Gamma_phi}}^{2}N\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})}{(4\lambda+\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}))^{2}}+t_{2}t_{3}\sigma_{\xi}\frac{C_{\ref*{C_noise_dependent_3}}\left(\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})+N\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}^{2}\right)}{(4\lambda+\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}))^{2}}\sqrt{N}\left\|f^{*}-f^{**}\right\|_{L_{2}}

\displaystyle+t_{3}^{2}\frac{C_{\ref*{C_noise_dependent_3}}\left(\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})+N\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}^{2}\right)}{(4\lambda+\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}))^{2}}N\left\|f^{*}-f^{**}\right\|_{L_{2+\varepsilon}}^{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\geq 1-{\mathbb{P}}(\Omega_{0}^{c})-\exp(-ct_{2}^{2})-c_{\ref*{c_Men_3}}t_{3}^{-(2+\varepsilon)}N^{-\frac{\varepsilon}{4}}-\exp\left(-c(t_{1}^{2}\wedge t_{1})\frac{8C_{\ref*{C_sum_Gamma_phi}}N\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})}{C_{\ref*{C_DMU}}^{2}\left(\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})+N\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}\right)}\right)

where
{C_{70}}=32C_{\ref*{C_DMU}}c_{\ref*{c_Men_2}}\left(\frac{2+\varepsilon}{2+\varepsilon-2(\varepsilon/4+1)}\right)^{1/2}
.

For simplicity, we only consider the case where
\sigma_{1}N>\kappa_{DM}(4\lambda+\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}))
and
k\lesssim N
.
Replace the usage of Proposition
27
in Section
6.1
with Proposition
28
and repeat the proof. We can conclude as follows:

Proposition 29
.

Grant the assumptions of Theorem
3
and Proposition
28
. Assume that
\sigma_{1}N>\kappa_{DM}(4\lambda+\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}))
. Then with the same probability deviation as in Theorem
3
but with
\bar{p}_{\xi}
replaced by (
123
) and

\displaystyle\left(\frac{C_{\ref*{C_noise}}\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})}{|J_{1}|\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})+N\left(\sum_{j\in J_{2}}\sigma_{j}\right)}\right)^{\frac{r}{4}}

replaced by (
124
), we have:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left\|\hat{f}_{\lambda}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}}&\lesssim\left\|f^{*}-f^{**}\right\|_{L_{2}}+\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{\frac{\left|J_{1}\right|}{N}}+\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{j\in J_{2}}\sigma_{j}}{4\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}}+\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}f_{k+1:\infty}^{**}\right\|_{\cal H}\\
&+{\left\|\tilde{\Gamma}_{1,\mathrm{thre}}^{-1/2}f_{1:k}^{**}\right\|_{\cal H}\frac{2\lambda+3\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}{N}}+4C_{\ref*{C_sum_Gamma_phi}}\sigma_{\xi}\frac{\sqrt{N\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})}}{4\lambda+\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})}\\
&+\frac{C_{\ref*{C_noise_dependent_3}}^{1/2}\sqrt{\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})+N\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}^{2}}}{4\lambda+\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})}\sqrt{N}\left\|f^{*}-f^{**}\right\|_{L_{2+\varepsilon}}.\end{aligned}

(125)

[
Bac24
, Section 7.5.2]
uses
\inf\{\left\|f^{*}-f\right\|_{L_{2}}^{2}+\lambda\left\|f\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}:f\in{\cal H}\}
to characterize the approximation error of
{\cal H}
, that is, the trade-off between the approximation error and
\left\|f\right\|_{\cal H}
. Our Proposition
29
shows that the approximation error is actually traded off against
{\left\|\tilde{\Gamma}_{1,\mathrm{thre}}^{-1/2}f_{1:k}^{**}\right\|_{\cal H}\frac{2\lambda+3\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}{N}}
and
\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}f_{k+1:\infty}^{**}\right\|_{\cal H}
instead of
\left\|f^{**}\right\|_{\cal H}
.

We observe that when the following holds, we still have benign overfitting, even though there is model-misspecification, when
\sigma_{\xi}\sim 1
: for
k\lesssim N
such that
N\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}\lesssim\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})
,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\left|J_{1}\right|=o(N),\,\sum_{j\in J_{2}}\sigma_{j}=o(\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})),\,\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}f_{k+1:\infty}^{**}\right\|_{\cal H}=o(1),\,{\left\|\tilde{\Gamma}_{1,\mathrm{thre}}^{-1/2}f_{1:k}^{**}\right\|_{\cal H}\frac{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}{N}}=o(1),\\
&\frac{\sqrt{N\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})}}{\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})}=o(1),\,\sqrt{N}\left\|f^{*}-f^{**}\right\|_{L_{2+\varepsilon}}=o\left(\frac{\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})}{\sqrt{\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})}}\wedge\frac{\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})}{\sqrt{N}\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}}\wedge\sqrt{N}\right).\end{aligned}

(126)

Remark 8
.

We emphasize that this conclusion does not contradict the counterexamples presented in
[
CLvdG22
]
and
[
Sha22
]
in the setting of adversarial noise and model-misspecification. This is because in
[
CLvdG22
]
,
\|f^{*}-f^{**}\|_{L_{2}}=\Theta(\sigma_{\xi})
(see
[
CLvdG22
, Appendix D]
); in
[
Sha22
]
,
f^{*}:{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\exp(x_{1})
, where
x_{1}
is the first coordinate of
{\boldsymbol{x}}
, and
f^{**}=\left<{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\right>
for some
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Under the probability measure
\mu
assumed in
[
Sha22
, Example 1]
,
\left\|f^{*}-f^{**}\right\|_{L_{2}}
is
\inf_{\mathbf{w}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}R_{d}(\mathbf{w})
in the language of
[
Sha22
, Example 1]
, and it is grater than a constant depending only on
\mu
. Therefore, when
\|f^{*}-f^{**}\|_{L_{2}}=\Theta(1)
, our (
126
) does not necessarily yield benign overfitting, thus not conflicting with
[
CLvdG22
,
Sha22
]
.

6.5.12
Proof of Proposition
9

The proof of the results in the asymptotic regime is presented in Section
6.5.23
. In this section, we focus on the proof of the non-asymptotic conclusions.

The proof of Proposition
9
is rather lengthy, and we complete it in Section
6.5.13
, Section
6.5.14
, Section
6.5.15
, Section
6.5.16
, and Section
6.5.21
. Section
6.5.21
relies on the proof of Theorem
10
in Section
6.5.17
.

Before we begin, we first indicate how to obtain an upper bound for

\displaystyle\left|\|\hat{f}_{0}-f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}-\|f_{>\iota}^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}-\|f^{*}-f^{**}\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}\right|.

For convenience, we denote
\hat{f}_{0}
by
\hat{f}
. Recall that
{\cal H}_{1:k}
is orthogonal to
{\cal H}_{k+1:\infty}
, we have
\|\hat{f}_{0}-f^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}=\|\Gamma^{1/2}(\hat{f}-f^{*})\|_{\cal H}^{2}=\|\hat{f}_{1:k}-f_{1:k}^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\|\hat{f}_{k+1:\infty}-f_{k+1:\infty}^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}
. By subtracting
(\|f_{k+1:\infty}^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\|f^{*}-f^{**}\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2})
from both sides of the equation and taking the absolute value, we have by the triangle inequality:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\left|\left\|\hat{f}_{0}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}-\left\|f_{k+1:\infty}^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}-\left\|f^{*}-f^{**}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}\right|\\
&\leq\|\hat{f}_{1:k}-f_{1:k}^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\|\hat{f}_{k+1:\infty}-f_{k+1:\infty}^{*}\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\left\|f_{k+1:\infty}^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}+\left\|f^{*}-f^{**}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}^{2}.\end{aligned}

(127)

The right side can be further bounded from above by
(r_{0,k}^{*})^{2}
(see Proposition
16
and Proposition
17
), and in the following, we will see that
f_{k+1:\infty}^{*}
is identified as
f_{>\iota}^{*}
.

6.5.13
Spectrum of
\Gamma

The goal of this section is to use Theorem
3
and Theorem
6
to prove the multiple descent phenomenon. For applying Theorem
3
, we need to proceed with the following steps:

1.

Verify Assumption
1
, Assumption
2
and Assumption
3
.

To achieve diagonal concentration, it suffices to verify (
6
), (
12
), and (
13
). This is done in Section
6.5.15
. To validate the norm-equivalence condition stated in Assumption
1
, Assumption
2
, and Assumption
3
, we will examine the scenario where
\epsilon=6
over the entire space
{\cal H}
. This shows the norm-equivalence on
{\cal H}_{1:k}
and
{\cal H}_{k+1:\infty}
, as these subspaces are contained within
{\cal H}
. This is done in Section
6.5.14
.

2.

Compute the appropriate
k
such that
N\leq c_{\ref*{c_kappa_DM}}\kappa_{DM}d_{0}^{*}\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}B_{\cal H}\right)
, and
k\leq c_{\ref*{c_RIP}}N
. In the context of multiple descent, we will make use of Theorem
3
, meaning that we will observe multiple descents occurring when
k\lesssim N
. Given that
\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty})\sim 1
, our findings remain valid even when
\lambda\lesssim 1
.

3.

Compute
\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
,
\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}
and
\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)
.

The last two steps are done in the following two paragraphs.

In this subsection, the determination of the appropriate value for
k
is discussed. The selection of the value of
k
can be divided into two distinct scenarios: under a sub-Gaussian (the setup in
[
LRZ20
]
) or a uniform distribution assumption (the setup in
[
GMMM21
,
MMM22
,
MM20
,
Mis22
]
) and for a kernel
K
such that (
16
) holds.

Sub-Gaussian design

We have the following proposition, whose proof can be found in Section
6.5.16

Proposition 30
.

Suppose that Assumption
10
holds. For a given
0\leq\iota\leq L-1
, there exist absolute constants
{C_{71}}
and
{c_{34}}
(where
c_{\ref*{c_multiple_upper}}
depending on
L
) such that
C_{\ref*{C_multiple_lower}}d^{\iota}\leq N\leq c_{\ref*{c_multiple_upper}}d^{\iota+1}
. We consider
k=\sum_{0\leq l\leq\iota}d^{l}
and decompose the kernel function into two terms:

K(X,X)=h\left(\frac{\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\right)=\sum_{0\leq i\leq\iota}\alpha_{i}\left(\frac{\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\right)^{i}+\sum_{i>\iota}\alpha_{i}\left(\frac{\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\right)^{i}.

Then,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{k}{N}\sim\frac{d^{\iota}}{N},\quad\frac{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}{N}\sim\frac{1}{N},\quad\frac{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}{\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}}\gtrsim d^{\iota+1}>c_{\ref*{c_kappa_DM}}\kappa_{DM}N,\mbox{ and }\\
&\frac{\sqrt{N\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}}{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}\sim\sqrt{\frac{N}{d^{\iota+1}}}.\end{aligned}

(128)

It is worth noting that the selection of
k=\sum_{l\leq\iota}d^{l}
means that
\hat{f}_{0}
learns
f_{\leq\iota}^{*}
while considering
f_{>\iota}^{*}
as noise. By observing that
f_{\leq\iota}^{*}
represents the projection onto the subspace formed by the orthogonal polynomials of degree
\iota
in the Hilbert space
L_{2}(\mu)
, it becomes evident that
\hat{f}_{0}
acquires knowledge of a polynomial approximation of degree
\iota
for
f^{*}
. By increasing the number of samples inside the range of
C_{\ref*{C_multiple_lower}}d^{\iota}\leq N\leq c_{\ref*{c_multiple_upper}}d^{\iota+1}
to
C_{\ref*{C_multiple_lower}}d^{\iota+1}\leq N\leq c_{\ref*{c_multiple_upper}}d^{\iota+2}
, a polynomial approximation may be achieved with an additional degree. Proposition
30
is still valid when
\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
is replaced by
\lambda+\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)
, where
\lambda\lesssim 1
.

Uniform distribution design

When
\Omega=\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1}
and
\mu
(the distribution of
X
) is uniform over
\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1}
,
L_{2}(\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1},\mu)
admits an orthonormal decomposition. The following statements are taken from
[
MMM22
,
MM20
,
GMMM21
,
Mis22
]
. In fact, for
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let
\tilde{V}_{d,l}
be the space of homogeneous harmonic polynomials of degree
l
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, that is, the space of degree-
l
polynomials
q({\boldsymbol{x}})
such that
\Delta q({\boldsymbol{x}})=0
, where
\Delta
is the Laplacian. Let
V_{d,l}
be the linear space of functions obtained by restricting the polynomials in
\tilde{V}_{d,l}
to
\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1}
. Then
L_{2}(\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1},\mu)=\oplus^{\perp}_{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}V_{d,l}
. Furthermore, by
[
XHM+22
, section 2]
, when
l<L
and
L!=O(d)
, we have

\displaystyle\dim\left(V_{d,l}\right)=\frac{d^{l}}{l!}+O(d^{l-1})\sim d^{l}.

(129)

When Assumption
11
is true and for
0\leq\iota\leq L-1
such that
C_{\ref*{C_multiple_lower}}d^{\iota}\leq N\leq c_{\ref*{c_multiple_upper}}d^{\iota+1}
, we define
k=\sum_{l=0}^{\iota}\dim(V_{d,l})
. In the work of
[
XHM+22
, section 2]
, it is demonstrated that the eigenvalues with multiplicity (denoted by
\lambda_{l}
) of the matrix
\Gamma
exhibit a decay rate on the order of
d^{-l}
. Under Assumption
11
,
\sigma_{l}
exhibits a multi-plateau trend as in the sub-Gaussian case. Consequently, the spectrum of the variable
\Gamma
exhibits similarity to that of the sub-Gaussian design scenario, but with a constant factor, and we decompose

\displaystyle K(X,X)=\sum_{i\leq\iota}\alpha_{i}\left(\frac{\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\right)^{i}+\sum_{i>\iota}\alpha_{i}\left(\frac{\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2}}{d}\right)^{i}=:K_{1:k}(X,X)+K_{k+1:\infty}(X,X)

(130)

as well. Therefore,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{k}{N}\sim\frac{d^{\iota}}{N},\quad\frac{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}{N}\sim\frac{1}{N},\quad\frac{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}{\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right\|_{\text{op}}}\gtrsim d^{\iota+1}>c_{\ref*{c_kappa_DM}}\kappa_{DM}N,\mbox{ and }\\
&\frac{\sqrt{N\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2}\right)}}{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}\sim\sqrt{\frac{N}{d^{\iota+1}}}.\end{aligned}

(131)

In this way, we have completed the steps 2) and 3) outlined at the beginning of this subsection. In the following two subsections, we will complete step 1).

6.5.14
Norm equivalence.

Under Assumption
10

We strengthen
[
LRZ20
, Lemma 10]
from
L_{4}-L_{2}
norm equivalence to
L_{8}-L_{2}
norm equivalence. The proof of the following Proposition can be found in Section
6.5.22
.

Proposition 31
.

Under Assumption
10
, there exists
\kappa\geq 1
depending only on
L
(with
L^{L}
dependence), such that for all
f\in{\cal H}
,

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{L_{8}}\leq\kappa\left\|f\right\|_{L_{2}}.

The proof of Proposition
31
may be found in Section
6.5.22
.

Under Assumption
11

For every
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
(V_{d,l},L_{2}(\mu))
admits an ONB
(Y_{l,m})_{m\leq\dim\left(V_{d,l}\right)}
where
(Y_{l,m})_{m\leq\dim\left(V_{d,l}\right)}
are homogeneous polynomials of degree
l
. For further details, please refer to section 4.2 of
[
FE12
]
. Given that
\mu
follows a uniform distribution over
\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1}
and
{\cal H}
is a subset of
\oplus_{l>\iota}V_{d,l}
that consists of polynomials with degrees lower than
L
, (
7
) can be derived directly from the work of
[
Bec92
]
by setting the values of the parameters
\epsilon=6
and
\kappa<7^{L}
, respectively.

6.5.15
Diagonal terms.

In this subsection, we check (
6
) under Assumption
11
and Assumption
10
. The concentration property of the diagonal terms appeared in Assumption
2
and Assumption
3
followed by the same idea. The scenario described in Assumption
11
is comparatively simpler than the case outlined in Assumption
10
. We therefore prioritize addressing the former case initially.

Under Assumption
11

Because
\mu
is a uniform distribution over
\sqrt{d}S_{2}^{d-1}
,
K
is a translation-invariant kernel. Recall that we have chosen
k=\sum_{l=0}^{\iota}\dim(V_{d,l})
. In fact,
\left\|\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{{\cal H}}=\sqrt{K_{k+1:\infty}(X,X)}=\sqrt{\sum_{i>\iota}\alpha_{i}\left(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2}/d\right)^{i}}=\sqrt{\sum_{i=\iota+1}^{L}\alpha_{i}}=\sqrt{\Tr\left(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}\right)}
which is a constant. As a result, (
6
) follows by taking
\delta=\gamma=0
. The validity of the diagonal terms appeared in Assumption
3
and Assumption
2
follows by noticing that
\sigma_{k}^{-1}\sim k
and
\sigma_{k+1}\sim\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})
for our choice of
k
. Hence
\left\|\Gamma_{1:k}^{-1/2}\phi_{1:k}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\leq\sigma_{k}^{-1}\left\|\phi_{1:k}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\sim k
, and
\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\leq\sigma_{k+1}\left\|\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\sim\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})
. As a result, we can take
{\gamma_{\ref*{gamma_RIP_k>N}}}={\gamma_{\ref*{gamma_DMU_L2}}}=0
and
\delta_{\ref*{delta_DMU_L2}},\delta_{\ref*{delta_RIP_k>N}}\sim 1
.

Under Assumption
10

Under Assumption
10
, we need to develop a new concentration inequality similar to the Hanson-Wright inequality for non-asymptotic verification of (
6
). For the sake of generality, we will establish a concentration inequality for the general kernel function
K
in the following theorem, although we will only utilize the special case of
K=K_{k+1:\infty}
.

Theorem 10
.

Let
X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
be a random vector with i.i.d. mean zero coordinates
(x_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
. Assume that
x_{1}
is sub-Gaussian with the sub-Gaussian norm denoted by
\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}
, that is to say, for any
q\geq 2
, we have
{\mathbb{E}}x_{1}^{q}\leq C_{\ref*{C_subgaussian}}q^{q/2}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{q}
. Given
L\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, satisfying
L\geq 2
, and
2^{2L}L^{3L}<\sqrt{d}
. Given
\gamma_{1},\cdots,\gamma_{L}\in{\mathbb{R}}
, we let
h:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto\sum_{\iota=1}^{L}\gamma_{\iota}t^{\iota}
. We suppose
h(1)\neq 0
for the sake of simplicity. Define
K:({\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto h(\left<{\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y}\right>/d)\in{\mathbb{R}}
. There exists an absolute constant
{C_{72}}
depending on
L
and
\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}
, such that for any
t>0
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|K(X,X)-{\mathbb{E}}K(X,X)\right|\geq t\right)\leq\exp\left(-C_{\ref*{C_non_linear_HS}}\frac{t^{\frac{1}{L}}d^{\frac{1}{2L}}}{4L^{3}\left(\sum_{\iota=1}^{L}\gamma_{\iota}\right)^{\frac{1}{L}}}\right).

The proof of Theorem
10
is postponed to Section
6.5.17
.

Applying Theorem
10
to
K=K_{k+1:\infty}
,
h:u\mapsto\sum_{i>\iota}^{L}\alpha_{i}u^{i}
and
t=\delta(\sum_{i>\iota}^{L}\alpha_{i})
together with a union bound, we can take

\displaystyle\delta=1/(200\sqrt{C_{\ref*{C_distortion_1}}}),\mbox{ and }\gamma=N\exp\left(-C_{\ref*{C_non_linear_HS_1}}\frac{d^{\frac{1}{2L}}}{4L^{3}}\right)

(132)

for an absolute constant
{C_{73}}
. Here, the item [4] from Assumption
10
ensures that when
d\gtrsim_{\mu_{1}}(\log(N))^{2L}L^{6L}
, we have
\gamma<1
.

We postpone the verification under Assumption
10
of the diagonal concentration condition appeared in Assumption
3
and Assumption
2
to Section
6.5.21
.

6.5.16
Proof of Proposition
30

We write the feature map explicitly

\phi:{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto\sum_{\left|{\cal I}\right|=0}^{L}{\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{\left|{\cal I}\right|}}{d^{\left|{\cal I}\right|}}}\sqrt{C_{{\cal I}}}}{P_{{\cal I}}({\boldsymbol{x}})}e_{\cal I},\mbox{ s.t. }K({\boldsymbol{x}},\mathbf{y})=\left\langle\phi({\boldsymbol{x}}),\phi(\mathbf{y})\right\rangle_{\ell_{2}}

(133)

where
C_{{\cal I}}=\left|{\cal I}\right|!/\left|{\cal I}_{1}\right|!\cdots\left|{\cal I}_{d}\right|!
,
P_{{\cal I}}({\boldsymbol{x}})=x_{1}^{\left|{\cal I}_{1}\right|}\cdot\cdots\cdot x_{d}^{\left|{\cal I}_{d}\right|}
and
e_{\cal I}
are ONB of
\ell_{2}^{\binom{d+L}{L}}
. Notice that here we embed
{\cal H}
into
\ell_{2}
. The notation
{\cal I}
is defined in Section
1.4
.

Notice that
(P_{\cal I}(X))_{\left|{\cal I}\right|\leq L}
is not an ONB of
L_{2}(\mu)
, because they are not orthogonal in
L_{2}(\mu)
. To obtain an ONB, we utilize the Gram-Schmidt procedure, employing the identical rationale as in
[
LRZ20
, Appendix A.2]
:

Lemma 19
.

Define
q_{0},q_{1},\cdots
as the ONB of
L_{2}({\mathbb{R}},\mu_{1})
produced by applying the Gram-Schmidt process on the basis
\{1,t,\cdots,t^{k},\cdots\}
.
Then we have the following properties:

•

(Q_{\cal I})_{\left|{\cal I}\right|=1}^{\infty}
is an ONB of
L_{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d},\mu)
when
Q_{\cal I}:{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto\prod_{j=1}^{d}q_{\left|{\cal I}_{j}\right|}(x_{j})
.

•

Let

\psi:{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto\sum_{\left|{\cal I}\right|=0}^{L}\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{\left|{\cal I}\right|}}{d^{\left|{\cal I}\right|}}}\sqrt{C_{\cal I}}Q_{\cal I}({\boldsymbol{x}})e_{\cal I}.

(134)

There exists an upper triangular non-singular matrix
\Lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\binom{d+L}{L}\times\binom{d+L}{L}}
with
\max\{\left\|\Lambda\right\|_{\text{op}},\,\left\|\Lambda^{-1}\right\|_{\text{op}}\}\leq C_{L}
, such that
\phi=\Lambda^{\top}\psi
.

The matrix
\Lambda^{\top}
functions as the transformation matrix that converts the orthonormal basis
(Q_{\cal I})_{\left|{\cal I}\right|\leq L}
into a linearly independent basis
(P_{\cal I})_{\left|{\cal I}\right|\leq L}
, which may be found in
[
MS23
, section 4.8, section 5.5]
. As a result,
\Gamma={\mathbb{E}}\left[\phi(X)\otimes\phi(X)\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\Lambda^{\top}\psi(X)\right)\otimes\left(\Lambda^{\top}\psi(X)\right)\right]=\Lambda^{\top}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\psi(X)\otimes\psi(X)\right]\Lambda
. By Courant-Fisher’s max-min theorem, see for example,
[
Ver18
, section 4.1]
, together with Lemma
19
, the eigenvalue of
\Gamma
is equivalent to that of
{\mathbb{E}}\left[\psi(X)\otimes\psi(X)\right]
up to absolute constants depending on
L
, because
\max\{\left\|\Lambda\right\|_{\text{op}},\left\|\Lambda^{-1}\right\|_{\text{op}}\}\leq C_{L}
.

We now study the spectrum of
{\mathbb{E}}[\psi(X)\otimes\psi(X)]
, which is up to constant, the spectrum of
\Gamma
. The spectrum of
\Gamma
performs a “multi-stage” pattern up to
\alpha
. More precisely, by (
134
), we have the following.

•

When
\left|{\cal I}\right|=0
. By (
134
), there is a unique eigenvalue
\alpha_{0}
, which is of constant order.

•

When
\left|{\cal I}\right|=1
. By (
134
), for every such
{\cal I}
,
C_{\cal I}=1
, thus the first stage of
\mathrm{spec}(\Gamma)
is a plateau of height
1/d
(up to
\alpha
), and of length
d
.

•

When
\left|{\cal I}\right|=2
. By (
134
), we know that
C_{\cal I}=1
for diagonal
{\cal I}
and
C_{\cal I}=2
for off-diagonal. Therefore the second stage is of height
1/d^{2}
(up to constants depending on
\alpha
) and of length
d^{2}
.

•

Generally, if
\left|{\cal I}\right|=qd+r
for
q\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
0\leq r<d
, the multi-nomial coefficient
C_{\cal I}
has maximum
\left|{\cal I}\right|!/\left((q!)^{d-r}\left((q+1)!\right)^{r}\right)
. When
L<d
, we have
1\leq C_{\cal I}\leq\left|{\cal I}\right|!/2^{\left|{\cal I}\right|}<\exp(\left|{\cal I}\right|)
. As in
[
LRZ20
]
, we ignore
C_{\cal I}
(this happens when
e^{L}<d
, which is guaranteed by Assumption
10
). The constraint mentioned above may be removed through careful computation; however, we have chosen to avoid doing so in order to give priority to simplicity. In fact, as
\left|{\cal I}\right|
increases, each stage’s slope rises rapidly (with an exponential rate), because of
C_{\cal I}
. For instance, denoting
\left|{\cal I}\right|=l
, then the
l
-th stage of
\mathrm{spec}(\Gamma)
has length
d^{l}
and drops from
l!/(2^{l}\cdot d^{l})
to
1/d^{l}
.

6.5.17
Proof of Theorem
10

In this section, we prove Theorem
10
. We will introduce the notation for tensor products and differential calculus used in the proof of Theorem
10
in Section
6.5.18
and Section
6.5.19
, respectively. Subsequently, in Section
6.5.20
, we will employ these notations and lemmas to prove Theorem
10
.

6.5.18
Tensors

In algebra, the tensor product of multiple vector spaces is used to deal with multilinear mappings on these vector spaces. Given vectors
4
4

4

In this paper, we only consider real vector spaces.
spaces
U,V,W
, we write
U\otimes V
as the tensor product between
U
and
V
, with the correspondence given by
({\boldsymbol{u}},{\boldsymbol{v}})\mapsto{\boldsymbol{u}}\otimes{\boldsymbol{v}}
. We also write
L(U;V)
as the space of linear map from
U
to
V
, which is itself a vector space. The tensor product provides the following isomorphism:
L(U;L(V;W))\cong L(U\otimes V;W)
, where “
\cong
” is algebraic isomorphism, see
[
Lan02
, Chapter 16, Section 2, pp.607]
. Tensor product has associativity, that is,
U\otimes V\otimes W\cong(U\otimes V)\otimes W\cong U\otimes(V\otimes W)
, see
[
Yok92
, Proposition 1.7, Proposition 1.8]
. The following property of tensor product is frequently utilized in the remaining part of this section. Let
V_{1},V_{2}
,
W_{1},W_{2}
and
U_{1},U_{2}
be real vector spaces, let
F_{1},G_{1}\in L(V_{1};W_{1})
,
F_{2},G_{2}\in L(V_{2};W_{2})
,
H_{1}\in L(W_{1};U_{1})
and
H_{2}\in L(W_{2};U_{2})
. Then
(H_{1}\circ F_{1})\otimes(H_{2}\circ F_{2})=(H_{1}\otimes H_{2})\circ(F_{1}\otimes F_{2})
, see
[
Yok92
, Proposition 1.9]
. Given a tensor
T
, and some
k\geq 1
, we write
T^{\otimes k}
as
T\otimes\cdots\otimes T
(
k
times).

In statistics and computer science, it is common to select a basis and define tensors by enumerating their elements. Given
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
and
d_{1},d_{2},\cdots,d_{k}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, a tensor of degree
k
, dimension
d_{1},d_{2},\cdots,d_{k}=d
is an array
T=(t_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}})
, where
\{t_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}}\}^{\prime}s
are in
{\mathbb{R}}
(we only consider real field in this paper). If
d_{1}=d_{2}=\cdots=d_{k}
, we call
T
as a tensor of degree
k
, dimension
d
. A degree
k
dimension
d
tensor
T
is called symmetric, if it is unchanged upon permuting the indices. The space of symmetric degree
k
, dimension
d
tensors, denoted as
\mathrm{Sym}_{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. Given a tensor
T\in\mathrm{Sym}_{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, we can always unfold
T
into
{\mathbb{R}}^{d^{k}}
. Given two matrices
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m\times n}
,
B\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p\times q}
for some
m,n,p,q\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
,
(A\otimes B)^{\top}=(A^{\top})\otimes(B^{\top})
,
[
Yok92
, Proposition 1.9’]
.

Given two tensors
A=(a_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}})
and
B=(b_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}})
of degree
k
and dimension
d
, the inner product between
A
and
B
is defined as:

\displaystyle\left<A,B\right>=\sum_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}=1}^{d}a_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}}b_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}},

which is equivalent to the inner product of the two vectors obtained by unfolding
A
and
B
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d^{k}}
. We let
\left\|A\right\|_{HS}=\sqrt{\left<A,A\right>}
as the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of
A
.

Given a tensor
T\in\mathrm{Sym}_{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, whose entries are random variables on
{\mathbb{R}}
, the expectation of
T
is defined as a tensor
{\mathbb{E}}T=({\mathbb{E}}t_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}})\in\mathrm{Sym}_{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.

Given
r,d\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
and
A
be a degree
r
, dimension
d
tensor. Let
P_{r}
denote the set of partitions of
[r]
into non-empty, pairwise disjoint sets, and for each partition
{\cal J}\in P_{r}
,
{\cal J}=\left\{J_{1},\cdots,J_{k}\right\}
, we define

\left\|A\right\|_{\cal J}=\sup\left(\sum_{{\cal I}\in[d]^{r}}a_{\cal I}\prod_{l=1}^{k}x_{{\cal I}_{J_{l}}}^{(l)}:\,\sqrt{\sum_{\left|{\cal I}_{J_{l}}\right|\leq d}\left(x_{{\cal I}_{J_{l}}}^{(l)}\right)^{2}}\leq 1,\,1\leq l\leq k\right),

see also
[
Leh11
]
for an alternative notation by using injective tensor products.
For example, when
r=2
,
P_{2}=\{\{1,2\},\{\{1\},\{2\}\}\}
,
a_{\cal I}=a_{ij}
. For
J_{1}=\{1,2\}
, we have
k=l=1
,
{\cal I}\in[d]^{2}
and
\sqrt{\sum_{i,j\leq d}x_{ij}^{2}}=\left\|x\right\|_{HS}
for
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d^{2}}
, thus
\left\|A\right\|_{\left\{1,2\right\}}=\left\|A\right\|_{HS}
. For
J_{1}=\{1\},J_{2}=\{2\}
, we have
k=2
and for the sake of simplicity, we write
x_{{\cal I}_{1}}^{(1)}
and
x_{{\cal I}_{2}}^{(2)}
as
x,y
, thus
\sqrt{\sum_{i\leq d}x_{i}^{2}},\sqrt{\sum_{j\leq d}y_{j}^{2}}\leq 1
, and
\left\|A\right\|_{\left\{1\right\},\left\{2\right\}}=\left\|A\right\|_{\text{op}}
. For
r=3
, we can write
[3]=\{1,2,3\}
, corresponding to view
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d^{3}}
as a linear functional with norm
\left\|A\right\|_{\{1,2,3\}}=\sqrt{\sum_{i,j,k}a_{i,j,k}^{2}}
; or
\{1,2\},\{3\}
, corresponding to view
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d^{3}}
as a bi-linear functional on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d^{2}}\times{\mathbb{R}}
, with the norm
\left\|A\right\|_{\{1,2\},\{3\}}=\sup\left(\left|\sum_{i,j,k}a_{i,j,k}x_{i,j}y_{k}\right|:\,\sum x_{i,j}^{2}\leq 1,\,\sum y_{k}^{2}\leq 1\right)
(
\{1,3\},\{2\}
or
\{2,3\},\{1\}
are similar); or
\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}
, corresponding to view
A
as a tri-linear functional on
\left({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\right)^{3}
.
We have

\left\|A\right\|_{\{1\},\{2\},\{3\}}\leq\left\|A\right\|_{\{1,2\},\{3\}},\,\left\|A\right\|_{\{1,3\},\{2\}},\,\left\|A\right\|_{\{2,3\},\{1\}}\leq\left\|A\right\|_{\{1,2,3\}}.

If
{\cal K}
is a finer partition than
{\cal J}
(any element in
{\cal K}
is contained in an element in
{\cal J}
), then
\left\|A\right\|_{\cal K}\leq\left\|A\right\|_{\cal J}
, see
[
Leh11
, Section 2.1]
.

6.5.19
Differential Calculus

Let
{\cal X},{\cal Y}
be two normed vector spaces with norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\cal X}
and
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\cal Y}
,
\Omega\subseteq{\cal X}
be an open set,
f:\Omega\to{\cal Y}
is Fréchet differentiable at
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\Omega
, if there exists a bounded linear operator
A_{\boldsymbol{x}}\in L({\cal X},{\cal Y})
, such that

\displaystyle\lim_{{\boldsymbol{h}}\to{\boldsymbol{0}}}\frac{\left\|f({\boldsymbol{x}}+{\boldsymbol{h}})-f({\boldsymbol{x}})-A_{\boldsymbol{x}}{\boldsymbol{h}}\right\|_{\cal Y}}{\left\|{\boldsymbol{h}}\right\|_{\cal X}}=0,

(135)

where the limit is in the sense of the topology generated by
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{\cal X}
, see
[
AK16
, Definition 14.2.1]
. Denote
\mathbf{D}f({\boldsymbol{x}})=A_{\boldsymbol{x}}
. In the case of
{\cal X}=({\mathbb{R}}^{d},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2})
and
{\cal Y}=({\mathbb{R}},\left\|\cdot\right\|_{1})
, the Fréchet derivative at
{\boldsymbol{x}}
is simply the transpose of the gradient of
f
at
{\boldsymbol{x}}
. If we omit
{\boldsymbol{x}}
, then
\mathbf{D}f:{\boldsymbol{x}}\in{\cal X}\mapsto A_{\boldsymbol{x}}\in L({\cal X},{\cal Y})
. Given
g:{\cal Z}\to\Omega\subseteq{\cal X}
, the chain rule gives that
\mathbf{D}(f\circ g)({\boldsymbol{z}})=\mathbf{D}f(g({\boldsymbol{z}}))\mathbf{D}g({\boldsymbol{z}})\in L({\cal Z},{\cal Y})
. We omit the variables and write
\mathbf{D}(f\circ g)=(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g)
and
\mathbf{D}(fg)=(\mathbf{D}f)g+f(\mathbf{D}g)
. These notations make sense because
\mathbf{D}g:{\cal Z}\to L({\cal Z},{\cal X})
and
\mathbf{D}f\circ g:{\cal Z}\to L({\cal X},{\cal Y})
, hence
(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g):{\cal Z}\to L({\cal Z},{\cal Y})
.

The second order Fréchet derivative is defined as the Fréchet derivative of
\mathbf{D}f
, denoted as
\mathbf{D}^{2}f:{\cal X}\to L({\cal X},L({\cal X},{\cal Y}))\cong L({\cal X}\otimes{\cal X},{\cal Y})
. That is to say, the second-order Fréchet derivative is a degree 2 tensor. If
{\cal X}
and
{\cal Y}
are both (finite-dimensional) Euclidean spaces, and we represent
\mathbf{D}^{2}f
in the standard orthogonal basis of Euclidean space, we will obtain that
\mathbf{D}^{2}f
is actually the Hessian, that is,
(\mathbf{D}^{2}f)_{ij}=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}f({\boldsymbol{x}})
. Generally, for
k\geq 1
,
\mathbf{D}^{k}f
for
f
defined on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
can be defined iteratively as
\mathbf{D}^{k}f=\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}^{k-1}f)
, resulting in a tensor in
\mathrm{Sym}_{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, and if we represent
\mathbf{D}^{k}f
in the standard ONB of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we have
(\mathbf{D}^{k}f)_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}}=\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial x_{i_{1}}\cdots\partial x_{i_{k}}}f({\boldsymbol{x}})
.

Providing a high-order chain rule is not easy. To see this, recall that we define
\mathbf{D}^{2}(f\circ g)=\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}(f\circ g))
. Applying the product rule gives that
\mathbf{D}^{2}(f\circ g)=\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}g+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}^{2}g
. However, if we use the chain rule for computing
\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)=(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}g
, we face the issue that
\mathbf{D}g:{\cal Z}\to L({\cal Z};{\cal X})
, however,
\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g:{\cal Z}\to L({\cal X}\otimes{\cal X};{\cal Y})
. This leads to its evaluation at
{\boldsymbol{z}}
, that is,
((\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)({\boldsymbol{z}}))(\mathbf{D}g({\boldsymbol{z}}))
not being well-defined.

To address this issue, we adopt the tensor notations developed by
[
Man13
]
, that is,

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)&=(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g\otimes I_{{\cal Z}\to L({\cal X};{\cal X})}),\\
\mathbf{D}((\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g))&=\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(I_{{\cal Z}\to L({\cal Z};{\cal Z})}\otimes\mathbf{D}g)+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{2}g),\\
\mbox{ and }\mathbf{D}(f\otimes g)&=(\mathbf{D}f\otimes g)+(f\otimes\mathbf{D}g).\end{aligned}

(136)

Let’s verify that this notation makes sense. Recall that
\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g:{\cal Z}\to L({\cal X};L({\cal X};{\cal Y}))\cong L({\cal X}\otimes{\cal X};{\cal Y})
, and
\mathbf{D}g:{\cal Z}\to L({\cal Z};{\cal X})
. Hence,
\mathbf{D}g\otimes I_{{\cal Z}\to L({\cal X};{\cal X})}:{\cal Z}\otimes{\cal Z}\to L({\cal Z};{\cal X})\otimes L({\cal X};{\cal X})\cong L({\cal Z}\otimes{\cal X};{\cal X}\otimes{\cal X})
. Therefore, the right-hand-side of the modified chain rule satisfies,
(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g\otimes I_{{\cal Z}\to L({\cal X};{\cal X})}):{\cal Z}\otimes{\cal Z}\to L({\cal Z}\otimes{\cal X};{\cal Y})\cong L({\cal Z};L({\cal X};{\cal Y}))
. The left-hand-side of the modified chain rule
\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}f\circ g):{\cal Z}\to L({\cal Z};L({\cal X};{\cal Y}))
, coincides with the right-hand-side formula. As a result, the modified chain rule notation makes sense. The modified product rule follows from the same idea.

The chain rule of higher-order Fréchet derivatives can be conveniently represented using the tensor product language introduced in (
136
) as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{2}(f\circ g)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}(f\circ g))=\mathbf{D}\left((\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g)\right)=\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(I_{{\cal Z}\to L({\cal Z};{\cal Z})}\otimes\mathbf{D}g)+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{2}g)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g\otimes I_{{\cal Z}\to L({\cal X};{\cal X})})(I_{{\cal Z}\to L({\cal Z};{\cal Z})}\otimes\mathbf{D}g)+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{2}g)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g\otimes\mathbf{D}g)+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{2}g).

(137)

Since the domain and codomain of the identity map are unambiguously understood, we henceforth omit the subscripts for the identity map.

Let
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{i})_{i=1}^{\infty}
are an ONB of
\ell_{2}
, define
\leq
be a partial order on
\ell_{2}
defined by
{\boldsymbol{x}}\leq\mathbf{y}\Leftrightarrow\left<{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\right>\leq\left<\mathbf{y},{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\right>
for any
i\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
. We say
{\boldsymbol{x}}\lesssim\mathbf{y}
if there exists an absolute constant
C
such that
{\boldsymbol{x}}\leq C\mathbf{y}
. We say
{\boldsymbol{x}}\lesssim_{K}\mathbf{y}
if the constant
C
depends on
K
. Let us prove the lemma below.

Lemma 20
.

Let
d,p,q\geq 1
,
K\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
,
K\geq 3
. Suppose
g:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
and
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{p}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{q}
be
C^{K}
functions. Given any
n,k\in[K]
and
i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 0
, we view every tensor of the form
(\mathbf{D}^{n}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g)
as orthonormal vectors
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k};n)}
in
\ell_{2}
, that is, given
k,l,m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
,
i_{1},\cdots,i_{k},i_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,i_{l}^{\prime}\geq 0
, then
\left<{\boldsymbol{v}}_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k};n)},{\boldsymbol{v}}_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,i_{l}^{\prime};m)}\right>=\mathbbm{1}_{\{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k};n)=(i_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,i_{l}^{\prime};m)\}}
. In particular, if
l\neq k
or
n\neq m
, then
\left<{\boldsymbol{v}}_{(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k};n)},{\boldsymbol{v}}_{(i_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,i_{l}^{\prime};m)}\right>=0
.
There then exists an absolute constant
{C_{74}}
depending only on
K
, such that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} C_{\ref*{C_concentration}}^{-1}\mathbf{D}^{K}(f\circ g)&\leq(\mathbf{D}^{K}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes K}+\sum_{k=2}^{K-1}(\mathbf{D}^{k}f\circ g)\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right)\\
&+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}^{K}g.\end{aligned}

(138)

The overall strategy for proving Lemma
20
is based on the induction method. We first prove the following lemma. To avoid certain pathological cases, in the following text, we always assume that the functions appearing are sufficiently many times Fréchet differentiable (for our purposes, this assumption always holds).

Lemma 21
.

Given any
k\geq 1
, for any normed vector spaces
{\cal Z},{\cal X}
and
g:{\cal Z}\to{\cal X}
be
(k+1)
-times Fréchet differentiable on some open set
\Omega\subseteq{\cal Z}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}\left((\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes k}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=k+1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{k}}g.

(139)

Moreover, given any
(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k})
satisfying
i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1
and
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=k+1
, we denote
(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k})+1
as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} (i_{1},\cdots,i_{k})+1:=\bigcup_{j\in[k]}(i_{1},\cdots,i_{j-1},i_{j}+1,i_{j+1},\cdots,i_{k}),\end{aligned}

(140)

then

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}\left(\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{k}}g\right)=\sum_{(l_{1},\cdots,l_{k})\in(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k})+1}\mathbf{D}^{\otimes l_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes l_{k}}g.

(141)

Proof.
When
k=1
,
\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}g)=\mathbf{D}^{2}g
. Suppose (
139
) holds for some
K\geq 1
, by (
136
) we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}((\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes(K+1)})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}\left((\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes K}\otimes\mathbf{D}g\right)=\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes K}\otimes\mathbf{D}g+(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes K}\otimes\mathbf{D}^{2}g

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{K}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{K}=K+1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{K}}g\otimes\mathbf{D}g\right)+(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes K}\otimes\mathbf{D}^{2}g

(142)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{K+1}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{K+1}=K+2\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{K+1}}g,

where the last equality is via the following observation: given
l\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
and
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{l}=l+1
and
i_{1},\cdots,i_{l}\geq 1
, by the Pigeonhole principle, there must be a unique
n(l)\in[l]
such that
n(l)=2
. Now let
l=K+1
, we have:

1.

the first term in (
142
) corresponds to the case where
n(K+1)
belongs to
[K]
, that is, we have:
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{K}+i_{K+1}=K+2
,
i_{1},\cdots,i_{K},i_{K+1}\geq 1
, and
i_{K+1}=1
. Therefore,
n(K+1)
must necessarily appear among
[K]
, and

2.

the second term in (
142
) corresponds to the case where
n(K+1)=K+1
. That is,
i_{1},\cdots,i_{K},i_{K+1}\geq 1
,
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{K}+i_{K+1}=K+2
, and
i_{1}=\cdots=i_{K}=1
.

(
139
) is therefore derived.

Now we prove (
141
). When
k=1
,
i_{1}=2
and
(i_{1})+1=(i_{1}+1)=(3)
.
\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}^{2}g)=\mathbf{D}^{3}g
. Hence (
141
) is valid when
k=1
. Suppose (
141
) is valid for some
K\geq 1
, then for any
(i_{1},\cdots,i_{K},i_{K+1})
such that
i_{1},\cdots,i_{K+1}\geq 1
and
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{K+1}=K+1
, by (
136
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}\left(\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{K}}g\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{K+1}}g\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}\left(\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{K}}g\right)\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{K+1}}g+\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{K}}g\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{K+1}+1}g

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l_{1},\cdots,l_{k})\in(i_{1},\cdots,i_{K})+1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{\otimes l_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes l_{K}}g\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{K+1}}g\right)+\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{K}}g\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{K+1}+1}g

\displaystyle=\sum_{(l_{1},\cdots,l_{K+1})\in(i_{1},\cdots,i_{K+1})+1}\mathbf{D}^{\otimes l_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes l_{K+1}}g.

Having proved Lemma
21
, we proceed to prove Lemma
20
.

Proof of Lemma
20

We begin with
\mathbf{D}^{3}(f\circ g)
.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbf{D}^{3}(f\circ g)&=\mathbf{D}\left((\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g\otimes\mathbf{D}g)\right)+\mathbf{D}\left((\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{2}g)\right)\\
&=(\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g))(I\otimes(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes 2})+(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}((\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes 2})+(\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}f\circ g))(I\otimes\mathbf{D}^{2}g)+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{3}g)\\
&=(\mathbf{D}^{3}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g\otimes I)(I\otimes(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes 2})+(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{2}g\otimes\mathbf{D}g+\mathbf{D}g\otimes\mathbf{D}^{2}g)\\
&+(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g\otimes I)(I\otimes\mathbf{D}^{2}g)+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{3}g)\\
&=(\mathbf{D}^{3}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes 3}+(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{2}g\otimes\mathbf{D}g+\mathbf{D}g\otimes\mathbf{D}^{2}g+\mathbf{D}g\otimes\mathbf{D}^{2}g)+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}^{3}g.\end{aligned}

(143)

All terms in (
143
) of the form
(\mathbf{D}^{n}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g)
can be regarded as orthonormal vectors in
\ell_{2}
:
(\mathbf{D}^{3}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes 3}
is viewed as
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{(1,1,1;3)}
,
(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{2}g\otimes\mathbf{D}g)
is viewed as
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{(2,1;2)}
,
(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g\otimes\mathbf{D}^{2}g)
is viewed as
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{(1,2;2)}
,
(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}^{3}g
is viewed as
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{(3;1)}
. If we consider
\mathbf{D}^{3}(f\circ g)
as a vector in
\ell_{2}
and expand it in this ONB, the coordinates of
\mathbf{D}^{3}(f\circ g)
are (we ignore terms with coordinates equal to
0
):
(1,1,2,1)
. The coordinates of the right-hand side of (
138
) in this ONB when
K=3
are
(1,1,1,1)
. Therefore, when
K=3
and
C_{\ref*{C_concentration}}=2
, (
138
) holds.

Suppose (
138
) is satisfied with some
K\geq 3
. Then

\displaystyle C_{\ref*{C_concentration}}^{-1}\mathbf{D}^{K+1}(f\circ g)\leq\mathbf{D}\left((\mathbf{D}^{K}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes K}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbf{D}\left(\sum_{k=2}^{K-1}(\mathbf{D}^{k}f\circ g)\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\mathbf{D}\left((\mathbf{D}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}^{K}g\right).

By (
136
) and (
139
) we have:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathbf{D}\left((\mathbf{D}^{K}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes K}\right)&=\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}^{K}f\circ g)(I\otimes(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes K})+(\mathbf{D}^{K}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes K}\\
&=(\mathbf{D}^{K+1}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g\otimes I)(I\otimes(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes K})\\
&+(\mathbf{D}^{K}f\circ g)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{K}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{K}=K+1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{K}}g\\
&=(\mathbf{D}^{K+1}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes(K+1)}\\
&+(\mathbf{D}^{K}f\circ g)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{K}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{K}=K+1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes i_{K}}g,\end{aligned}

(144)

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}\left((\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{K}g)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)(I\otimes\mathbf{D}^{K}g)+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}^{K+1}g

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g\otimes I)(I\otimes\mathbf{D}^{K}g)+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}^{K+1}g

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{D}^{2}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g\otimes\mathbf{D}^{K}g)+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}^{K+1}g.

(145)

We are left with the middle term in (
138
). For any
2\leq k\leq K-1
, by (
136
), we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}\left((\mathbf{D}^{k}f\circ g)\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{D}(\mathbf{D}^{k}f\circ g)\left(I\otimes\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right)\right)

(146)

\displaystyle+(\mathbf{D}^{k}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right).

(147)

We now separately compute (
146
) and (
147
). By (
136
) we have:

(
146
)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{D}^{k+1}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g\otimes I)\left(I\otimes\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right)\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{D}^{k+1}f\circ g)\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}g\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{D}^{k+1}f\circ g)\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k+1}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k+1}=K+1\\
i_{1}=1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k+1}}g\right).

(148)

By (
141
) we have:

(
147
)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{D}^{k}f\circ g)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}\left(\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{D}^{k}f\circ g)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K\end{subarray}}\sum_{(l_{1},\cdots,l_{k})\in(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k})+1}\mathbf{D}^{\otimes l_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes l_{k}}g.

(149)

By the definition of
(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k})+1
, see (
140
),
l_{1}+\cdots+l_{k}=i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}+1=K+1
. This implies that every term
\mathbf{D}^{\otimes l_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes l_{k}}g
appeared in (
149
) is contained in one term of the following sum

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}^{\prime}\cdots,i_{k}^{\prime}\geq 1\\
i_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+i_{k}^{\prime}=K+1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}^{\prime}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}^{\prime}}g.

(150)

We therefore only need to compute the repetition counts. Given any
i_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,i_{k}^{\prime}\geq 1
such that
i_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+i_{k}^{\prime}=K+1
, then there are
\left|\left\{j\in[k]:i_{j}^{\prime}\geq 2\right\}\right|
choices of
(i_{1},\cdots,i_{k})
in (
149
) that satisfy
l_{1}=i_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,l_{k}=i_{k}^{\prime}
. For example, with
K=7
and
k=4
, consider a term
(i_{1}^{\prime},i_{2}^{\prime},i_{3}^{\prime},i_{4}^{\prime})=(3,1,3,1)
from (
150
) (this term is in (
150
) because
3+1+3+1=K+1
). Then, only
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})=(2,1,3,1)
and
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})=(3,1,2,1)
from
(
149
)
may be transformed into
(3,1,3,1)
. In other words, given any
i_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,i_{k}^{\prime}\geq 1
such that
i_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+i_{k}^{\prime}=K+1
, if we compute the multiplicity, then
(\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}^{\prime}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}^{\prime}}g)
appears
\left|\{j\in[k]:i_{j}^{\prime}\geq 2\}\right|
times in the summation of (
149
). We have
\left|\{j\in[k]:i_{j}^{\prime}\geq 2\}\right|\leq k<K
uniformly over all
i_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,i_{k}^{\prime}\geq 1
,
i_{1}^{\prime}+\cdots+i_{k}^{\prime}=K+1
and
k<K
.

Therefore, if we view each
(\mathbf{D}^{k}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}^{\otimes l_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{\otimes l_{k}}g)
in (
149
) as a vector
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{(l_{1},\cdots,l_{k};k)}
in
\ell^{2}
, and define a partial order on
\ell^{2}
as
{\boldsymbol{x}}\leq\mathbf{y}
if
x_{i}\leq y_{i}
for any
i\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, then

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:proof_chain_rule_higer_5}\leq K(\mathbf{D}^{k}f\circ g)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K+1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g.

(151)

Combining (
144
), (
145
), (
146
), (
147
), (
148
) and (
151
) indicates

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}^{K+1}(f\circ g)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{K}(\mathbf{D}^{K+1}f\circ g)(\mathbf{D}g)^{\otimes{K+1}}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=2}^{K}(\mathbf{D}^{k}f\circ g)\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K+1\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right)

\displaystyle+(\mathbf{D}f\circ g)\mathbf{D}^{K+1}g.

Lemma
20
follows by induction.

6.5.20
Continue: Proof of Theorem
10

The proof of Theorem
10
relies on the following theorem, taken from
[
AW15
]
.

Theorem 11
.

Let
X=(x_{j})_{j=1}^{d}
be a random vector with independent coordinates, such that
\max(\|x_{j}\|_{\psi_{2}}:\,j\in[d])<\infty
. Then for every polynomial
g:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of degree
D
and every
q\geq 2
,

\left\|g(X)-{\mathbb{E}}g(X)\right\|_{L_{q}}\leq C_{D}\sum_{r=1}^{D}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{r}\sum_{{\cal J}\in P_{r}}q^{\left|J\right|/2}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}g(X)\right\|_{\cal J}.

As a consequence, for any
t>0
,

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|g(X)-{\mathbb{E}}g(X)\right|\geq t\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{1}{C_{D}}\eta_{g}(t)\right),

where
C_{D}
is an absolute constant depending on
D
and

\eta_{g}(t)=\min\left(\min\left(\left(\frac{t}{\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{r}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}g(X)\right\|_{\cal J}}\right)^{2/\left|{\cal J}\right|}:\,{\cal J}\in P_{r}\right):\,1\leq r\leq D\right).

Due to Theorem
11
, we only need to apply Theorem
11
to
g(\cdot)=K(\cdot,\cdot)
, which is a
2L
-degree polynomial. Consequently, we only need to estimate
\eta_{g}(t)
from below. Given that
{\cal J}
is a partition of the set
[r]
, we will employ the straightforward upper bound
\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}g(X)\right\|_{\cal J}\leq\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}g(X)\right\|_{HS}
uniformly for all
{\cal J}
belonging to the set
P_{r}
and for all values of
r\in[2L]
. For any
r\in[2L]
, we have:

\displaystyle\max\left(\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}K(X,X)\right\|_{{\cal J}}:\,{\cal J}\in P_{r}\right)\leq\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}K(X,X)\right\|_{HS}\leq\sum_{\iota=1}^{L}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}\frac{\gamma_{\iota}}{d^{\iota}}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}\right\|_{HS}.

(152)

Given any
r\in[2L]
, it suffices to bound
\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}\right\|_{HS}
from above.

Warm up: the cases for
r=1
and
r=2

We first deal with the cases where
r=1
and
r=2
, that is, we bound
\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}\right\|_{HS}
and
\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{2}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}\right\|_{HS}
from above.

1.

\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}\right\|_{HS}
. For any
j\in[d]
, we have

\displaystyle({\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota})_{j}=2\iota{\mathbb{E}}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-1}x_{j}

\displaystyle=2\iota{\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-1\\
\forall i\in[d],n_{i}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{(\iota-1)!}{n_{1}!\cdot\cdots\cdot n_{d}!}x_{1}^{2n_{1}}\cdot\cdots\cdot x_{j-1}^{2n_{j-1}}x_{j}^{2n_{j}+1}x_{j+1}^{2n_{j+1}}\cdot\cdots\cdot x_{d}^{2n_{d}}\right].

As we have assumed that
d>\iota
, given any
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-1
and
n_{1},\cdots,n_{d}\geq 0
, there exists
i^{*}\in[d]
such that
n_{i^{*}}=0
. If
j=i^{*}
, by the independence of the coordinates and the mean zero assumption,
({\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota})_{j}=0
. Given the
j
-th component of this first-order Fréchet derivative, in the summation above, only the terms corresponding to
n_{j}\geq 1
contributes.
By
[
Juk11
, Proposition 1.5]
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\iota}\left({\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}\right)_{j}={\mathbb{E}}\left[\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-1\\
0\leq n_{1},\cdots,n_{j-1},n_{j+1},\cdots,n_{d}\leq\iota-1\\
n_{j}\geq 1\end{subarray}}^{d}\frac{(\iota-1)!}{n_{1}!\cdot\cdots\cdot n_{d}!}x_{1}^{2n_{1}}\cdot\cdots\cdot x_{d}^{2n_{d}}x_{j}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{b=1}^{\iota-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{j-1}+n_{j+1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-b-1\\
0\leq n_{1},\cdots,n_{j-1},n_{j+1},\cdots,n_{d}\leq\iota-1\end{subarray}}\frac{(\iota-1)!}{n_{1}!\cdot\cdots\cdot n_{d}!}{\mathbb{E}}[x_{1}^{2n_{1}}]\cdot\cdots\cdot{\mathbb{E}}[x_{{j-1}}^{2n_{j-1}}]\cdot{\mathbb{E}}[x_{j}^{2b+1}]\cdot{\mathbb{E}}[x_{{j+1}}^{2n_{j+1}}]\cdot\cdots\cdot{\mathbb{E}}[x_{d}^{2n_{d}}]

\displaystyle\leq(\iota-1)!(2(\iota-2))^{\iota-\frac{3}{2}}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2\iota-1}\sum_{b=1}^{\iota-1}\binom{d-1+\iota-b-2}{\iota-b-1}

\displaystyle<(\iota-1)!\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2\iota-1}(2(\iota-2))^{\iota-\frac{3}{2}}\sum_{b=1}^{\iota-1}\binom{2d}{\iota-b-1}

\displaystyle<(\iota-1)!(2(\iota-2))^{\iota-\frac{3}{2}}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2\iota-1}\left(\frac{2ed}{\iota-1}\right)^{\iota-1}.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{\left\|\gamma_{\iota}{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}\right\|_{HS}^{2}}{d^{2\iota}}

\displaystyle<4\iota^{2}\gamma_{\iota}^{2}((\iota-1)!)^{2}(2(\iota-2))^{2\iota-3}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{4\iota-2}d^{-2\iota}\left(\frac{2ed}{\iota-1}\right)^{2(\iota-1)}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\iota^{2}\gamma_{\iota}^{2}((\iota-1)!)^{2}(2(\iota-2))^{2\iota-3}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{4\iota-2}(2e)^{2(\iota-1)}}{(\iota-1)^{2(\iota-1)}d^{2}}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{4\iota^{2}\gamma_{\iota}^{2}(2(\iota-2))^{2\iota-3}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{4\iota-2}}{d^{2}}.

(153)

2.

\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{2}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}\right\|_{HS}
. By (
137
), we know that
{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{2}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}=\iota(\iota-1)(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-2}((X^{\top})\otimes(X^{\top}))+2\iota(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-1}I_{d}
. Take the
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{HS}
and by triangular inequality we obtain that

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{2}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}\right\|_{HS}

\displaystyle\leq\iota(\iota-1)\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-2}((X^{\top})\otimes(X^{\top}))\right]\right\|_{HS}+2\iota\sqrt{d}{\mathbb{E}}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-1}

\displaystyle=\iota(\iota-1)\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-2}(X\otimes X)\right]\right\|_{HS}+2\iota\sqrt{d}{\mathbb{E}}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-1}.

We have:
\sqrt{d}{\mathbb{E}}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-1}\lesssim(2(\iota-1))^{\iota-1}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2(\iota-1)}d^{\iota-1/2}
.
For any
j_{1},j_{2}\in[d]
, we discuss the following two cases separately:

(a)

j_{1}\neq j_{2}
. We have:

\displaystyle\left({\mathbb{E}}\left[(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-2}(X\otimes X)\right]\right)_{j_{1},j_{2}}={\mathbb{E}}\left[x_{j_{1}}x_{j_{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-2\\
\forall i\in[d],n_{i}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{(\iota-2)!}{n_{1}!\cdot\cdots\cdot n_{d}!}x_{1}^{2n_{1}}\cdot\cdots\cdot x_{d}^{2n_{d}}\right].

(154)

Repeat the argument in case
1.
, we obtain that

(
154
)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-2\\
\forall i\in[d],n_{i}\geq 0\\
n_{j_{1}}n_{j_{2}}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{(\iota-2)!}{n_{1}!\cdot\cdots\cdot n_{d}!}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in[d]\\
\alpha\neq j_{1},j_{2}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}[x_{\alpha}^{2n_{\alpha}}]{\mathbb{E}}[x_{j_{1}}^{2n_{j_{1}}+1}]{\mathbb{E}}[x_{j_{2}}^{2n_{j_{2}}+1}]

\displaystyle\lesssim(\iota-2)!(2\iota-1)^{\iota-1}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2\iota-2}\left(\frac{2ed}{\iota-2}\right)^{\iota-2}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{1},j_{2}\in[d]\\
j_{1}\neq j_{2}\end{subarray}}\left({\mathbb{E}}\left[(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-2}(X\otimes X)\right]\right)_{j_{1},j_{2}}^{2}\lesssim(2\iota-1)^{2(\iota-1)}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{4\iota-4}d^{2\iota-2}.

(b)

j_{1}=j_{2}
. In this scenario, we cannot employ the method of
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
to obtain sparsity in this second-order gradient tensor. Fortunately, in this case, the Hilbert-Schmidt norm of this second-order tensor only possesses one “degree of freedom”:

\displaystyle\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{d}\left({\mathbb{E}}\left[(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-2}(X\otimes X)\right]\right)_{j_{1},j_{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{d}\left((2\iota-1)^{\iota-1}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2\iota-2}d^{\iota-2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=(2\iota-1)^{2(\iota-1)}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{4\iota-4}d^{2\iota-3}.

In summary,

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{\iota}^{2}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{2}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}\right\|_{HS}^{2}}{d^{2\iota}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\gamma_{\iota}^{2}(2\iota-1)^{2(\iota-1)}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{4\iota-4}d^{-2}+\gamma_{\iota}^{2}(2\iota-1)^{2(\iota-1)}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{4\iota-4}d^{-3}

\displaystyle+\gamma_{\iota}^{2}(2(\iota-1))^{2(\iota-1)}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{4(\iota-1)}d^{-1}\sim\frac{\gamma_{\iota}^{2}}{d}(2\iota-1)^{2(\iota-1)}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{4\iota-4}.

(155)

Before addressing the case of
r\geq 3
, let us first review the proof for
r=2
, as the main proof strategy for the case of
r\geq 3
will be utilized from the case of
r=2
. The computation of
\mathbb{E}\mathbf{D}^{2}(\|X\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}
can be broadly divided into two parts: corresponding to the first and last terms in equation (
138
). For the computation of the first term, we divide it into two cases: in the off-diagonal case, we exploit the independence of coordinates of
X
and its mean-zero property to induce sparsity; in the diagonal case, since there are fewer entries (referred to as ”degrees of freedom”) in the diagonal of the gradient tensor, we compute it directly. Next, in the case of
r\geq 3
, we will continue using this approach. The difference lies in the fact that for
r\geq 3
, we also need to consider the intermediate term in equation (
138
).

The case for
3\leq r\leq 2\iota

We temporarily substitute
r
with
K
for ease of use with Lemma
20
.

For any
\iota\geq 1
, let
g:{\boldsymbol{z}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto\left\|{\boldsymbol{z}}\right\|_{2}^{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
f:t\in{\mathbb{R}}\mapsto t^{\iota}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then
f\circ g:{\boldsymbol{z}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\mapsto\left\|{\boldsymbol{z}}\right\|_{2}^{2\iota}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{D}g({\boldsymbol{z}})=2{\boldsymbol{z}}^{\top}
,
\mathbf{D}^{2}g({\boldsymbol{z}})=2I_{d}
and
\mathbf{D}^{3}g({\boldsymbol{z}})={\boldsymbol{0}}
, the zero tensor of degree
3
, dimension
d
. We also have that for any
k\in[\iota]
,
(\mathbf{D}^{k}f\circ g)({\boldsymbol{z}})=((\iota!)/(\iota-k)!)\left\|{\boldsymbol{z}}\right\|_{2}^{2\iota-2k}
and for
k>\iota
,
(\mathbf{D}^{k}f\circ g)({\boldsymbol{z}})={\boldsymbol{0}}
. Let
{\boldsymbol{z}}=X
introduced in Theorem
10
.

By Lemma
20
, take expectation and Hilbert-Schmidt norm, and by triangular inequality, we obtain that there exists an absolute constant
{C_{75}}
depending only on
K
such that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{K}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}\right\|_{HS}&\leq C_{\ref*{C_Concentration_2}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K\leq\iota\}}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-K}(X^{\top})^{\otimes K}\right]\right\|_{HS}+C_{\ref*{C_Concentration_2}}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-1}\mathbf{D}^{K}\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\right\|_{HS}\\
&+C_{\ref*{C_Concentration_2}}\left\|\sum_{k=2}^{K-1\wedge\iota}{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-k}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\geq 1\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right)(X)\right]\right\|_{HS}.\end{aligned}

By triangular inequality again, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{K}\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2\iota}\right\|_{HS}\lesssim_{K}\mathbbm{1}_{\{K\leq\iota\}}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2\iota-2K}(X^{\top})^{\otimes K}\right\|_{HS}\\
&+\sum_{k=2}^{K-1\wedge\iota}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2\iota-2k}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\in\{1,2\}\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right)\right\|_{HS}.\end{aligned}

(156)

We first deal with the last term in (
156
).

For any
2\leq k\leq K-1\wedge\iota

Given
i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\in\{1,2\}
such that
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K
, we can separate
[K]
into
k
groups, that is,
[K]=\sqcup_{a=1}^{k}J_{a}
, where
J_{a}
is of the form
J_{a}=\{j,j+1\}
or
J_{a}=\{j\}
for
j\in[K]
according to
i_{a}
for
a\in[k]
, that is, we have
i_{a}=\left|J_{a}\right|
equals either
1
or
2
. Denote
{\cal J}=\cup_{a=1}^{k}\{J_{a}:\,\left|J_{a}\right|=2\}
, and
{\cal J}^{c}=\cup_{a=1}^{k}\{J_{a}:\,\left|J_{a}\right|=1\}
. As
{\cal J}\sqcup{\cal J}^{c}=[K]
, there exists a surjective
\pi:l\in[K]\mapsto\pi(l)\in[k]
such that
l\in J_{\pi(l)}
. Given any
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{K}
, denote
{\cal I}=\cup_{j=1}^{K-1}\{\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j+1}:\,\pi(j)=\pi(j+1)\}
, and
{\cal I}^{*}=\cup_{j=1}^{K}\{\alpha_{j}:\,\left|J_{\pi(j)}\right|=1\}
. We have
{\cal I}\sqcup{\cal I}^{*}=\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{K}\}
. We have
\left|{\cal I}^{*}\right|=\sum_{a=1}^{k}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\left|J_{a}\right|=1\}}=\left|{\cal J}^{c}\right|
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{K}\in[d]
. For
a^{*}\in[k]
such that
i_{a^{*}}=2
, we have
\mathbf{D}^{i_{a^{*}}}g(X)=\mathbf{D}^{2}g(X)=2I_{d}
, and for
a^{*}\in[k]
such that
i_{a^{*}}=1
, we have
\mathbf{D}^{i_{a^{*}}}g(X)=2X^{\top}
. We therefore have: for any
j\in[K]
, if
\left|J_{\pi(j)}\right|=2
, then there exists an
j^{\prime}=j\pm 1
such that
\pi(j^{\prime})=\pi(j)
and
(\mathbf{D}^{\left|J_{\pi(j)}\right|}g(X))_{\alpha_{j},\alpha_{j^{\prime}}}=\mathbbm{1}_{\{\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{j^{\prime}}\}}
; if
\left|J_{\pi(j)}\right|=1
, then
((\mathbf{D}^{\left|J_{\pi(j)}\right|})g(X)^{\top})_{\alpha_{j}}=x_{\alpha_{j}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right)_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{K}}^{\top}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[K]\\
\left|J_{\pi(j)}\right|=1\end{subarray}}x_{\alpha_{j}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[K]\\
\left|J_{\pi(j)}\right|=2\end{subarray}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{j^{\prime}}\}}.

Depending on the relation between
2k
and
K
, there are three regimes in general:

1.

When
2k<K
, it is impossible to have
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K
while
i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\in\{1,2\}
.

2.

When
2k=K
,
\left|{\cal J}^{c}\right|=0
, and
\left|{\cal J}\right|=K/2
.

In this case,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2\iota-2k}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right]=2^{k}{\mathbb{E}}[\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2\iota-2k}](I_{d})^{\otimes k}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left[\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2\iota-2k}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right]\right\|_{HS}^{2}

\displaystyle=2^{2k}\left({\mathbb{E}}\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2\iota-2k}\right)^{2}d^{k}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{2k}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{4(\iota-k)}(2(\iota-k))^{\iota-k}d^{2\iota-k}.

3.

When
2k>K
,
\left|{\cal J}^{c}\right|=2k-K\geq 1
, and
\left|{\cal J}\right|=K-k
. Recall that

\displaystyle(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-k}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-k\\
\forall i\in[d],n_{i}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{(\iota-k)!}{n_{1}!\cdot\cdots\cdot n_{d}!}x_{1}^{2n_{1}}\cdot\cdots\cdot x_{d}^{2n_{d}}.

As we have assumed that
d>\iota
, there exists
i^{*}\in[d]
such that
n_{i^{*}}=0
. Given
(\alpha_{j})_{j=1}^{K}\in[d]^{K}
, if there exists
j^{*}\in[K]
such that
\alpha_{j^{*}}=i^{*}
, and
\left|J_{\pi(j^{*})}\right|=1
, then
({\mathbb{E}}[(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-k}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g])_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{K}}=0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\left({\mathbb{E}}[(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-k}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g]\right)_{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{K}}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[K]\\
\left|I_{\pi(j)}\right|=1\end{subarray}}x_{\alpha_{j}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-k\\
\forall i\in[d],n_{i}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{(\iota-k)!}{n_{1}!\cdot\cdots\cdot n_{d}!}x_{1}^{2n_{1}}\cdot\cdots\cdot x_{d}^{2n_{d}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in[K]\\
\left|I_{\pi(j)}\right|=2\end{subarray}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\alpha_{j}=\alpha_{j^{\prime}}\}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-k\\
\forall i\in[d],n_{i}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{(\iota-k)!}{n_{1}!\cdot\cdots\cdot n_{d}!}\prod_{i^{*}\in{\cal I}^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x_{i^{*}}^{2n_{i^{*}}+1}\right]\prod_{i\notin{\cal I}^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x_{i}^{2n_{i}}\right]\prod_{(i_{1},i_{1}+1)\in{\cal I}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{i_{1}=i_{1}+1\}}.

(157)

To have (
157
) not equals to
0
, we necessarily need for any
i^{*}\in{\cal I}^{*}
,
n_{i^{*}}\geq 1
. Therefore,

(
157
)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-k\\
\forall i\in[d],n_{i}\geq 0\\
\forall i^{*}\in{\cal I}^{*},n_{i^{*}}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{(\iota-k)!}{n_{1}!\cdot\cdots\cdot n_{d}!}\prod_{i^{*}\in{\cal I}^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x_{i^{*}}^{2n_{i^{*}}+1}\right]\prod_{i\notin{\cal I}^{*}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[x_{i}^{2n_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim(2(\iota-k)+1)^{\iota+\frac{k}{2}}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2(\iota-k)+\left|{\cal I}^{*}\right|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-k\\
\forall i\in[d],n_{i}\geq 0\\
\forall i^{*}\in{\cal I}^{*},n_{i^{*}}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{(\iota-k)!}{n_{1}!\cdot\cdots\cdot n_{d}!}

\displaystyle\leq(2(\iota-k)+1)^{\iota+\frac{k}{2}}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2\iota-k}(\iota-k)!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-k\\
\forall i\in[d],n_{i}\geq 0\\
\forall i^{*}\in{\cal I}^{*},n_{i^{*}}\geq 1\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle=(2(\iota-k)+1)^{\iota+\frac{k}{2}}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2\iota-k}(\iota-k)!\binom{d+(\iota-k)-1-\left|{\cal I}^{*}\right|}{\iota-k-\left|{\cal I}^{*}\right|}

\displaystyle\leq(2(\iota-k)+1)^{\iota+\frac{k}{2}}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2\iota-k}(\iota-k)!\left(\frac{2ed}{\iota-k-1}\right)^{\iota-k-1}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\gamma_{\iota}^{2}d^{-2\iota}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-k}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\in\{1,2\}\\
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{k}=K\end{subarray}}\mathbf{D}^{i_{1}}g\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbf{D}^{i_{k}}g\right)\right\|_{HS}^{2}

\displaystyle<\gamma_{\iota}^{2}(2(\iota-k)+1)^{2\iota+k}d^{-2\iota}d^{k}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2(2\iota-k)}((\iota-k)!)^{2}\left(\frac{2e}{\iota-k-1}\right)^{2(\iota-k-1)}d^{2(\iota-k-1)}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{\iota}^{2}(2(\iota-k)+1)^{2\iota+k}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2(2\iota-k)}((\iota-k)!)^{2}\left(\frac{2e}{\iota-k-1}\right)^{2(\iota-k-1)}d^{-k-2}

\displaystyle\sim\gamma_{\iota}^{2}\left(2(\iota-k)+1\right)^{2\iota+k}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2(2\iota-k)}d^{-k-2}.

(158)

We then deal with the first term in (
156
).

The case for
\iota=K
:
\left\|{\mathbb{E}}(X^{\top})^{\otimes K}\right\|_{HS}

Given any
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{K})\in[d]^{K}
, we construct a graph
G({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
with vertex set
[K]
. Let
\{i\}
is connected with
\{j\}
, if
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}
. To have
({\mathbb{E}}(X^{\top})^{\otimes K})_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}\neq 0
, we necessarily need
G({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
has no isolated vertex. Let
E({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
as the set of edges of
G({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
, then
\left|E({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|\leq\lceil K/2\rceil
. For each
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
such that
\left|E({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|\leq\lceil K/2\rceil
, we can bound
({\mathbb{E}}(X^{\top})^{\otimes K})_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}
from above as
C_{\ref*{C_subgaussian}}K^{K/2}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{K}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}(X^{\top})^{\otimes K}\right\|_{HS}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|{\mathbb{E}}X^{\otimes K}\right\|_{HS}^{2}\lesssim K^{K}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2K}\sum_{i=0}^{\lceil K/2\rceil}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in[d]^{K}\\
\left|E({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|=i\end{subarray}}d^{i}\lesssim K^{K}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2K}\sum_{i=0}^{\lceil K/2\rceil}d^{i}\binom{K}{2}^{i}

\displaystyle\lesssim K^{K}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2K}\sum_{i=0}^{\lceil K/2\rceil}d^{i}K^{2i}\lesssim\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2K}d^{\lceil K/2\rceil}K^{2K}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{K}^{2}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}(X^{\top})^{\otimes K}\right\|_{HS}^{2}}{d^{K}}\lesssim\gamma_{\iota}^{2}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2K}\iota^{2\iota}d^{-\iota/2+1/2}.

(159)

The case for
K<\iota
:
\mathbbm{1}_{\{K<\iota\}}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2\iota-2K}(X^{\top})^{\otimes K}\right\|_{HS}

Given
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}:=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{K})\in[d]^{K}
, we construct a undirected graph
G({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
with vertex
[K]
, and
\{i\}
is connected with
\{j\}
for
i,j\in[K]
, if
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}
. We define a equivalence relation on
[K]
, denoted by
i\sim j
, where
i,j\in[K]
, if there is a path connecting
\{i\}
and
\{j\}
. We partition
[K]
into equivalence classes
{\cal C}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\cup_{l}{\cal C}_{l}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
, that is, for every
i,j\in{\cal C}_{l}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
(possibly
i=j
), we have
i\sim j
, and
{\cal C}_{l}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
is largest, in the sense that if
i\sim j
,
i\in{\cal C}_{l}
, then
j\in{\cal C}_{l}
; for every
i\in{\cal C}_{l}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
,
j\in{\cal C}_{m}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
,
l\neq m
, we have
i\not\sim j
. Define the map
q:j\in[K]\mapsto q(j)\in[\left|{\cal C}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|]
such that
j\in{\cal C}_{q(j)}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
, that is, the map
q
maps each vertex into its unique equivalent class.
Let
{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\{\alpha_{j}:\,j\in[K],\left|{\cal C}_{q(j)}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|=1\}
, and
{\cal O}^{**}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\{\alpha_{j}:\,j\in[K],\left|{\cal C}_{q(j)}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|>1\}
. We have the following relation:
\left|{\cal C}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|\leq\lfloor\frac{K-\left|{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|}{2}\rfloor
. This is because
{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
and
\{{\cal C}_{l}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}):l\in[\left|{\cal C}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|],\left|{\cal C}_{l}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|=1\}
are one-to-one corresponded; and for
{\cal C}_{l}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
with
\left|{\cal C}_{l}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|>1
, the smallest size of
{\cal C}_{l}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
is
2
.

Notice that
{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\cup{\cal O}^{**}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
might not be
[d]
. This is illustrated in the following example: let
K=8
,
d=100
,
\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{8}\}=\{1,1,2,2,3,3,3,4\}
, then
\{1\},\{2\}
are connected, so as
\{3\},\{4\}
and
\{5\},\{6\},\{7\}
. Moreover,
1\sim 2
,
3\sim 4
,
5\sim 6\sim 7
, thus
{\cal C}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})={\cal C}_{1}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\cup{\cal C}_{2}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\cup{\cal C}_{3}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\cup{\cal C}_{4}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
where
{\cal C}_{1}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\{1,2\}
,
{\cal C}_{2}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\{3,4\}
,
{\cal C}_{3}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\{5,6,7\}
and
{\cal C}_{4}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\{8\}
. For any
j\in[8]
, only for
j=8
we have
\left|{\cal C}_{q(8)}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|=\left|{\cal C}_{4}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|=1
, hence
{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\{\alpha_{8}\}=\{4\}
. Similarly,
{\cal O}^{**}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=\{1,2,3\}
. Thus
{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\cup{\cal O}^{**}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})=[4]
, however
[d]=[100]
.

\displaystyle\left({\mathbb{E}}\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2\iota-2K}X^{\otimes K}\right)_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-K\\
\forall i\in[d],n_{i}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{(\iota-K)!}{n_{1}!\cdot\cdots\cdot n_{d}!}x_{1}^{2n_{1}}\cdot\cdots\cdot x_{d}^{2n_{d}}\right)\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{j}\in{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\end{subarray}}x_{\alpha_{j}}\right)\left(\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{j}\in{\cal O}^{**}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\end{subarray}}x_{\alpha_{j}}\right)\right].

(160)

We now seek
n_{1},\cdots,n_{d}
such that (
160
) is non-zero. A necessary condition is as follows: for every
i\in{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
, we must have
n_{i}\geq 1
. Therefore,

(
160
)

\displaystyle<(2\iota-K)^{\iota}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}+\cdots+n_{d}=\iota-K\\
\forall i\in[d],n_{i}\geq 0\\
\forall i\in{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}}),n_{i}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\frac{(\iota-K)!}{n_{1}!\cdot\cdots\cdot n_{d}!}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2\iota-K}

\displaystyle<(2\iota-K)^{\iota}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2\iota-K}(\iota-K)!\binom{d+(\iota-K)-1-\left|{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|}{\iota-K-\left|{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|},

where we emphasize that when
\iota-K-\left|{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|=0
, the binomial coefficient defined as
1
, and when
\iota-K-\left|{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|<0
, the binomial coefficient is
0
. Before we taking square and taking sum over all
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in[d]^{k}
, we need to count the “degree of freedom”. Recall the example
K=8
,
d=100
and
\{\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{8}\}=\{1,1,2,2,3,3,3,4\}
. In this example, if we take sum over all
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}
with the edge of associated
G({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
are the same, it is equivalent to taking sum over
x_{1}=x_{2}
,
x_{3}=x_{4}
,
x_{5}=x_{6}=x_{7}
, and none of
x_{1},x_{3},x_{5},x_{8}
are equal. Therefore, the sum is over
x_{1},x_{3},x_{5},x_{8}\in[d]
, thus have
d^{4}=d^{\left|{\cal C}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|}
terms. Moreover, given
\left|{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|=\chi
where
\chi
is fixed, there are at most
(K-\chi)^{K-\chi}
choices of
G({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})
.

Now, we take square and sum over all
{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{K})\in[d]^{k}
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-K}(X^{\top})^{\otimes K}\right\|_{HS}^{2}=\left\|{\mathbb{E}}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-K}X^{\otimes K}\right\|_{HS}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\chi=1}^{K}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\in[d]^{\otimes K}\\
\left|{\cal O}^{*}({\boldsymbol{\alpha}})\right|=\chi\end{subarray}}\left({\mathbb{E}}\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2\iota-2K}X^{\otimes K}\right)_{{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{\chi=1}^{\iota-K}(K-\chi)^{K-\chi}d^{\lfloor\frac{K-\chi}{2}\rfloor}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2(2\iota-K)}(2\iota-K)^{2\iota}((\iota-K)!)^{2}\left(\frac{2ed}{\iota-K-\chi}\right)^{2(\iota-K-\chi)}

\displaystyle\lesssim(K-1)^{K-1}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2(2\iota-K)}(2\iota-K)^{2\iota}((\iota-K)!)^{2}\left(\frac{2e}{\iota-K}\right)^{2(\iota-K)}\sum_{\chi=1}^{\iota-K}d^{2\iota-\frac{3}{2}K-\frac{5}{2}\chi}

\displaystyle\lesssim(K-1)^{K-1}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2(2\iota-K)}(2\iota-K)^{2\iota}((\iota-K)!)^{2}\left(\frac{2e}{\iota-K}\right)^{2(\iota-K)}d^{2\iota-\frac{3}{2}K-\frac{5}{2}}.

As a result,

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma_{\iota}^{2}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota-K}(X^{\top})^{\otimes K}\right\|_{HS}^{2}}{d^{2\iota}}\lesssim\gamma_{\iota}^{2}(K-1)^{K-1}\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2(2\iota-K)}(2\iota-K)^{2\iota}d^{2\iota-\frac{3}{2}K-\frac{5}{2}}.

(161)

Combining (
153
), (
155
), (
158
), (
159
) and (
161
), we know that: for any
r\in[2L]
, we have,

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}K(X,X)\right\|_{HS}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\iota=1}^{L}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}\frac{\gamma_{\iota}}{d^{\iota}}(\left\|X\right\|_{2}^{2})^{\iota}\right\|_{HS}\lesssim\frac{2^{2L}L^{3L}}{\sqrt{d}}(\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2L-1}\vee 1)\sum_{\iota=1}^{L}\gamma_{\iota}.

(162)

Plug it into (
152
), we obtain that for any
r\in[2L]
,

\displaystyle\left(\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{r}\max\left(\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}K(X,X)\right\|_{\cal J}:{\cal J}\in P_{r}\right)\right)^{2/r}\lesssim\left(\frac{2^{2L}L^{3L}}{\sqrt{d}}(\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2L+r-1}\vee\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{r})\sum_{\iota=1}^{L}\gamma_{\iota}\right)^{2/r}.

By Theorem
11
, we have, for any
t>0
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|K(X,X)-{\mathbb{E}}K(X,X)\right|\geq t\right)\leq\exp\left(-C_{\ref*{C_non_linear_HS}}\frac{t^{\frac{1}{L}}d^{\frac{1}{2L}}}{4L^{3}\left(\sum_{\iota=1}^{L}\gamma_{\iota}\right)^{\frac{1}{L}}}\right).

6.5.21
Verifying (
6
) from Assumption
3
and Assumption
2
under sub-Gaussian assumption.

We apply Theorem
11
to
g(X)=\left\|\Gamma_{1:k}^{-1/2}\phi_{1:k}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}
, and
g(X)=\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}
.

Checking diagonal concentration in Assumption
2
,
g(X)=\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}

By the definition of
\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}
and
\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)
, we know that
\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)=\sum_{j>k}\sqrt{\sigma_{j}}\varphi_{j}(X)\varphi_{j}
. For each
j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{+}
, let
f_{j}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\sigma_{j}}}\varphi_{j}
be the corresponding ONB in
L_{2}(\mu)
, then
\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}=\sum_{j>k}\sigma_{j}^{2}f_{j}^{2}(X)
. Compared to
\left\|\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}=\sum_{j>k}\sigma_{j}f_{j}^{2}(X)
, we observe that the eigenvalues are replaced by their square.

For any
r\geq 0
,
i_{1},\cdots,i_{r}\in[d]
and
X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{D}^{r}\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\right)_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{r}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial^{r}}{\partial x_{1}\cdots\partial x_{r}}\right)\sum_{j>k}\sigma_{j}^{2}f_{j}^{2}(X)

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{k+1}\left(\frac{\partial^{r}}{\partial x_{1}\cdots\partial x_{r}}\right)\sum_{j>k}\sigma_{j}f_{j}^{2}(X)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k+1}\left(\mathbf{D}^{r}\left\|\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\right)_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{r}}.

By the positive homogeneity of expectation and Hilbert-Schmidt norm, we know that for any
r
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}\left\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\right\|_{HS}\leq\sigma_{k+1}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}\left\|\phi_{k+1:\infty}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\right\|_{HS},

where upper bounds for the right-hand-side may be found in (
162
). We apply Theorem
11
with
t=1000\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})
for
k=\sum_{i=0}^{\iota}d^{\iota}
. Notice that at this time,
\Tr(\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{2})\sim d^{-(\iota+1)}
(see the discussion after Lemma
19
), and
\sigma_{k+1}\sim d^{-(\iota+1)}
. By Theorem
11
together with (
162
), we can take
\delta_{\ref*{delta_DMU_L2}}=999
and
{\gamma_{\ref*{gamma_DMU_L2}}}\sim\gamma
.

Checking diagonal term concentration in Assumption
3
,
g(X)=\left\|\Gamma_{1:k}^{-1/2}\phi_{1:k}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}

The idea is again to use Theorem
11
, by noticing that for any
r\geq 0
,
i_{1},\cdots,i_{r}\in[d]
and
X\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{D}^{r}\left\|\Gamma_{1:k}^{-1/2}\phi_{1:k}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\right)_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{r}}
