\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\partial^{r}}{\partial x_{1}\cdots\partial x_{r}}\right)\sum_{j\leq k}f_{j}^{2}(X)

\displaystyle\leq\sigma_{k}^{-1}\left(\frac{\partial^{r}}{\partial x_{1}\cdots\partial x_{r}}\right)\sum_{j\leq k}\sigma_{j}f_{j}^{2}(X)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{k}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{D}^{r}\left\|\phi_{1:k}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\right)_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{r}}.

As a result, for any
r
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}\left\|\Gamma_{1:k}^{-1/2}\phi_{1:k}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\right\|_{HS}\leq\sigma_{k}^{-1}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}\mathbf{D}^{r}\left\|\phi_{1:k}(X)\right\|_{\cal H}^{2}\right\|_{HS},

where the upper bound for the right-hand-side may be found in (
162
). We apply Theorem
11
with
t=1000k
,
k=\sum_{i=0}^{\iota}d^{i}
. Notice that
\sigma_{k}^{-1}\sim d^{\iota}\sim k
, we can take
\delta_{\ref*{delta_RIP}}=999
and
{\gamma_{\ref*{gamma_RIP}}}\sim\gamma
.

6.5.22
Proof of Proposition
31

In this subsection, we verify that (
7
), (
12
) and (
13
) hold under Assumption
10
with
\epsilon=6
. The following Lemma is taken from
[
LRZ20
, Lemma 10]
:

Lemma 22
.

Suppose [1] of Assumption
10
holds. There exists
\kappa_{L}\geq 1
such that for any
\beta\in\ell_{2}^{\binom{d+L}{L}}
and
f_{\beta}(\cdot):=\sum_{\left|{\cal I}\right|\leq L}\beta_{\cal I}Q_{\cal I}(\cdot)
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f_{\beta}\right\|_{L_{4}}\leq\kappa_{L}\left\|f_{\beta}\right\|_{L_{2}}=\kappa_{L}\sqrt{\sum_{\left|{\cal I}\right|\leq L}\beta_{\cal I}^{2}}.

The original assumption of
[
LRZ20
]
in Lemma
22
is: there exist absolute constants
\nu>1,{C_{76}}>0
such that
{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|x_{1}\right|\geq t\right)\leq C_{\ref*{C_LRZ}}(1+t)^{-\nu}
for all
t\geq 0
. This assumption is implied by Assumption
10
. By Assumption
10
,
x_{1}
has a finite
\psi_{2}
norm
\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}
such that
{\mathbb{P}}\left(\left|x_{1}\right|\geq t\right)\leq\exp\left(-{C_{77}}t^{2}/\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}\right)
for some absolute constant
C_{\ref*{C_subgaussian}}>0
. In fact, one can choose
\nu=\sqrt{2C_{\ref*{C_subgaussian}}\log{C_{\ref*{C_LRZ}}}/\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}}>1
and
C_{\ref*{C_LRZ}}>\exp\left(\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}/(2C_{\ref*{C_subgaussian}})\right)>1
. This is because
\exp\left(-C_{\ref*{C_subgaussian}}t^{2}/\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2}\right)\leq C_{\ref*{C_LRZ}}(1+t)^{-\nu}
for all
t\geq 0
is equivalent to
(-C_{\ref*{C_subgaussian}}/\left\|x_{1}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2})t^{2}+\nu\log(1+t)-\log{C_{\ref*{C_LRZ}}}\leq 0
for all
t\geq 0
, and the above choice of
C_{\ref*{C_LRZ}}
and
\nu
is a sufficient condition for it.

Lemma
22
should not be confused with Bourgain’s
\Lambda_{p}
problem
[
Bou89
]
, as the ONB here is polynomial and not uniformly bounded.

Proof.
[Proposition
31
]
To prove Proposition
31
, we simply note that
(Q_{\cal I})_{\cal I}
is an ONB of
L_{2}(\mu)
by Lemma
19
. Notice that for any
\gamma\in\ell_{2}^{\binom{d+L}{L}}
,
(f_{\gamma})^{2}\in L_{2}(\mu)
due to
\mu
being sub-Gaussian and
(Q_{\cal I})
being finite-degree polynomial. There therefore exists
\zeta\in\ell_{2}^{\binom{d+2L}{2L}}
such that

\displaystyle\left(f_{\gamma}(\cdot)\right)^{2}=f_{\zeta}(\cdot)=\sum_{\left|{\cal I}\right|\leq 2L}\zeta_{\cal I}Q_{\cal I}(\cdot).

Applying Lemma
22
with
L
replaced by
2L
and
\beta
replaced by
\zeta
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|f_{\gamma}\right\|_{L_{8}}^{2}=\left\|f_{\zeta}\right\|_{L_{4}}\leq\kappa_{2L}\sqrt{\sum_{\left|{\cal I}\right|\leq 2L}\zeta_{\cal I}^{2}}.

On the other hand, we know that
\sum_{\left|{\cal I}\right|\leq 2L}\zeta_{\cal I}^{2}=\left\|f_{\gamma}\right\|_{L_{4}}^{2}
. This is because

\displaystyle f_{\gamma}^{4}(X)=\left(f_{\gamma}^{2}(X)\right)^{2}=\left(f_{\zeta}(X)\right)^{2}=\sum_{\left|{\cal I}\right|,\left|{\cal J}\right|\leq 2L}\zeta_{\cal I}\zeta_{\cal J}Q_{\cal I}(X)Q_{\cal J}(X),

which implies that
\left\|f_{\gamma}\right\|_{L_{4}}^{4}=\sum_{\left|{\cal I}\right|\leq 2L}\zeta_{\cal I}^{2}
. Applying Lemma
22
again with
\beta=\gamma
, we have
\sum_{\left|{\cal I}\right|\leq 2L}\zeta_{\cal I}^{2}=\left\|f_{\gamma}\right\|_{L_{4}}^{4}\leq\kappa_{L}^{4}\left\|f_{\gamma}\right\|_{L_{2}}^{4}
. As a result,

\displaystyle\left\|f_{\gamma}\right\|_{L_{8}}\leq\sqrt{\kappa_{2L}}\kappa_{L}\left\|f_{\gamma}\right\|_{L_{2}}.

Thus, Proposition
30
as a corollary of Theorem
3
has been proven.

6.5.23
Multiple descent in asymptotic regime

By (
127
), and when
f^{*}=f^{**}
, we only need to prove that
r_{0,k}^{*}=o_{d}(1)(\sigma_{\xi}+\|f^{*}\|_{\cal H})
and the probability deviation tends to
1
when
N,d\to\infty
with
\omega_{d}(d^{\iota})\leq N\leq o_{d}(d^{\iota+1})
. For the probability deviation, by (
24
), (
132
) and the fact that
{\gamma_{\ref*{gamma_DMU_L2}}}\sim\gamma
and
{\gamma_{\ref*{gamma_RIP}}}\sim\gamma
, we have:
\eqref{eq:prob_devi_multiple_descent_sub_Gaussian}\to 1
. Moreover, by the definition of
r_{0,k}^{*}
from (
31
),

1.

\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{d^{\iota}/N}=o_{d}(1)\sigma_{\xi}
;

2.

\sigma_{\xi}\sqrt{N/d^{\iota+1}}=o_{d}(1)\sigma_{\xi}
;

3.

\|\Gamma_{>\iota}^{1/2}f_{>\iota}^{*}\|_{\cal H}\leq\|\Gamma_{k+1:\infty}^{1/2}\|_{\text{op}}\|f^{*}\|_{\cal H}=o_{d}(1)\|f^{*}\|_{\cal H}
, see the discussion after Lemma
19
;

4.

\|\Gamma_{\leq\iota}^{-1/2}f_{\leq\iota}^{*}\|N^{-1}\leq\|f^{*}\|_{\cal H}\sigma_{k}(\Gamma)^{-1/2}N^{-1}\lesssim\|f^{*}\|_{\cal H}d^{\iota}/N=o_{d}(1)\|f^{*}\|_{\cal H}
.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left|\left\|\hat{f}_{0}-f^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}-\left\|f_{>\iota}^{*}\right\|_{L_{2}(\mu)}\right|=o_{d,{\mathbb{P}}}(1)\left(\sigma_{\xi}+\left\|f^{*}\right\|_{\cal H}\right).
